i

HOWARD D. KIRKPATRICK
Grand Master
October 15, 2003 – October 13, 2004

ii

HOWARD D. KIRKPATRICK
Grand Master – 2003-2004
Howard lives in Palo Cedro, California, which is just outside of
Redding, with his wife of 46 years, Jackie. They have four daughters and nine
grandchildren. He was born in Colorado, grew up in Oklahoma and came to
Chico, Ca. after being discharged from the US Army as a Sergeant in the
artillery.
Howard received a Bachelor of Science Degree from California State
University, Chico in Business Administration with the help from the old Korean
GI Bill and a Chartered Life Underwriter Degree (CLU) from Bryn Mawr
College in Pennsylvania. He owns an independent insurance agency in Redding,
which is being operated by two of their daughters since his retirement in 1997.
His civic involvement includes having served as Chairman of the local
Public Utilities District, Mayor and council member for the City of Redding,
Chairman of the Redding Medical Center Board of Governors and several other
boards and commissions. He has also served as President of the California State
University, Chico Alumni Association and a 10-year term on a committee for the
US Department of Defense. He is a 33-year member of Redding East Rotary
and served as President and Secretary.
In November 1967, he was raised in Chico Lodge No. 111 and later
affiliated with Reading Lodge No. 254, serving as Master in 1976. His other
Masonic involvement includes serving as President of the Redding Masonic
Temple Association and later as Treasurer. He has served the local Bethel of
Job’s Daughters and was a founding trustee of the California Job’s Daughters
Foundation. He is a KCCH in Sacramento Scottish Rite, and belongs to Ben Ali
Shrine, Order of the Eastern Star and National Sojourners. Brother Kirkpatrick
th
has served on several Grand Lodge Committees, and as an Inspector of the 213
Masonic District. Also as a Trustee of the California Masonic Foundation,
California Masonic Memorial Temple, Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc., and
the Masonic Homes Board. He served as Grand Pursuivant in1995.
Brother Howard served as Grand Master of the Grand Lodge of
California during the year 2003-2004.

PUBLIC OPENING CEREMONY
Sunday, October 10, 2004
1:00 PM
The Public Opening of Grand Lodge was held in the Masonic
Auditorium of the California Masonic Memorial Temple, 1111 California Street,
San Francisco, California beginning at 1:00 p.m. The Grand Lodge was raised
by Worshipful Gary R. Quintrell, Master of Six Rivers Lodge No. 106.
Grand Master Howard D. Kirkpatrick, accompanied by his Grand
Lodge Officers, entered the Auditorium to the sound of the organ, and assumed
their stations. The Grand Chaplain gave the invocation.
The Grand Master welcomed all in attendance to the Public Opening of
th
the 155 Annual Communication of the Grand Lodge of California. He thanked
Worshipful Quintrell for raising the Grand Lodge.
The Grand Marshal was directed to introduce the visiting dignitaries
from other Masonic jurisdictions and the leaders of the concordant and
appendant bodies of the Family of Freemasonry in California. The following
were present:

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

7

At the conclusion of the introductions, the Grand Master remarked, “It
is a special pleasure and a privilege to welcome you to our Annual
Communication. To each of you I express the appreciation of all Masons in
California.”
The Grand Master asked the Grand Marshal to escort Bro. Michael
Salazar, Executive Officer of DeMolay in Northern California and Hawaii, to the
East, and the Grand Master asked him to bring the greetings on behalf of all the
members of the Family of Freemasonry in California.
The Grand Master thanked Bro. Salazar for his remarks, and asked the
Grand Marshal to conduct him to a seat in the auditorium.
The Grand Master then directed the Grand Marshal to retire and with
the assistance of the Knights of St. Andrew of the San Francisco Scottish Rite
Bodies, present the Flag of Our Country before the Altar of Freemasonry.
The Grand Master then invited Samuel J. Wybenga, a Sergeant in the
United States Marine Corps, to lead the Pledge of Allegiance to the Flag of Our
Country. Brother Sam recently returned from a tour of duty in Iraq. Sam was
deployed to Iraq on February 21, 2004, and returned home on September 11,
2004. Jenaha McLearn, Grand Drill Leader, International Order of Rainbow for
Girls, performed the National Anthem.
Masonic Student Assistance Program Presentation
The Grand Master called on Most Worshipful M. William Holsinger,
PGM, President of the California Masonic Foundation, who gave a report on the
Masonic Student Assistance Program. At the conclusion of his remarks, Most
Worshipful Holsinger introduced guest speaker Barbara Schemm.
The Grand Master thanked Most Worshipful Holsinger and Mrs.
th
Schemm for their remarks. The presentation celebrated the 10 Anniversary of
the MSAP Program.
Remarks from the Youth Orders
The Grand Master called on the four youth leaders of the three Masonic
youth orders in California. Miss Cameron Elder, Grand Bethel Honored Queen,
represented the International Order of Job's Daughters; Miss Erin Barclay, Grand
Worthy Advisor, represented the International Order of Rainbow for Girls;
Brother Benjamin Blythe, Jurisdiction Master Councilor, represented Northern
California Jurisdiction, Order of DeMolay; and Mr. Taylor Blakely, Jurisdiction
Master Councilor, represented Southern California Jurisdiction, Order of
DeMolay.

8

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Presentations to the Youth Orders
As each youth leader completed his or her remarks, the Grand Master
presented each youth leader with a contribution in the amount of $1500 from the
California Masonic Foundation to be used by the youth orders for leadership
development.
Daniel Carter Beard Masonic Scouter Award
The Grand Marshal was directed to conduct the Daniel Carter Beard
Masonic Souter Award recipients West of the Altar. The Grand Master, with the
assistance of the Deputy Grand Master, presented the following brethren with
this award recognizing their service to youth through Scouting: George M.
Brown, a member of Estero Lodge No. 719; Howard E. McMackin, a member of
Union Kit Carson Lodge No. 58; and James R. Reget, a member of Joseph L.
Shell Daylight Lodge No. 837.
Grand Master's Youth Support Award
The Grand Master announced that four brothers had been selected to
receive the Grand Master's Youth Support Award to recognize their outstanding
contribution to the Masonic youth orders. The Grand Master then called upon
Worshipful V. Kent Armstrong, Chairman of the Grand Lodge Youth Orders
Committee to assist him in presenting the Grand Master’s Youth Support Award
to the following honorees: Richard W. Hopper, a member of Visalia Mineral
King Lodge No. 128; Paul E. Lacroix, a member of Upland-Mt. Baldy Lodge
No. 419; and Howard E. McMackin, a member of Union Kit Carson Lodge No.
58. Brother Clifford Clause, a member of Sacramento Lodge No. 40, could not
be present.
The Grand Master directed the Grand Marshal to escort the recipients
to a place West of the Altar. The Grand Master and the Deputy Grand Master
then presented the awards while Brother Daley read the biography of each
honoree.
Richard W. Hopper
Richard came to Masonry through his family.
His
grandfathers both were Masons and his father was a Past Master of Las
Palmas Lodge, was active in Scottish Rite and belonged to Tehran
Shrine Temple. Richard started his “Masonic” career as a member of
the Fresno Chapter of DeMolay. He served as Master Councilor in
1960 and currently holds the Degree of Chevalier and the Blue Honor
Key for membership development. Richard joined Las Palmas Lodge

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

No. 366 located in Fresno and was raised a Master Mason in 1964. He
is a plural member in Visalia Mineral King Lodge No. 128 where he
served as Worshipful Master in 1998. He has served in various
capacities in this lodge since serving as Master.
Although his first association with Rainbow came when he was
dating his wife, a former Rainbow Girl, Richard has worked with the
International Order of the Rainbow for Girls since 1981 when his
daughter, Jana, joined Visalia Assembly No. 55. During this time he
has played the piano for Visalia Assembly at their meetings (since
1985) and was awarded the Grand Cross of Color for extended service
to this organization. In 1992 he was appointed to the Grand Executive
Committee where he has served to date. In 2003, after the death of the
Chairman of the Grand Executive Committee, he was appointed
Chairman of this committee. It should be noted that his wife is a former
Grand Officer in the International Order of Rainbow for Girls, and
currently serves as the California Grand Director of Grand Officers, a
capacity she has filled for the past five years.
Richard has served as Co-General Chairman for the annual
Grand Assembly sessions each year for the past ten years.
Richard works diligently for the International Order of the
Rainbow for Girls, giving freely of his time and energies to promote the
welfare of this Order. Additionally, he currently serves as a Trustee of
the Masonic Homes of California, part of a nine-member Board,
serving for a three-year term.
Paul E. Lacroix
Paul is a member of Upland-Mt. Baldy Lodge No. 419 in
Upland, California. When I first met him, I was “League Dad” of what
was then Citrus League in Southern California Jurisdiction, Order of
DeMolay. Citrus was one of nine leagues, or geographical divisions of
the jurisdiction. There were nine chapters in the league at that time, and
Paul was trying to get Pomona Chapter re-started from being inactive
for about two years. Unfortunately, his best efforts met with no support
from the two lodges in Pomona and he started to focus his efforts on
creating a chapter in Upland, where they had not had a chapter, but
there was an active Job’s Daughters Bethel and active Rainbow
Assembly. For his efforts instituting Foothill Chapter in Upland, he
received the Zerubbabel Key. Additionally, he has received the Cross
of Honor, Chevalier Degree and Legion of Honor, the Guild of the
Leather Apron (advisor of the year), and in 2001 was made a Deputy
member of the International Supreme Council.

9

10

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Since I met Paul, he has been a supporter of all the Masonic
Youth Groups and has also supported Boy Scouts, with both of his sons
in scouting. His oldest son, Jacque is our Junior Past Master councilor,
and his youngest son, Garrett, is our Senior Councilor. Paul has worked
closely with the lodges and Eastern Star Chapters in the area and has
invited all of them to all of our open ceremonies. He and several other
members of our advisory council are currently working diligently to
reinstitute the Rainbow Assembly at Upland.
While serving as Chapter Dad of Foothill Chapter, Paul also
served on the staff of Citrus League in various positions, always with
distinction. He also served on the jurisdiction staff as Director of
Conclave, Director of Ritual, and as a District Governor when the
jurisdiction was reorganized a few years ago. He is now again serving
Foothill Chapter as Chapter Dad and continues to promote Masonry
through his work with the Lodge, Eastern Star and the Masonic Youth
Groups.
Howard E. McMackin
Howard is a Mason in Union Kit Carson Lodge No. 58,
Sacramento, California. He has served as Treasurer for 15 years and
does attend meetings.
Howard and Delores’ daughter joined Job’s Daughters in 1974
and he started taking photos for Bethel No. 314. Shortly after that,
other Bethels asked him to take photos for them. He still takes photos
for all Bethels that want him to and provides most of the photos for the
Job’s Daughters newspaper. He was appointed Official Photographer
for the Job’s Daughters newspaper in 1994. He has been Chairman of
Arrangements for Grand Bethel, Chairman of Housing for Grand
Bethel, is on the 911 Committee (which consists of Past Associate
Bethel Guardians that fill in for Associate Bethel Guardians where and
when needed), and serves on the Location Committee.
In 1980, he became Associate Bethel Guardian of Bethel No.
314, Sacramento, and remained in that position until the Bethel merged
in 1992 and he then served as Promoter of Finance for a few years. He
became Associate Bethel Guardian of Bethel No. 291 and remained in
that position until he was elected Associate Grand Guardian in 1997.
As soon as his year was over as Associate Grand Guardian, he again
became Associate Bethel Guardian of Bethel NO. 291 and still serves in
that capacity with Mrs. Kim Waits.
During his year as Associate Grand Guardian with Mrs. Elaine
Davies, he headed the project that was very near to his heart and he did

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

11

a fantastic job of promoting this philanthropic project. The project was
“Child Life Program of the Shrine Hospitals” which helped to purchase
dolls that were called “Patient Dolls” for the Shrine Hospitals. The
dolls helped to demonstrate to the young patients what their operation
would be like and helped them to conquer some of their fears.
The doll that Howard took everywhere with him to
demonstrate the project was called J-Dee and she became like his own
child. He was very sorry, but very proud to see her become a
permanent resident of the Shrine Hospital when his term was done.
Howard still has visiting rights to visit J-Dee at the Shrine Hospital and
there could not be a more proud papa!
The project provided four dolls for the Shrine Hospitals, two
in Sacramento and two in Los Angeles. The Grand Officers and many
others in the International Order of Job’s Daughters also provided many
recreational items for the Hospitals, such as a pool table, fooz ball sets,
boom boxes, Nintendo boxes and video games. Thanks to all of
Howard’s dedication this was a very successful project for a very good
cause. He motivated many people to help!
Before becoming involved in Job’s Daughters he served as an
adult leader with Boy Scouts. He has been registered as a Boy Scout
since 1968. He worked with his son in Boy Scouts and again with his
grandsons.
When his grandson joined DeMolay he became a member of
the Advisory Committee and has worked with the DeMolay for six
years. Currently he is Chapter Dad for the Robert L. Wade Chapter in
Sacramento.
The distinguished Masons were saluted with the Public Grand Honors.
The Grand Master then asked the Grand Marshal to conduct the recipients to
seats in the auditorium.

12

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

DEVOTIONAL SERVICE
The Grand Master then observed that as the program drew to a close,
we would turn our thoughts inward and to the attention of the Grand Chaplain
for an Inspirational Message.
The Grand Master then called upon the Grand Chaplain, the Very
Reverend Raymond E. Davies, for his message. As part of the service, Gail and
Marianna Zachers, sang Amazing Grace.
The Grand Master thanked the Very Reverend Raymond E. Davies for
his truly inspirational message.
The Grand Master concluded by thanking all for attending the Public
th
Opening of the 155 Annual Communication of Grand Lodge. He led the Grand
Lodge Officers out of the auditorium.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

ONE HUNDRED FIFTY-FIFTH
ANNUAL COMMUNICATION
MONDAY MORNING, OCTOBER 11, 2004
th

The 155 Annual Communication of the Grand Lodge of Free and
Accepted Masons of the State of California was called to order by Grand Master
Howard D. Kirkpatrick, on Monday, October 11, 2004.
Worshipful Robert Haas, Past Master of La Jolla Lodge No. 518, raised
the opening session of Grand Lodge.
Reception of the Past Grand Masters
The Grand Master welcomed the brethren and directed the Grand
Marshal to present the Past Grand Masters of the Grand Lodge of California at
the Altar. The Grand Marshal then introduced each Past Grand Master,
following which the Grand Master, accompanied by the Deputy Grand Master,
personally greeted each one. The Grand Master asked the brethren to join him in
saluting our Past Grand Masters by the battery of three times three. The Past
Grand Masters were then escorted to seats reserved for them in the auditorium.
The Grand Master then directed the Grand Secretary to send to each absent Past
Grand Master the usual letter of greeting from the Grand Lodge.
The Grand Master read a communication from Most Worshipful Myron
E. Smith, Past Grand Master, expressing his regrets at not being able to attend
and extended his best wishes to the Grand Master and the Grand Lodge.
Roll Call
The Grand Secretary was asked to call the roll of Grand Lodge
Officers, Past Grand Masters, and Past Grand Officers, and to ascertain if a
constitutional number was present to convene the Grand Lodge of California.
The following were present as indicated by their response to the calling of the
roll:
Elected Officers

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Memorial Service
The Grand Master called on the Very Reverend Raymond E. Davies,
Grand Chaplain, to present a service of remembrance for our departed brethren.
Let us pray.
Creator of us all, we recognize that our time in this existence is
scheduled by You. As we pause to reflect on the memories of the Brethren you
have summoned to Your sessions of the Celestial Lodge this passed Masonic
year, may we dwell on the recollections of their virtuous and well spent lives,
and remember them as examples for our own efforts for this Fraternity and for
Thee. Amen
“It has been a custom among the Fraternity of Free and Accepted
Masons from time immemorial, at the request of a departed Brother or his
family, to assemble in the character of Masons, and with the solemn formalities
of the Craft, to offer up to his memory, before the world, this last tribute of our
affection.”
Thus begins the Ritual of the Funeral Service adopted for use in this
Jurisdiction by this Grand Lodge. It has been brought to use far too often in the
past few years. This past Masonic year alone has seen the passing of more than
twenty-five hundred Brethren who devoted a large part of their lives and fortunes
to this Fraternity.
Some labored among us quite noticeably. Some hardly noticed more
than locally. Some hardly noticed at all, until we are touched by their generosity
and dedication to our Fraternity when it is brought to our attention several years
after their passing. Some surprise us because the character they displayed
outwardly during their life gives no hint of the depth of their love for our
Fraternity.
Charles Richard Prime, a Master Mason of Downey United Lodge No.
220 passed from us November 22, 1998.
Theodore John Haddad, a Past Master of Arabic 763, now a component
of Pasadena 272, passed from us June 20, 2003.
It wasn’t until this year that we found that both of these Brothers had
bequeathed all that they possessed to programs of this Fraternity.
Bernard Reid Seamans of Liberty Lodge No. 299, born October 13,
1923, passed from us December 11, 2003. He was raised in San Bernardino on
May 15, 1958, and served as Master of San Bernardino Lodge No. 348 in 1970.
He received the Hiram Award from of Estero Lodge No. 719 in 1994,
subsequently serving as Wardens and then as Master in 1998. During his
exemplary period of efforts for his Lodges, Brother Bernie also found the time to
be very active in the York Rite bodies, and devoted a portion of his time to the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

17

welfare of our affiliated ladies as he served as Worthy Grand Patron for the State
of California during 1988. It was my privilege to serve as Worthy Patron of a
subordinate Chapter during that time. Brother Bernie was a true, caring, Master
Mason who inspired all who were associated with him.
We shall speak of two other Most Worshipful Brothers whose efforts
and dedication were well known during their lifetime.
Raymond Alwin Ebbage, Grand Master of this Jurisdiction from
October of 1973 till October of 1974, passed from us December 20, 2003.
John W Driskell, Grand Master of this Jurisdiction from October of
1985 till October of 1986, passed from us May 27, 2004.
During their lifetimes, these Most Worshipfuls left little doubt where
they stood relative to this Fraternity. Their commitment was full measure and
constant.
Most Worshipful Donald B. McCaw, PGM, delivered a eulogy for
Most Worshipful Raymond A. Ebbage, Past Grand Master, who passed away
December 20, 2003.
Most Worshipful Anthony P. Wordlow, PGM, delivered a eulogy for
Most Worshipful John W. Driskell, Past Grand Master, who passed away May
27, 2004.

18

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

EULOGY
RAYMOND ALWIN EBBAGE
Grand Master 1973-1974
Born: March 20, 1913
Entered into Rest: December 20, 2003
th

Most Worshipful Raymond Alwin Ebbage was installed as our 108
Grand Master in October 1973 during the first public ceremony of installation in
the history of this Grand Lodge. At that time, we had a membership of more
than 220,000 Master Masons and 681 Lodges in this jurisdiction (which then
included the State of Hawaii), and were led by Brother Ray with great honor and
distinction.
Our Grand Master was born in Vancouver, British Columbia, on March
th
20 , 1913; orphaned at an early age; and came to California to live with
relatives. His early education was in the public schools, in fact in 43 of them,
according to Ray’s account, culminating in his graduation from Polytechnic High
School in San Francisco. He then matriculated to the University of San
Francisco, where he majored in accounting.
Upon coming of age, Ray became a citizen of the United States. He
married his wife, Ruth, in October of 1937. Sadly, Ruth was ill during Ray’s
climb up the Grand Line of the Grand Lodge, and was not able to participate
with him; yet Ray carried on all of his responsibilities with that well-known
wonderful smile. Ruth preceded him in death some 25 years ago, and then, in
1980, he married Billie, who was of tremendous support to him in all of his
many Masonic activities thereafter.
During World War II, Ray being the patriot that he was, enlisted as a
private in the Infantry of his adopted country. His duties took him to the
European Theater of Operations, where his Infantry Division participated in the
historic Battle of the Bulge, which was the last counter-offensive of the
retreating German Army. That battle took place from December 16, 1944 to
January 25, 1945 and has been called the largest and bloodiest single encounter
American soldiers ever fought. Hitler had sent a quarter of a million troops
across an 85 mile stretch of the Allied Front from southern Belgium into
Luxembourg, causing a “bulge” in some 50 miles of the Allied defenses, thus the
reason for naming it “The Battle of the Bulge”.
In the deadly cold winter weather of that battle, over 76,000 Americans
were killed, wounded or captured, and the German Army suffered over 100,000
th
casualties. The 7 Army Infantry Battalion, in which Ray served, suffered
greatly and Ray’s Infantry Company lost nearly all of its troops. As a result of
his courage and leadership abilities, however, our Brother received field
promotions to the rank of Captain and Company Commander and was awarded

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

19

the Silver Star, the Bronze Star with Oak Leaf Clusters, and the French Croix de
Guerre.
The terrible memories of those days gave us a fierce patriot and great
American as our Grand Master a quarter of a century later. Most Worshipful
Brother Ebbage was distinguished not only by his tall, erect appearance with
silver hair, but also as an accomplished silver-tongued orator.
Those of us who had the experience of listening to him speak marveled
that he did so substantially without notes, and while walking among his
audiences, speaking directly from his heart. He expounded upon the duties,
responsibilities and privileges of being an American, and among other honors, he
received the Freedom Foundation of Valley Forge, George Washington
Honorary Gold Medal Award for a stirring speech about “Our Flag”. I well
remember that after giving that speech at a Grand Master’s Reception, my wife,
“Skip”, told Ray how much she enjoyed his dramatic speech. As just one
example of the attention paid by him to his audiences, Grand Master Ray said to
Skip: “If you enjoyed it so much, why were you crying?” Indeed, Ray brought
his emotions into our hearts.
Upon his return from military service, Ray resumed his career in the
insurance industry, and in due time became a Chartered Life Underwriter, and
the president of the Life Managers’ Association, while serving as a General
Agent in the Sacramento area for a major life insurance company.
In his early Masonic career, Brother Ebbage received the Degree of
Master Mason on February 17, 1950 in Capital City Lodge No. 499, and served
as its Master in 1957. He was appointed to the office of Senior Grand Deacon
by Most Worshipful Brother Ira Coburn in 1962; became President of the
Northern California Past Masters’ Association in 1967; and served upon
numerous Grand Lodge Committees and Boards prior to being elected as our
Junior Grand Warden in 1970.
Ray became a member of the Sacramento Bodies of the Scottish Rite;
served as the Venerable Master of its Lodge of Perfection in 1968; was
decorated with the Rank and Designation of a Knight Commander of the Court
of Honor the following year; and was Coroneted a Scottish Rite Mason of the
rd
33 Degree eight years later. Brother Ray was appointed as Chairman and
Personal Representative in the Sacramento Bodies to the Sovereign Grand
Inspector General in California in 1977 by Most Worshipful Henry Christian
Claussen, and served in that office for 27 years until his death at age 91.
Other Concordant and Appendant Masonic organizations of which
Most Worshipful Brother Ray was a member and leader include the Sacramento
York Rite Bodies, the Sciots Tract Pyramid No. 11, the Ben Ali Temple of the
Shrine, Sacramento Court No. 119 of the Royal Order of Jesters, Sacramento
Chapter No. 133 of the National Sojourners, and the St. Francis Conclave of the
Red Cross of Constantine. He received the Honorary Legion of Honor Award of

20

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

the Order of DeMolay, and was a great supporter of that Order as he was of the
International Orders of Job’s Daughters and Rainbow for Girls.
In his community, Ray was a member of the Fort Sutter Lions Club; the
Sacramento Metropolitan Area Chamber of Commerce; the Red Cross; the
United Crusade; and the Golden Empire Council of the Boy Scouts of America.
Always the salesman, our Grand Master on every proper occasion took
time and effort to encourage our members to be “salesmen” of Masonry by
personally wearing our honored Square and Compass emblem and by each of us
displaying through our personal actions, our conviction in the value of the tenets
of Freemasonry.
We remember the life of Grand Master Raymond A. Ebbage as a great
citizen of the United States and a fierce patriotic promoter of its virtues. We
were blessed with his leadership as our Grand Master, and, in this beautiful
auditorium in which he presided 30 years ago on this day, we honor his memory.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

RAYMOND ALWIN EBBAGE
PAST GRAND MASTER
March 20, 1913 – December 20, 2003

21

22

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

JOHN WILLARD DRISKELL
PAST GRAND MASTER
August 15, 1924 – May 27, 2004

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

23

EULOGY
JOHN WILLARD DRISKELL
Grand Master 1985-1986
Born: August 15, 1924
Entered into Rest: May 27, 2004
Our one hundred twentieth Grand Master John W. Driskell,
affectionately known as “Big John”, left an indelible mark on the Masonic
fraternity. He touched the lives of countless Masons and their families.
Open, caring, friendly, and approachable, he was a Grand Master who
was willing to listen.
Born in Barnham, Texas, August 15, 1924, young John was educated in
the local public schools. As the storm clouds of war gathered early in 1941,
John joined the U.S. Naval Air Corps. He served in the Pacific Theater during
the second world war.
A very moving experience for John during his tenure as Grand Master
was to view the plaque dedicated to those members of the Masonic fraternity
who lost their lives at Pearl Harbor on December 7, 1941. He reverently placed
a beautiful lei upon the now still waters above the U.S.S. Arizona Monument in
their memory.
He served in the U.S. Naval Air Corps until 1947. In 1948 he entered
the U.S. Air Force and served until 1953. His concern for others entered into his
work in the U.S. Air Force on his assignment to the Air-Sea Search and Rescue
Team.
In 1945 John married his beloved Eleanor. They were blessed with two
daughters, born in Hawaii while he was stationed there. As teenagers, the girls
became active members of Bethel No. 74, International Order of Job’s
Daughters; Janet was Honored Queen and Pamela was an appointed officer.
John and Eleanor devoted many gratifying years of service to Job’s Daughters.
He was proud to be made a “Member of Honor” in Bethel No. 74.
Following military service, the Driskell family moved to the
Sacramento area. John worked at Aerojet, and later began a long career at the
University of California at Davis. He worked in the physical plant department
and retired as the Senior Superintendent, Services Division of the Physical Plant
Department.
While employed at Aerojet in the early 1960’s, he was impressed by
several men with whom he worked – they were Masons. He petitioned to
receive the degrees of Masonry in Capital City Lodge No. 499 in Sacramento.
John Willard Driskell was raised a Master Mason on April 27, 1962. From that

24

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

day forward, he was an active member of his Lodge. He served with distinction
as Master in 1969.
John held membership in several appendant and concordant Masonic
bodies including National Sojourners, Royal Order of Scotland, Sciots Tract
Pyramid No. 11, Davis High Twelve, Ben Ali Past Masters Club and Chinese
Acacia Club. A member of Ben Ali Shrine and an honorary member of Al
Malaikah Shrine, he served as an Ambassador at Large for both Temples.
In recognition of his service to Freemasonry and the Sacramento
Scottish Rite, he was decorated a Knight Commander of the Court of Honor in
1983 and coroneted an Inspector General Honorary of the Thirty-third and Last
Degree in 1987 by the Supreme Council.
He enjoyed working with our Masonic youth groups. Besides his long,
dedicated service to Job’s Daughters, he was a strong supporter of the
International Order of Rainbow for Girls and the Order of DeMolay. He was the
recipient of the DeMolay Honorary Legion of Honor Degree.
In 1974 John was appointed Senior Grand Deacon of the Grand Lodge
of California and Hawaii. He was a member of the 1977 Junior Grand Warden
Selection Committee. From 1977 through 1981 he was Inspector of the 219th
Masonic District. He was appointed to the 1979-80 Commission to Study the
Organizational Structure of Grand Lodge. As a Trustee on the Masonic Homes
Board, his expertise in the building field was of immense value.
His talents, abilities and dedication to the Masonic Fraternity were
recognized when he was elected Junior Grand Warden in 1982. He was
subsequently elected to each successive office. On October 16, 1985 he was
elected Grand Master of Masons in California and Hawaii.
The one hundred and thirty-seventh Annual Communication of Grand
Lodge over which Most Worshipful Brother John presided is noted as the
session when we adopted the regulation of suspension from all Lodges a Brother
is a member of if he fails to pay his dues in one; and the rejection of applicants
based on physical defects was removed from the CMC.
A man of character, ability, and exemplary accomplishments oozing
with charisma and a warm spirit, our Most Worshipful Brother John was unique.
He seemed to bring out the best in people. A stranger was but a friend he had
yet to meet.
He was passionate about Masonry.
He loved the outdoors and was an avid and skilled fisherman.
He had a quick wit, and relished a good story. He could dish it out, but
he could take it, too.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

25

He adored his family. Eleanor was called before him to that House not
made with Hands. Oh, how he missed her.
Always there was that special twinkle in his eyes as he extended his
hand to all in brotherly love and affection.
He never lost that little Texas twang.
He was a gentleman who was a gentle man.
As Grand Master, Most Worshipful John Driskell will be remembered
as an open, friendly, decisive and positive leader.
He brought “Masonry, the Last Frontier” into reality to every corner of
California and Hawaii, from the largest to the smallest of Lodges.
Farewell, “Big John”, farewell.

26

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Of the number of Brethren who have passed from us this year, not all
have dedicated themselves or the fruit of their efforts to the extent of these
mentioned Brothers. But the average number of years of membership of those
who have passed recently tells us that all those who passed from us this year did
commit themselves at least to the extent of that many years dues and
philanthropic contributions; and to the promotion of this Fraternity for that
number of years, by virtue of their individual forms of displaying their
membership.
There was a time when the short list of those who passed from us each
year could be read in less time than I have talked so far. That is not the case at
this time, and will not be the case for several years to come. Dust to dust is a
fact of life, and as we were of large number in past years, the number of those
passing from us is a large number now.
As much as each Brother deserves recognition for his time devoted to
this Fraternity, we can only speak of a small group as representative of the
whole.
We ask that the remembrance of the specific devotion of each of those
we have not spoken about shall be the duty of us here in attendance. Each of us
here today carries the memory of at least one Brother who has passed from us.
That is their true legacy to us. The recollection of their virtuous and well-spent
life. It provides us not only with comfort and consolation, but inspiration to be a
similar example for others to recall after our passing.
None of us passes through our allotted span of time without being
touched and influenced by others, nor without ourselves touching and
influencing those around us. As a leaf falling into a pond sends ripples in all
directions, even the smallest of our acts, good or ill, radiates out from us to be
noticed by those around us in all directions. As the memories of our Brethren
who have passed from this existence inspire us by their lifetime example of
applying the Three Great Lights of Masonry, so too must we both revere their
example and conduct our own lives in the same manner. That is their
immortality manifesting through us, and the remembrance of our conduct after
we depart will be our immortality on this Earth.
We are all subject to the final decree, yet, there is hope. We all believe
there is something beyond this mortal existence. Somewhere where we shall all
meet again. An existence where we are sure to meet, in that Celestial Lodge,
where the Supreme Architect of the Universe forever presides.
Until then, we are left with bidding our departed Brethren a temporary
farewell. The silver cord is loosed: the golden bowl is broken; the pitcher is
broken at the fountain; and the wheel is broken at the cistern. The dust has
returned to the earth as it was, and the spirit has returned to God who gave it.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

27

Almighty Father, into Thy hands we commend the souls of our departed
Brothers.
Amen

TUESDAY AFTERNOON, OCTOBER 12, 2004
At 1:00 p.m. the Grand Master called the Grand Lodge from
refreshment to labor, following which the Grand Chaplain offered prayer. The
Grand Master then announced that the Grand Lodge had been raised for the
afternoon session by Worshipful Bro. Gene Ryan, Master of Reading Lodge No.
254.
Credentials Committee Report
The Grand Master called on the Grand Secretary for the final report of
the Credentials Committee. The Grand Secretary reported that there were 328
Lodges registered as being in attendance, and a total of 1,260 Master Masons
and others in attendance.
Adoption of the 2004-2005 Grand Lodge Budget
The Grand Master called on the Deputy Grand Master, Right
Worshipful David R. Doan, to present the 2004-2005 Grand Lodge Budget. He
did so, and a copy of the budget is found in Appendix B. The Chairman of the
Finance Committee, Worshipful Miguel Cheng, moved the adoption of the 20042005 Budget, which was seconded by Past Grand Master Alvin J. Weis, Acting
Grand Treasurer, and was carried by a majority affirmative vote.
Adoption of the 2005-2006 Grand Lodge Per Capita
The Grand Master then called on the Senior Grand Warden, Right
Worshipful Frederick L. Sorsabal, to present the 2005-2006 Grand Lodge Per
Capita. He did so, and a copy of the proposal is found in Appendix C.
The Chairman of the Finance Committee, Worshipful Miguel Cheng,
moved the adoption of the 2005-2006 Grand Lodge Per Capita in the amount of
$26.00, which was seconded by Past Grand Master Alvin J. Weis, Acting Grand
Treasurer, and carried by a majority affirmative vote.
Election of Officers
The Grand Master then asked the Grand Secretary to explain the
balloting procedure for the election of officers. No ballot would be necessary if
the office were not contested. If more than one nomination were received for an
office, ballot cards would be distributed for the purpose of balloting for that
office.

36

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Election of the Grand Master
Deputy Grand Master David R. Doan was nominated Grand Master by
Most Worshipful R. Stephen Doan, Past Grand Master. No further nominations
appearing, it was moved, seconded and carried that the nominations be closed,
and that the Grand Secretary be instructed to cast a unanimous ballot for Right
Worshipful David R. Doan to serve as Grand Master for the ensuing Grand
Lodge Year. Upon his acceptance, he was declared unanimously elected.
Election of Deputy Grand Master
Senior Grand Warden Frederick L. Sorsabal was nominated Deputy
Grand Master by Most Worshipful Alvin J. Weis, Past Grand Master. No
further nominations appearing, it was moved, seconded and carried that the
nominations be closed, and that the Grand Secretary be instructed to cast a
unanimous ballot for Right Worshipful Frederick L. Sorsabal to serve as Deputy
Grand Master for the ensuing Grand Lodge Year. Upon his acceptance, he was
declared unanimously elected.
Election of Senior Grand Warden
Junior Grand Warden Melvyn B. Stein was nominated Senior Grand
Warden by Most Worshipful Jack R. Levitt, Past Grand Master. No further
nominations appearing, it was moved, seconded and carried that the nominations
be closed, and that the Grand Secretary be instructed to cast a unanimous ballot
for Right Worshipful Melvyn B. Stein to serve as Senior Grand Warden for the
ensuing Grand Lodge Year. Upon his acceptance, he was declared unanimously
elected.
Election of Junior Grand Warden
The Grand Master announced that pursuant to Section 510 of the
California Masonic Code, Worshipful Brother Richard W. Hopper, a Past
Master of Visalia Mineral King Lodge No. 128 in Visalia had been
recommended for the office of Junior Grand Warden of the Grand Lodge of
California for the ensuing Grand Lodge Year. The Grand Secretary then read
the biography previously circulated in accordance with this section, while
Brother Hopper stood before Grand Lodge. He was then escorted to the Grand
Tiler’s Room, while other nominations were called for. None appearing, the
Junior Grand Warden moved that the Grand Secretary be instructed to cast a
unanimous ballot for Worshipful Brother Richard W. Hopper to serve as Junior
Grand Warden for the ensuing Grand Lodge Year. The motion was seconded by
the Grand Lecturer and unanimously carried. Upon his return to the floor of the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

Grand Lodge, and upon his acceptance, he was declared unanimously elected by
the Grand Master.

that the Grand Secretary be instructed to cast a unanimous ballot for Warren J.
Blomseth to serve as Grand Treasurer. The motion was duly seconded and
adopted unanimously, and upon his acceptance, the Grand Master declared him
elected.

Most Worshipful Stanley M. Cazneaux, Past Grand Master, nominated

nominations be closed, and that the Grand Treasurer be instructed to cast a
unanimous ballot for John L. Cooper III to serve as Grand Secretary. The
motion was duly seconded and adopted unanimously, and upon his acceptance,
the Grand Master declared him elected.

Most Worshipful Stanley L. Channon, Past Grand Master, nominated
Worshipful Harold A. Macy to serve as Grand Lecturer for the ensuing Grand
Lodge Year. No further nominations appearing, he moved that the nominations
be closed, and that the Grand Secretary be instructed to cast a unanimous ballot
for Harold A. Macy to serve as Grand Lecturer. The motion was duly seconded
and adopted unanimously, and upon his acceptance, the Grand Master declared
him elected.

APPENDIX A

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

43

GRAND MASTER’S MESSAGE
The California Masonic Code requires that the Grand Master at the
Annual Communication present a written message covering:
1.

All of his official acts during the year.

2.

The general condition of Masonry in the jurisdiction.

3.

Recommend such legislation, which is deemed necessary or
expedient for the welfare of the Craft.

I will cover these items in a few minutes.
th

Welcome to everyone to the 155 Annual Communication of the Grand
Lodge of California. I sincerely hope that you will enjoy this session whether
you are a member from our jurisdiction or if you are one of our visitors from
another jurisdiction. We are glad you are here. The theme I chose last year was
“Personal Responsibility – Accept It and Teach It”. It means something
different to each person and that is what I expected because everyone will apply
it in a different way. My application of personal responsibility was to create a
vision for our Fraternity and to set the stage for a change of attitude within our
Fraternity so the vision could be implemented after I completed my year as
Garnd Master.
In my acceptance speech, one year ago, I talked about vision. A vision
of the future of Freemasonry in California and invited all that wanted to
participate to contact me. Well, I received a lot of well thought out comments
and suggestions, of which, I don’t have the time or space to repeat all of it.
However, a summary of these suggestions is really quite simple. The lodges and
their members need to be involved in community centered activities, family
centered activities, more Masonic education or philosophy of Masonry, more
and better leadership training and a multi-generational approach in our message
to members and the public.
We are dealing with a membership that comes from diverse generations,
as you heard this morning. But it doesn’t make any difference how old you are
to wield a paintbrush at a widow’s home. Everyone enjoys the fellowship and
the old timers have fun teaching the younger ones how to use the paintbrush and
the younger ones enjoy the stories and tall tales the older ones can tell. An
activity like this can fulfill many of the suggestions that I just mentioned. For
Masonic education I would encourage each lodge to join the Southern California
Research Lodge and utilize their program by notifying the Research Lodge of
every Entered Apprentice you have on the rolls. It is a proven program that will
help.

44

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

My vision is the Grand Lodge and the constituent lodges should
develop men through wisdom to improve their leadership ability with the
principle to engage the heart, mind and spirit of those who choose to participate.
State of the Craft
Leadership Training: The new Lodge Certification Program was
launched to teach lodge leaders the basics about administration and management
of our lodges, freeing up the Wardens’ Retreats to turn to an emphasis on
leadership instead of management. The curriculum for the Wardens’ Retreat was
thus revised in the direction of true leadership training, a new emphasis that was
warmly welcomed by those who attended. In addition, the first-ever Board
Governance Conference was instituted – a conference designed to help members
of our constituent Boards become more effective leaders in our Fraternity and
our community. I hear comments all the time that we are not attracting
community leaders to our Fraternity. I believe we have the procedure all wrong.
We should not be concentrating on attracting community leaders to our
Fraternity but instead furnishing fraternal leadership to our community. If our
members were involved in community affairs then we would be represented in
our community.
The leadership training I believe will lead more of our members to
involve themselves in community organizations. They will have the confidence
to volunteer for local committees or even run for political office. Hopefully, this
will begin the movement necessary to again have our Fraternity heavily involved
in local, state and national governments as our ancestors were in the beginning of
our great country and state.
Restructuring our Boards: The California Masonic Foundation was
reorganized to take the lead in developing educational programs for Grand
Lodge as well as the traditional scholarship program and the Masonic Student
Assistance Program. Without diminishing a commitment to these programs, the
Foundation accepted the challenge to become a focus for leadership training in
our Grand Lodge. The Foundation took over the responsibility for our Historic
Sites and Buildings, as well as the Institute for Masonic Studies and its Henry
Wilson Coil Masonic Library and Museum. I hope to work on a project this next
year that will add a list of Masonic Centers throughout the state, with pictures of
each building. This will include which buildings are of a historic nature so that
they may be preserved. Also, I want to provide a listing of historic items so
many of our odges have in their possession, also with an eye towards
preservation. In addition, the California Masonic Memorial Temple was brought
under the umbrella of the California Masonic Foundation as a “supporting
organization” – allowing it to concentrate on the preservation of our beautiful
California Masonic Temple – and absorbing the responsibilities formerly
dischared by Nob Hill Masonic Management. The result was a leaner and more

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

45

cost-effective organization – with an enhanced ability to carry out the primary
functions of our charitable endeavors on behalf of Grand Lodge.
New Lodges: This year we promoted the idea of new lodges working
in languages other than English and as a result two new lodges were issued
Dispensations with one lodge working in Spanish and one in Armenian. This
effort had an additional benefit in that it encouraged the formation of one
“European-style” lodge in San Diego, and one “Traditional Observance” ldoge
in Oakland. These four new lodges are the greatest number of new lodges in one
year in the last forty years.
However, many of our lodges continue to experience difficulty in
surviving, which results in a number of consolidations during the year. Two
lodges had such difficulty in managing their affairs that their charters were
suspended. Culver City-Foshay Lodge No. 467 continued for the second year in
a row under direct supervision by the Grand Master. La Jolla Lodge No. 518
reached a point of floundering and was not functioning properly. Therefore, I
appointed new officers at the time of their suspension. However, both are well
on the way to recovery and while much remains to be done, both can be proud of
the progress they have made toward the restoration of their charters.
In several other cases I sent a special representative or a special team to
a lodge that was experiencing turmoil. While the charters were not suspended,
there were enough problems to warrant this kind of special attention from the
Grand Master. In almost every case the trouble was caused by disputes over
finances. It is evident that we need a program to audit our lodges and hall
associations on a regular basis – to encourage good fiscal practices and to spot
and correct trouble in advance. I have made some suggestions to place audit
teams in each Division but unfortunately I have not had enough time to complete
this project but will encourage my successors to do so.
The general State of the Craft is good. We have made several major
changes this year as I have just mentioned to you. I believe these changes will
strengthen our Fraternity in the years ahead. However, these changes have not
removed all problems facing us.
Our Masonic Homes Board of Trustees is working hard to make sure
there are sufficient funds available 25 to 50 years in the future to meet our
commitments to the residents in the Home. This Board needs and deserves your
support.
Many of our brethren have forgotten the tenents of Freemasonry –
Brotherly Love, Relief and Truth. Instead they are reverting to greed,
degradation of their fellow Mason, mud slinging and power grabbing. You
know who you are and I invite you to change your ways or leave our Fraternity
before you stain it more than you have.

46

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

The majority of our members are God fearing, decent and trustworthy.
However, too many of you are remaining quiet and non-verbal when you should
be asking questions, talking and being active in your lodges and community. It
is time to stand up and be counted.
Charitable Assistance
The Grand Lodge answered a call for assistance in Southern California
after the fire in that area. We made a contribution to a Brother in need and also
a contribution to a hospital who cared for a Brother who lost his life due to the
fire. This was made possible by generous contributions when members
answered our appeal for funds. Leftover funds were added to our reserve for
needed assistance. In recet weeks we received an appeal for funds for Brother
sin the southern states, including Floriday, after they were hit by multiple
hurricanes. The Grand Lodge of California contributed $20,000 to the Masonic
Service Association for distribution in those states.
Official Acts
The California Masonic Code Section 26295 states that if a man does
not wish to be obligated on the Holy Bible, he must select alternate Holy
Writings from a list promulgated from time to time by the Grand Master of the
Holy Writings of those recognized religions whose theology is not inconsistent
with a belief in a Supreme Being and a future existence. I had occasion to
thoroughly research, study and examine one of those alternate Holy Writings on
the approved list and I determined that the Ordo Templi Orientis, commonly
referred to as the Oto, should not be on that list and removed it and reissued the
approved list.
Decisions and Recommendations
My Decisions and Recommendations will be covered in another part of
the program. However, I do want to mention one of them to you now. The
“Grand Master’s Lodge” has been proposed by me to meet a very dire need. In
the next 15 years we estimate that we will lose 160 lodges from this Grand
Lodge primarily due to the high death rate of our World War II veterans. At the
present time, we encourage and sometimes force consolidations but the
consolidations don’t always work for many reasons. When a lodge surrenders its
charter each member is issued a dimit that is good for one year. During that one
year period he must find another lodge to join or he loses all rights and benefits.
Many of these members may be elderly or ill and cannot find another lodge. The
Grand Master’s Lodge allows a Lodge to surrender their charter and their
members will be automatically transferred as active members to the Grand

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

47

Master’s Lodge thereby preserving all rights and benefits therein. It is simply a
safety net for our members.
Let me finish with some thoughts about what happens when we just sit
back and say, “It is not affecting me much, so it is okay”. Recently I read where
the county of Los Angeles was going to remove the cross from their Seal. Well,
it is just one more thing that most people are saying, “Well okay” but is it?
Billy Graham’s daughter was interviewed on the CBS program The
Early Show and Jane Clayson asked here “How could God let something like the
attacks on September 11 happen?” Anne Graham gave an extremely profound
and insightful response. She said, “I believe God is deeply saddened by this, just
as we are, but for years we’ve been telling God to get out of our schools, to get
out of our government and to get out of our lives. And being the gentleman he
is, I belive he has calmly backed out.
How can we expect God to give us his blessing and his protection if we
demand he leave us alone?”
This reminds me of a story I just heard. A dog had followed his owner
to school. His owner was a fourth grader at a public elementary school.
However, when the bell rang, the dog sidled inside the building and made it all
the way to the child’s classroom before the teacher noticed and shooed him
outside and closed the door behind him. The dog sat down, whimpered and
stared at the closed doors. Then God appeared beside the dog and patted his
head and said, “Don’t feel bad fella’ . . . they won’t let me in either.”
It may have started when Madeleine Murray O’Hare complained she
didn’t want prayer in our schools, and we said okay.
Then someone said you better not read the Bible in school, the Bible
says thou shalt not kill, thou shalt not steal, and love your neighbor as yourself,
and we said okay.
Then Dr. Benjamin Spock said we shouldn’t spank our children when
they misbehave because their little personalities would be warped and we might
damage their self-esteem (Dr. Spock’s son committed suicide). We said an
expert should know what he’s talking about. And we said okay.
Then someone said, let’s let our daughters have abortions if they want,
and they won’t even have to tell their parents. And we said okay.
Then some wise school board member said, since boys will be boys and
they’re going to do it anyway, let’s give our sons all the condoms they want so
they can have all the fun they desire, and we won’t have to tell their parents they
got them at school. And we said okay.
Then some of our top elected officials said it doesn’t matter what we do
in private as long as we do our jobs. Agreeing with them, we said it doesn’t

48

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

matter to me what anyone, including the President, does in private as long as I
have a job and the economy is good.
Then someone said let’s print magazines with pictures of nude women
and call it wholesome, down-to-earth appreciation for the beauty of the female
body and we said okay.
And then someone else took that appreciation a step further and
published pictures of nude children and then further again by making them
available on the internet. And we said okay, they’re entitled to free speech.
Then the entertainment industry said let’s make TV shows and movies
that promote profanity, violence, and illicit sex. Let’s record music that
encourages rape, drugs, murder, suicide, and satanic themes. And we said, “It’s
just entertainment, it has no adverse effect, nobody takes it seriously anywahy,
so go right ahead”.
Now we’re asking ourselves why our children have no conscience, why
they don’t know right from wrong, and why it doesn’t bother them to kill
strangers, their classmates, and themselves.
Probably, if we think about it long and hard enough, we can figure it
out. I think it has a great deal to do with “we reap what we sow.”
Funny how simple it is for people to trash God and then wonder why
the world’s going to hell. Funny how we believe what the newspapers say, but
question what the Bible says.
Funny how you can send “jokes” through email and they spread like
wildfire but when you start sending messages regarding the Lord, people think
twice about sharing.
Funny how lewd, crude, vulgar and obscene articles pass freely through
cyberspace, but public discussion of God is suppressed in the school and
workplace.
Funny how we can be more worried about what other people think of us
than what God thinks of us.
This all comes back to someone’s interpretation of the First
Amendment to the Constitution which is the restrictions on the powers of
Congress which reads in part: Congress shall make no law respecting an
establishment of religion, or prohibiting the free exercise therof. Nowhere does
it say anything about the separation of church and state and separation of church
and state is what is being used to justify the removing of any reference to God in
our schools and government.
We need to have the backbone that our founding Fathers had when they
created our great country, the determination our ancestors had when they came
west to settle this beautiful state and the vision to see what we need to do, to

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

49

stand shoulder to shoulder across our free and democratic republic and say we
have a toleration for different religions but government does not have the right to
remove religion from our lives.
It is time, for us as Masons, to use our body, mind and spirit, to stand
up and be counted!
May God bless you and keep you.
Respectfully submitted,
Howard D. Kirkpatrick, Grand Master

APPENDIX B

69

REPORT OF THE DEPUTY GRAND MASTER
2004-2005 Grand Lodge Budget
David R. Doan, Deputy Grand Master
(As mailed to the Secretaries of the Lodges in a
Memorandum dated August 24, 2004)
Section 7040 of the California Masonic Code requires me, as the
Deputy Grand Master, to prepare the Grand Lodge Budget for the period of
November 1, 2004 through October 31, 2005. The Budget is based on the
amount of Per Capita collected this year plus additional income from the interest
of the Grand Lodge Endowment Fund and Investments, anticipated degree fees,
as well as reimbursements from other Masonic Entities for services provided by
Grand Lodge.
This year’s Grand Lodge Budget is radically different from last year’s
budget and therefore, it is difficult to compare the two budgets. I have
restructured the budget to make it easier to understand and easier to hold people
accountable for how they use the precious resources that have been entrusted to
their use. The first page of the budget is a summary of that portion of the Budget
that is funded by money collected from Per Capita, Fees and income from the
interest of the Grand Lodge Endowment Fund and Investments. This is the Net
Grand Lodge Proposed Budget that is used to fund Grand Lodge activities. The
pages that follow it are graphs designed to make it easier for you to understand
how the Net Grand Lodge Budget is used.
The pages starting with page 5 provide a detailed breakdown of the
entire Grand Lodge Budget. The first column is the total budget of Grand Lodge
including all of the services that are provided by Grand Lodge. The middle
column shows where and how much money is reimbursed by other Masonic
Entities, such as the Masonic Homes, the Foundation and income collected from
things such as the fee charged to Warden’s Leadership Retreat attendees. The
third column is the Net Grand Lodge Budget described above. By providing you
this information, you can see all of the services that are provided by Grand
Lodge and which ones are being financed by other sources and which ones are
financed or supplemented by your per capita money.
I have restructured many areas of the budget to make it more
transparent and easier to understand. One such area is the Ritual Committee’s
Budget. I have separated the Ritual Committee Budget into several sections.
One section is the Inspectors’ expenses; it has been separated into five parts, so
that Assistant Grand Lecturers can better manage their Inspectors’ expenses.
The expenses of the Grand Lecturer and his Assistants have also been separated
from the Ritual Committee budget so that the Grand Lecturer can manage his
assistants’ expenses. The Ritual Budget line now represents the programs of the
Ritual Committee such as divisional conferences and ritual workshops that are
conducted around the state. This change allows the Ritual Committee to better

70

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

manage its resources and gives the craft better information about how its money
is being used.
As I proposed last year, when the per capita was approved, I have
placed additional money into Masonic Education, Leadership Training, and
Membership Retention and Development lines. I believe these programs are
critical to the long-term success of our craft. At Grand Lodge this year you will
hear more about how these programs have been enhanced to help lodges educate
their members, train the next generation of Masonic Leaders and build upon the
strengths of our membership and our values. Masonic Values brighten our
communities, SHARE THE LIGHT.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

INSTALLATION ADDRESS
DAVID R. DOAN
Grand Master of Masons in California, 2004-2005
Good Morning. Thank you for sharing this great day with the 2005
th
Grand Family. It is an honor and a privilege to be the 140 Grand Master of
Masons in California. As I look at the 139 men who have had the privilege of
being Grand Master in California, I am humbled.
th

Whether it is the 34 Grand Master, Most Worshipful James A. Foshay,
installed in 1900, who worked tirelessly for the improvement of Public
th
Education in California; or the 39 Grand Master, Most Worshipful Motley
Flint, installed in 1905, who was a banker, but committed his life to the
assistance of others as evidenced by his 25 years as Chairman of the Board of
Relief in Los Angeles, his relief efforts for San Francisco after the 1906
th
earthquake and who left his entire fortune to charity; or the 69 Grand Master,
Most Worshipful Earl Warren, installed in 1935, who was an attorney and served
his community and this state in many elected capacities, including District
Attorney and Governor of California. The only man elected as Governor by
both the Democrats and Republicans in the same election. He was also the Vice
Presidential Candidate in 1948 for Brother Thomas Dewey when he ran against
Most Worshipful Brother Harry Truman for President of the United States, and
who served our great country as Chief Justice of the Supreme Court during some
difficult times; or the Grand Masters that I have served during my 29 years of
membership in our Great Fraternity; I am inspired by their behavior as men and
as Masons that displayed in their fullest splendor their personal and their
Masonic values for all the world to see and to follow.
Light is a metaphor for good and for the act of gaining knowledge,
which makes each of us better, or more enlightened. Masons use light as a
metaphor for both good and gaining knowledge. The practice of our Masonic
Values in our daily lives is not only good in and of itself, but it demonstrates the
meaning of our Masonic values, such as brotherly love, relief, and truth. The
practice of our Masonic Values brightens our communities and makes them a
better place for everyone to live.
My theme for this year is, “Freemasonry, Values that Brighten our
Communities, Share the Light”. The symbol that I have selected to go with this
theme is the lighthouse. The lighthouse is a practical symbol of both good and
imparting knowledge. A lighthouse provides knowledge to the ships at sea that
helps them to navigate dangerous waters and reach safe harbor, which is a good
thing for the communities that they service. It takes vigilance and dedication by
lighthouse keepers to make sure that the light shines bright and properly, so that
ships can easily see it and recognize which lighthouse they are seeing, so they
can plot the proper course.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

81

We as individuals and particularly as Masons are reminded by the
symbol of the lighthouse and its keeper that each of us must be ever vigilant and
dedicated in our daily lives to make sure that we always act in keeping with our
values, so that our individual lights shine bright and no one has any doubt that
we are just and upright Masons upon whose fidelity you can rely. The practice
of our values not only does good in our communities, it teaches the value of right
living, which does far greater good for our communities, making them a brighter
place for everyone to live.
The practice of our values in our daily lives and more importantly in
our Communities as Masons is the first design that I, as Grand Master, have
drawn upon the trestle board for the craft to follow in the pursuit of their labors.
This instruction, like the ones that will follow, are the result of the planning
efforts by your Executive Committee. We work together as a Team so that each
year we build upon the efforts of those who preceded us towards a common goal,
instead of changing course every year. I encourage each of our lodges to follow
this example and develop a consensus in your lodge on what the lodge needs to
do over the next few years and then jointly develop plans to reach those goals.
The development of a building blueprint that can be followed by different
generations of leaders is another lesson that our ancient brothers, the Cathedral
Builders, have taught us and we are wise to follow their example.
This year Grand Lodge will continue to emphasize Masonic Education,
Leadership Development, Membership Retention, Lodge Development, Quality
Ritual, and Masonic Philanthropy.
Masonic Education is an essential tool for lodges to use to make a man
a Mason and for individual Masons to use in their pursuit of life long selfimprovement. Grand Lodge has prepared a series of articles for distribution to
the lodges for Masonic Education. These articles will be distributed each month
starting in December for the Masters to use next year in their lodges. We are
also encouraging increased Masonic Studies by our members on the values,
history, and meanings of the symbols of Freemasonry. We are also asking all of
the organizations in the Family of Freemasonry in California to submit articles to
Grand Lodge for consideration for distribution to the lodges for their use as
Masonic Education. We will also publish the best of these articles in the
California Freemason. Grand Lodge will also study ways to improve Masonic
Education in the lodges including the concept of a Lodge Education Officer.
If our fraternity is to last another 155 years in California, we will need
to continue to develop new leaders. Grand Lodge is looking at ways to increase
attendance at our leadership workshops, which have been so well received
around the state. We will also look for ways to expand the availability of the
Lodge Management Certification Program to more members around the state.
We are studying the use of online training courses as one possible method to
making these valuable programs more accessible to the craft. We will review
and update the Grand Lodge Strategic Plan. We will take another look at the

82

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

structure of Grand Lodge to ensure that we are operating as effectively and as
efficiently as we can. The Executive Committee Leadership Team will also be
working on ideas being considered for implementation over the next few years,
so that the Deputy Grand Master and Grand Wardens have the information
necessary for them to plan for the future of our craft.
Membership is the foundation of our fraternity. Our lodges must be
relevant to their members and to their communities to be successful. Grand
Lodge will make available to our lodges, successful programs that they can use
for such events as Constitutional Observance, Public Schools to name a few. We
will continue to encourage our lodges’ involvement in their communities. Grand
Lodge will continue our efforts to reach out to the many cultures that make up
our great state. If our lodges are to prosper, they will need to reach out to their
current members and meet their needs before they will be able to attract new
members.
The number of members in California continues to decline due to the
large number of members made after the Second World War. In 1965, when we
had a quarter of a million members in California, our members represented one
percent of the male population in California. Today we have a little over 71,000
members, which is about one tenth of one percent of the male population in
California. I know that Freemasonry is not for everyone, but I believe that at
least one percent of the male population in California are good men that we
would be proud to call a brother. If our membership were one percent of the
male population, today we would have over 350,000 members in California.
Imagine what good we could do with that many members.
Grand Lodge has looked at the membership trends and if we continue
on our current path, we will have only 35,000 members in 10 years. Our per
captia will be at least three times what it is today. To stop the decline and hold
our membership at 71,000, every lodge in California will need to raise 11 new
Master Masons each year for the next 10 years. I challenge every lodge to do its
part to meet this goal. Grand Lodge will try to help each of the lodges reach this
goal with the Pass-It-On Program. Only 29 percent of our lodges currently use
it. If we are going to reach the goal, we will need more lodges to participate.
Holding our membership at its current level can be done if we all work together.
While the creating of new members is the business of the individual
lodges, it is the business of Grand Lodge to make new lodges. Grand Lodge will
reach out to communities where there is no Masonic presence and try to establish
a Masonic Club with the express desire to charter a new lodge in that
community. We will continue to encourage the formation of more lodges in
foreign languages and will continue to encourage the diversity of our lodges’
membership. We are working with the Family of Freemasonry in California in
this effort to spread the light of Freemasonry.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

83

The foundation of our fraternity is the quality of our ritual. When it is
done well, it is inspiring, moving and teaches the significance of our core values.
Grand Lodge is committed to improving the quality of our ritual work in every
lodge in California. We are studying the idea used in many other Grand Lodges,
of developing multi media aids to be used during the lectures of our degrees.
These aids, like the trace boards of old, will add significant meaning and
understanding to our candidates. This will also allow us to add back to the
lectures much of the material that has been removed over the years and placed in
the monitor. We are carefully examining the experience of the other Grand
Lodges, including the State of Washington who has developed an excellent multi
media aid.
Grand Lodge will be trying the idea of ritual competitions in the state,
where the best ritualist will be recognized for their contribution to our craft at
Grand Lodge. Grand Lodge continues to hear how hard it is to find members
who can lead our lodges and can qualify to do the ritual work. This year, we will
study the idea of qualifying a lodge to do the degree work and qualify its
principle officers to open lodge, conduct its business, and close lodge. The
concept is that each year the lodge will have to demonstrate its ability to
properly conduct each of the degrees, using members of the lodge, in order for
the Inspector to qualify the lodge. The Master and Wardens will have to qualify
by demonstrating their ability to open a lodge in each of the degrees, conduct its
business and close the lodge in due form. It still would be preferred that the
Master and Wardens also perform their parts in each of the degrees, but it would
not be required. This is an interesting concept that requires extensive study this
year.
The past few years have seen us make great strides in the area of
Masonic Philanthropy. Last year, our Masonic charities were responsible for
improving and even saving, the lives of many of the most vulnerable among us.
This year we also have the opportunity to invest in important leadership and
education programs. We need to build on this success. Not only are more of our
brethren making meaningful gifts to our Homes and Foundation, but we also
have witnessed a culture develop whereby philanthropy is valued and cherished
as one of our important priorities. We need more participation by our members
and participation to a greater degree, if our charitable work is to continue for
generations to come. Your gifts make a huge difference to the craft. Continue to
spread the light of Freemasonry through your generous support of our
philanthropic programs.
This year my Grand Master’s Charity Project will benefit the Traditions
Unit’s garden at Union City. The traditions unit provides quality care for our
residents who are dealing with Alzheimer and dementia. This year we are
seeking to enhance the dementia program by upgrading and modifying the
existing wandering garden adjacent to Traditions and Lorber Skilled Nursing
Facility. The Garden is a soothing and familiar setting for our residents, but the

84

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

weather is often too cold or windy for the residents to be able to enjoy. The
improvements we hope to make include:
•

Construction of a 30-foot diameter yurt as the centerpiece of the
project;

•

Enhancement of the existing landscaping and repair of the existing
fountain to meet the code requirements for an area with dementia
patients;

•

Provide wheelchair access throughout the garden;

•

Add wheelchair accessible potting gardens/outdoor lighting;

The “Yurt” is a circular structure topped with a squat, conical roof,
originated with the nomads who once wandered Mongolia’s frozen wind-swept
plateaus in great numbers. Architectural Digest praises the yurt as an
“architectural wonder.”
The yurt will allow all the residents of the Home to use the garden in all
but the most inclement weather. We will also be putting together memory
boards, made up of everyday items that the residents touch and feel. The power
of touch brings out memories that have been locked away by dementia. This is a
project near to my heart, as I have seen too many family members, friends, and
brothers who have been struck down by dementia. There is a display in the
lobby of the auditorium that provides more information about our project.
Finally, this weekend, your Grand Lodge Leadership Team met with
many of the leaders of our concordant and appendant bodies, including the youth
groups that make up the Family of Freemasonry in California. We discussed
ways for each of us to combine our efforts in areas of leadership training,
membership development, and Masonic Education. The meeting was well
received and was a good first step in trying to bring the Family of Freemasonry
closer together in California. We will be getting back together again later this
year to continue this important effort.
Just like the lighthouse keeper, each of us, has a great deal of work to
do to keep our individual lighthouses shining brightly. Working together we will
complete the tasks before us and make our great fraternity stronger and more
relevant in our great state. By doing so, our Masonic Lighthouse will shine so
bright that our Masonic Values will have such a positive impact that not just our
communities or our state, but the entire country will be a better place for
everyone to live in. Freemasonry, values that brighten our communities, Share
the light and make our great nation a better place for everyone to live.
Thank you.

APPENDIX C

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

87

REPORT OF THE SENIOR GRAND WARDEN
Per Capita Recommendation for 2005-2006
Frederick L. Sorsabal, Senior Grand Warden
(As mailed to the Secretaries of the Lodges in a
Memorandum dated August 24, 2004)
As each of you knows, every year the Senior Grand Warden
recommends a per capita that will fund the Grand Lodge Budget for the year that
he will be Grand Master. After a great deal of work and forecasting, the Grand
Lodge Staff, Grand Treasurer and I have projected expenditures almost two
years in advance. We have accomplished this task in order to present to you a
per capita which realistically represents the cost of funding crucial programs for
Grand Lodge and its constituent Lodges for 2005-2006. The per capita
represents our best estimates on the number of members that we will have by that
time and also the degree of inflation. Per capita will continue to increase as we
see the decline in our membership along with the increasing expenses for
insurance, both for liability and workers’ compensation insurance premiums,
provided to every Lodge in this jurisdiction. Both of these items have a major
impact on our budget.
Much work has been accomplished by the Executive Committee over
the last several years to reduce expenses and continue to find the financial
resources to fund those programs that are so important to the continuation of
Masonry in California and to attract and retain the quality members that are
needed for the future. The per capita recommendation will continue to support
programs that meet the Vision and Strategic Plan of Grand Lodge.
The three areas of emphasis will continue to be Masonic Education,
Membership Development and Leadership Development. These programs have
seen some successes and we need to continue to fund them to further enhance
our Craft. Grand Lodge is not attempting to do the Lodges’ work; instead we are
endeavoring to provide support through an increased leadership training program
along with quality materials for the Lodges’ Masonic Education Program and for
their membership development. Therefore, the recommendation for the per
capita to be collected in July 2005 is twenty-six dollars ($26.00), an increase of
one dollar ($1.00) over the current year.
I am attaching, for your information and review, charts showing how
the per capita will be spent. As you can see, approximately two-thirds of it is
designated to be spent on services to Members, Lodges and Inspectors.
My Brothers, we are at a point where we need the Craft to invest in the
future of our beloved Fraternity in California. Remember that nothing will get
better without our actions to support our will to improve. Our values and the
lessons we teach are needed more in our communities today than ever before.

88

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

We can only let the world know what they are if we practice outside our Lodge
Rooms those lessons taught in it.
UNITED WE STAND, TOGETHER WE CAN MAKE A DIFFERENCE!
