REPORT OF THE GRAND TREASURER
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
As has been the long-established custom, the Grand Treasurer’s Report
to the Grand Lodge will be brief.
This report can be brief, and limited to an overview, as the annual
audited financial statements for the Grand Lodge of California and its entities
(Masonic Homes of California, California Masonic Foundation California
Masonic Memorial Temple, Nob Hill Masonic Management and the Retirement
Plan) and all related trust funds and special accounts are published in their
entirety in the Proceedings.
As Masonic leaders, it is one of our responsibilities and duties to take
the time to review these financial statements and become familiar with the fiscal
operations of this, our Grand Lodge. As you examine the financial statements, I
am confident that you will agree with me that your Grand Lodge is fiscally
sound, well managed and a source of both pride and confidence – pride in the
fact that the Masons of California have, over the decades, met every financial
challenge, time and again, through generous and committed financial support
and thereby demonstrated confidence in the future of this truly great fraternal
and charitable organization.
This is my second annual report to you as your Grand Treasurer. Last
year’s report highlighted seven areas of significant effort and related progress in
the continued effort to strengthen and improve both overall financial function
and cost-efficiency, as well as maximize transparency and accountability.
It is, therefore, appropriate to take a few minutes to report on the major
areas of special effort and progress made during this past year.
The previously established goal for centralization of all financial
functions is being implemented as staff time and funding permits. Currently, we
are working to:
•

establish and implement financial policies, processes and
procedures;

•

enhance internal controls and reconfigure staff to include a
controller position to broaden financial expertise and skills;

•

establish efficient use of resources as our constant goal and
objective;

•

implement uniform budget preparation, monitoring and reporting;

98

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

•

ensure comprehensive investment portfolio management,
monitoring and reporting for Grand Lodge and created an
investment program for the lodges;

•

provide support for and implementation for a comprehensive
evaluation and implementation of improved insurance coverage for
the Grand Lodge and its entities, with special emphasis on Hall
Associations;

•

re-evaluate banking structure and services to better meet our
organizational needs and modify process and procedures, as
required;

•

assist the Temple, following the sale of the Pine Street properties,
to create an endowment fund to support building maintenance and
enhancement;

•

implement financial management reporting to support the expanded
role of the Foundation, Temple and Nob Hill;

•

assist all Grand Lodge entities in the development of long-range
financial plans, similar to the four-year budget projections of Grand
Lodge;

•

and, finally, undertake an intensive workflow study to maximize
computerization and improve staff assignments to ensure
efficiency, transparency and full accountability.

As this list confirms, 2003/2004 fiscal year has been a challenging,
exciting and productive year for your Grand Treasurer, Director of Finance,
Mark Koenig, and the entire financial services staff. In support of our efforts,
we acknowledge the continuing contribution of the dedicated members of the
Grand Lodge committees on investment, insurance and finance. Their
professional expertise and commitment to our Grand Lodge is without equal.
We owe them, as well as our Grand Lodge office staff under the leadership of
our Grand Secretary, John L. Cooper III, a continuing debt of gratitude and
appreciation.
With confidence, I can assure you that everyone on the financial staff
looks forward to another year of service to this great fraternity.
Respectfully submitted,
Warren J. Blomseth, PGM, Grand Treasurer

101

REPORT OF THE GRAND SECRETARY
Most Worshipful Grand Masater, and Brethren. This is my thirteenth
report to you as Grand Secretary. It is a pleasure to have served each Grand
Master, in turn, as his Grand Secretary, and to have served you as your Grand
Secretary through the years. This brief report will only highlight some of the
activities of your Grand Lodge Office this year – and a more complete report
will be found in the Proceedings of this Annual Communication.
First, this year saw a significant step forward in our ability to serve the
secretaries of our lodges. The new iMember System was developed, and is now
about to become a reality for our lodges. This web-based system of membership
administration will allow our secretaries to work directly in a shared membership
database through the Internet. No longer will there be a need to have a lodge
membership database and a Grand Ldoge membership database. For the first
time in the history of our Grand Lodge we will have one membership database –
shared and used by our lodge secretaries and by our Grand Lodge staff. The
accuracy and efficiency that this will create means that we can both spend much
more time on things of greater importance than merely copying information from
one location to another. Our secretaries are looking forward to the launching of
this new system in the next Grand Lodge Year, and your Grand Lodge Office is
looking forward to it as well.
Secondly, the Grand Secretary assumed new responsibilities this year
with the passage of Grand Master Whitaker’s Recommendation No. 2 in 2002.
This restructuring of Grand Lodge committees took effect at the close of Grand
Lodge in 2003, and transferred to the Grand Secretary responsibilities heretofore
handled by the Charters Committee and the Masonic Service Bureaus
Committee. My office is now responsible for handling all ldoge consolidations,
and for monitoring the Masonic Service Bureaus in our jurisdiction. In both
these endeavors I had significant assistance from lodge secretaries, from
inspectors, and – in the case of the Masonic Service Bureaus – from the
executive secretaries of the several bureaus. However, I would like to pay
special tribute to one individual whose volunteer service to our Grand Lodge
made this a much easier task than it could have been otherwise. Worshipful Bro.
Albert Donnici, a past master of Alameda Lodge No. 167, a former inspector,
and a former Assistant Grand Tiler, worked closely with me to ensure that these
new duties were discharged with care and devotion. Ours is a volunteer
organization, and we could not function without all the dedicated volunteers that
we hav at the lodge level, and at the Grand Lodge level. I want to thank my
volunteer assistant, Worshipful Bro. Al Donnici, for his service to you, my
brethren, at your Grand Lodge Office!
There is another volunteer that deserves special thanks for his many
years of dedicated service to our lodges and to our Grand Lodge. Worshipful

102

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Bro. Norm Schultz is the author and owner of LodgeiCal – a local lodge
membership management program which has serviced our lodges through
fourteen editions since the mid 1980’s. However, it was as Webmaster for the
Grand Lodge Website – Freemason.org – that he contributed significantly to our
Grand Lodge as well. He took over this responsibility some seven years ago,
and until the sie was automated this past summer, handled a multitude of
problems daily, and kept our website up and functioning. While “thanks” is an
insufficient word to convey our true feelings, I know that I speak for all of us
when I express our deep and abiding appreciation to Bro. Norm for all that he
has done, and continues to do, for our Grand Lodge.
More will be published in the complete report from my office, but it is
now my duty to present certain business items which must now be brought
before you. The first of these is the recognition of two Grand Lodges. It is my
responsibility as Grand Secretary to keep the Grand Master informed on matters
pertaining to the recognition of other Grand Lodges, and to present to you in the
form of a motion the recognition of new Grand Lodges. There will be two
motions this year. The first one represents recognitions supported by the
Commission on Information for Recognition of the Conference of Grand Masters
of Masons in North America:
Most Worshipful, I move that recognition be extended to:
•
•

The Grand Lodge of Armenia
The Grand Lodge of San Marino

Motion Adopted.
Our next motion will deal with recognition of Prince Hall Grand
Lodges in the United States which have already been recognized by the Grand
Lodge in that jurisdiction which is presently recognized by the Grand Lodge of
California. Our law requires that the recognition of any other Grand Lodge in a
jurisdiction in the United States where there is a Grand Lodge already
recognized by us be contingent upon the current Grand Lodge granting its
permission to do so. The motion will address this issue. In addition, it is
important that any other Prince Hall Grand Lodge recognized by our Grand
Lodge be one which is in amity with the Prince Hall Grand Lodge of California.
The motion will therefore address both these issues. Before making this motion,
it is important to note that the Grand Lodge of California already recognizes
these Prince Hall Grand Lodges:
•
•
•
•

The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge of California, Inc.
The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Oregon
The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Hawaii
The Most Worshipful Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Pennsylvania

In making this motion I will only list the state where the Prince Hall
Grand Lodge mentioned is primarily located. In the interest of time I will not
read the full name of the Prince Hall Grand Lodge.
Most Worshipful, I move that the Grand Lodge of California recognize
the following Prince Hall Grand Lodges:
1. Alaska

And that this recognition be contingent upon receiving in writing the

Grand Lodge of California, a confirmation by the Most Worshipful Prince Hall
Grand Lodge of California, Inc., that these Prince Hall Grand Lodges are
recognized by them, and lastly, that the Prince Hall Garnd Lodge concerned
wishes to enter into mutual recognition with the Grand Lodge of California.
Motion Adopted.
This year Grand Master Kirkpatrick issued dispensations to four new
lodges in our jurisdiction. Having reviewed the progress of these four lodges, he
determined that two should be recommended to receive charters at this Annual
Communication, and that the two others should continue to work under

104

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

dispensation until qualified to receive a recommendation from the Grand Master
for a charter. The two lodges which received dispensations, and which he
believes should work a further time under dispensation are:
•

Panamericana Lodge UD, and
Ararat Lodge UD

Both these lodges were Instituted by the Grand Master of September 8,
2004. The first, Panamericana Lodge UD, has worked for some time as a club,
and expects to work in Spanish. The second lodge, Ararat Lodge UD, expects to
work in Armenian.
Lux Lodge UD was Instituted by the Grand Master on July 26, 2004, in
San Diego, and Academia Lodge UD was Instituted by the Grand Master on
August 22, 2004, in Oakland. Both lodges have satisfied the requirements of the
California Masonic Code pertaining to new ldoges, and having done so, it is the
recommendation of the Grand Master that charters be authorized and issued by
this Grand Lodge. Accordingly,
Most Worshipful, I move that a charter be granted to Lux Lodge UD, to
be known as Lux Lodge No. 846, and to Academia Lodge UD, to be known as
Academia Lodge No. 847.
Motion Adopted.
Finally, my brethren, one lodge, Masonic Origins Research Lodge, at
Clovis, has ceased to work for at least a year, and upon due investigation, it has
been recommended to the Grand Master that the charter of this lodge be revoked
by Grand Lodge. I therefore move that the charter of Masonic Origins Research
Lodge be revoked by action of Grand Lodge in Annual Communication
assembled.
Motion Adopted.
Most Worshipful, this concludes my report as Grand Secretary.
Respectfully submitted,
John L. Cooper III, Grand Secretary

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Lodge Consolidations
All requirements of the law with respect to consolidation of Lodges
having been complied with, authorization is requested to properly endorse the
charters of these Lodges as follows:
Mission Bay Lodge No. 771 and Clairemont Mesa Lodge No. 799,
effective December 3, 2003, to be known as Old Glory Lodge No. 799.
Fruitvale Lodge No. 113 and Alcatraz Lodge No. 244, effective January
1, 2004, to be known as San Leandro Lodge No. 113.
Guadalupe Lodge No. 237 and Arroyo Lodge No. 274, effective
January 1, 2004, to be known as Central Coast Lodge No. 237.
Gardena Ascot Lodge No. 372 and Moneta Lodge No. 405, effective
April 1, 2004, to be known as Gardena Moneta Lodge No. 372.
Hope Lodge No. 234 and Grizzly Lodge No. 601, effective May 1,
2004, to be known as Feather River Lodge No. 234.
Warren G. Harding Lodge No. 579 and San Juan Lodge No. 732,
effective May 1, 2004, to be known as Harding San Juan Lodge No. 579.
West Covina-La Puente Lodge No. 446 and Baldwin Park Lodge No.
660, effective September 1, 2004, to be known as West Covina Lodge No. 446.
El Cajon Valley Lodge No. 576 and Lakeside Lodge No. 760, effective
September 1, 2004, to be known as Heartland Lodge No. 576.
Lebanon-Pacific Lodge No. 136 and Starr King Lodge No. 344,
effective October 1, 2004, to be known as Pacific-Starr King Lodge No. 136.

APPENDIX F

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

123

REPORT OF THE GRAND LECTURER
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
Brethren, as this Masonic year nears its conclusion. On behalf of your
entire Ritual Committee, I express our collective gratitude for the opportunity to
serve this Grand Lodge.
The following report respecting ritual and Lodge conditions is offered
for your consideration.
Lodge Consolidations
Again this past year, some Lodges have consolidated for the usual
reasons. Many members seem to have forgotten some of those valuable lessons,
whereby we are taught to support our Lodge. Attendance and participation with
the Lodge activities, such as committees, greeters at meetings, officers or just
those being willing to render a helping had where and when there is need, was
sorely lacking. Perhaps each of us should look in a mirror and ask ourselves,
“Am I putting forth my best efforts to support my Lodge?” Most consolidations
seem not to be prompted because of financial positions, but rather member
participation. The down side is always that a consolidation could remove the
existence of Masonic identity in a given community and could ultimately create a
loss of members. Geographical location, along with member harmony and
participation should be the highest priority.
Inspector Conferences
Jurisdiction review has continued with two Inspectoral Ritual
Conferences, and two Inspector Administrative Conferences, one each in the
North and South.
Each Division scheduled two (2) Ritual Seminars for Officers Coaches
and two (2) Ritual Seminars for Master Masons. These Seminars were in
addition to the regularly scheduled Division Inspectors Schools of Instruction
that are conducted by each respective Assistant Grand Lecturer. Your
attendance and enthusiastic participation makes our efforts more meaningful.
We continue to strive to improve our instructional methods of what and how our
ritual is reviewed, delivered and presented. Future plans are being considered to
place some of the Ritual Committee schedule on the Grand Lodge website, so
more members may be better informed and can plan to attend an event at a
location near them.

124

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

California Cipher Ritual Supplement
The completion of a “Ritual Manual” identified as “California Cipher
Ritual Supplement – Version 1.03” was completed and is available in the Grand
Lodge Supply Rom. It is being used by many of our Lodges. Your Ritual
Committee has received many favorable comments, from those who are using it
and have received some suggestions for future implementation when the next
version can be considered and published.
One Day Conferral Demonstrations
During the 2003 Annual Communication of our Grand Lodge, we heard
discussion about Grand Master Holsinger’s Recommendation No. 2003-01
relative to “One Day Conferrals”. Subsequent to his election, Most Worshipful
Howard Kirkpatrick directed the Ritual Committee to prepare a script that would
demonstrate how all three Degrees of Masonry could be conferred in one day
and remain consistent with the proposed recommendation. The Committee
accomplished this task mainly through the tireless efforts of Worshipful Jack
Rose, Assistant Grand Lecturer of Division IV, who did a most commendable
job of drafting the script with the Committee’s critique.
Five ritual
demonstrations were conducted throughout the jurisdiction at Longh Beach,
Fresno, San Diego, San Francisco and Elk rove. I hope that you availed yourself
of the opportunity to attend one of these.
Thank You
The task to maintain ritual uniformity is shared by many. To may
colleagues, the Assistant Grand Lecturers, what a great group of Masons to work
with and my sincere appreciation is extended to each of them for their dedication
and understanding. Much gratitude is extended to all the Inspectors, each having
many challenges, some of which occur without any notice, yet they continue on
with their assignments. Let us not forget those Officers’ Coaches and Lodge
Officers who put forth their best efforts and strive to confer the best degrees
possible.
The Grand Secretary and his staff have always been available to assist
when needed and they are greatly appreciated.
Lastly, I offer my sincere appreciation to Most Worshipful Howard
Kirkpatrick and his wife, Jackie, to have had the opportunity to serve in his
Grand Lodge family. The memories will remain forever and I “Thank You”.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

125

To you, my Brethren, my sincere thanks for the opportunity to serve
you as your Grand Lecturer.
Respectfully submitted.
Harold A. Macy, Grand Lecturer

GRAND CHAPLAIN’S MESSAGE
by The Very Reverend Raymond E. Davies
Invocation – Sunday, October 10, 2004
Great Architect of the Universe, our Father and our God; we have
th
assembled this afternoon for the Public Opening of the 155 Communication of
this Grand Lodge, we humbly acknowledge Your comforting Presence; and we
ask that Your Presence provide guiding influence to us all during this
Communication.
We thank You for bringing us here safely.
Grant Thy special blessing on all who participate today; our Grand
Master, the Grand Officers, the adult leaders of our Concordant Bodies, and
especially the young leaders of our youth groups. We particularly thank You for
Your unceasing care as You watch over the young people of our Family
Fraternity. We thank You for bestowing them upon us to be our future. Grant
that we older members shall be correct examples for them; Give us the wisdom
to understand their needs and the strength to seek to help them as much as we are
able. May these young leaders reflect Your guidance on as they assume positions
of greater leadership in the future.
We know that You will comfort and sustain those who are unable to
attend this Communication.
And, we thank You for giving wisdom and strength to all who serve to
protect us.
May we always remember it is our duty to serve Thee through serving
our families, our Faiths, our communities, and our nation.
We ask all this in Thy Holy Name.
Amen

130

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Inspirational Message – Sunday, October 10, 2004
Public Opening of the
th
155 Annual Communication
of the Grand Lodge of California F&AM
A hymn performed by Brother Christopher Morris – Assistant Grand
Organist will precede this message.
Personal Responsibility
A Lakota Sioux Shaman, a Holy Man named Little Crow, was teaching
his grandchildren about life. He sat with them one day and said to them: "A fight
is going on inside me, it is a terrible fight and it is between two wolves. One
wolf is evil--he is fear, anger, envy, sorrow, regret, greed, arrogance, self-pity,
guilt, resentment, lies, false pride, inferiority, superiority and ego. The other
wolf is good--he is joy, peace, love, hope, sharing, serenity, humility, kindness,
benevolence, friendship, empathy, generosity, compassion, faith, and truth.” He
told his grandchildren: “This same fight is going on inside you and inside every
other person, also."
They thought about it for a minute and then one child slowly turned to
the grandfather and asked: "Which wolf will win?"
The wise old Shaman simply replied, "The one you feed."
~
Let us pray;
Creator of us all, as we begin this time of seeking inspiration and
expressing gratitude for Your Guidance, help us to feel Your always present
Spirit. Open our hearts and minds to absorb the whispers of Your voice, and
grant that we may seek to be of greater service for Thee.
Amen
~
Freemasonry, particularly Freemasonry in California, is a “religious
institution”. The great Masonic writer, MacKay, in many of his explanations of
our Fraternity, used these very words. Many who attack this Fraternity use these
very words to justify their claim that we are actually a church, leading our
members away from traditional organized faiths. Some, ignorant of the Truth,
accuse us of being even worse.
We must make the effort to clear those misunderstandings. We must
reinforce our individual expressions and practice of our own particular Faiths.
Thus, by our actions, put this organization in the correct light of what we are as a
Fraternity and what our purpose is; and in the process, restore ourselves to our
true relationship to God and His Word.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

131

Getting back to that “religious institution” label, we are an “institution”.
The legal and correct definition of an institution is: “An establishment, especially
one of charitable or public character, or one affecting the community. An
established or organized society or corporation.”
This Fraternity certainly fits that definition.
We are religious in character. We require a belief of the Supreme
Being. We ask for that Being’s strength and guidance before we begin anything.
At all of our gatherings there are Holy Writings present, and we quote from
those Holy Writings and in all our ceremonies we teach from them what we are
caused to believe is the correct form of personal conduct.
In combination, that makes us a “religious institution”.
It does not make us an organized Faith.
It does not make us a religion!
Nor does it make us a church!
Nor is attending our meetings a substitute for attending church!
In fact, it is one of the responsibilities of every member of this
Fraternity to regularly attend and be an active working member of their own
particular house of worship. Even more than that, it is the responsibility of each
member of this Fraternity to display their individual Faith in everything they do
in life.
That being said regarding our individual expressions of Faith, as the
Family of Freemasonry, what is our overall responsibility to God?
We sometimes get distracted from this question. We are attacked
because we don’t embrace the doctrine of any one particular organized faith or
another. We are criticized when we devote our efforts to doing good things for
our communities, because it is contrary to some organized faiths’ beliefs of how
to get to heaven. We are objected to by some organized faiths because we take
time away from what we as parishioners would devote to promoting that
particular religious group.
And the criticism goes on, and on…
A few years ago, when still immersed in the daily business world, my
manner of earning my daily bread was as a Manufacturing Engineer, constantly
attempting to find better ways to make things and solve manufacturing problems.
We had a sign posted in our office that read:
“When you are up to your knees in alligators, it is difficult to remember
that your objective is to drain the swamp.”
Basically, what it means is that when dealing with a multitude of
irritations, it is difficult to stay focused on the real job.

132

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

We of the Family of Freemasonry have been dealing with a multitude of
irritations, and many of us have been pushed into a defensive position so often
that we are no longer focused on what our task is relative to the expression of
our own individual Faiths. And, sometimes we are so busy following the
instructions of how to insure our own individual positive relationship with God
that we take our eyes off of what we are supposed to do for each other, which is
the relationship that God says is important. Please understand that only four of
the Ten Commandments deal with our relationship with God, the other six relate
to with how we are to treat with each other.
We have a responsibility to help each other, and give of ourselves for
the rest of the people in our communities.
And that is a significant part of our relationship with God. It doesn’t
require defending, it requires doing.
Good works and good relationships and displaying our individual Faith
are not only important, they become our duties if we really have a relationship
with God.
No matter what your individual Faith is, that is a true statement.
Good works and good relationships and displays of Faith are important.
One of the more profound statements of the Judeo-Christian faith is the
statement from Leviticus Chapter 19 Verse 18: “..Love thy neighbor as thyself..”
It doesn’t say Christian neighbor, or Jewish neighbor, or Arab neighbor, or
brown neighbor, or white neighbor or any other definition of neighbor. Just
neighbor. One of this Fraternity’s primary tenets is that we are all children of one
Almighty Parent, and as such, we must practice universal Brotherly Love for the
rest of the citizens of our communities.
I think we are all familiar with the phrase “Brotherly Love”, or “Sisterly
Love”.
It is what unites us as within this Family of Freemasonry, and it is the
one philosophy is most desperately needed by society today.
As the previous quote illustrates, in the Tanach, the Book of Leviticus
is full of instructions relating to our conduct relative to each other and the rest of
the people in our communities.
Are Good Works important? Do they have any meaning in our
relationship with God?
In the New Testament Book of Mathew, it is recorded in the Sermon on
the Mount: “Let your light so shine before men that they may see your good
works and glorify your Father which is in Heaven”. We should be doing good
works every day that promote the relationship with each other that God has
instructed us to practice.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

133

In the Quoran, in the Chapter of Hud, it is written: “But as for those
who believe and do good works, and humble themselves before their Lord, they
shall be the inhabitants of Paradise”.
Also from the New Testament Book of Revelations, we find in Chapter
14, Verse 13: “……Blessed are the dead which die in the Lord from Henceforth:
…that they may rest from their labors; and their works do follow them.”
Similar passages are found in almost every organized faith’s Holy
Writings. From the Tripitaka, to the Analects, to the Vedas, to the Zend Avesta,
all speak of good works.
To denote Good Works, our Fraternity uses the word “Relief”.
There are many organized religions that try to impress the Believer that
the only way to Heaven is through a personal relationship with God or His
manifestation on Earth. And they sometimes tell you that works don’t even
matter.
I won’t stand here today and tell you that “good works” will get
someone to a place of eternal bliss. What I will stand here and tell you is that not
doing “good works” may affect someone’s admission. It may be an indication
that they are not letting God work through them.
Now, what about Truth.
The Truth is you can, and must, display your faith whenever and
however you choose. We have a group of Judges in our Supreme Court that has
said there must be a separation of Church and State. They have said that there
may not be a scheduled time of prayer at any public event supported by tax
dollars or within any structure supported by tax dollars. You and I are educated,
we understand our own language, and we can read. The key words to remember
are “scheduled” or “sponsored”. The Constitution may forbid government
sponsored prayer, but it also forbids anyone from stopping you or I from the free
exercise of our individual faith. Anywhere, anytime, in any manner befitting our
faith that does not force others to join with us. On the street, in our stores, offices
and factories where we earn our daily bread, in the halls of Congress, before we
begin a test in school, in the very Supreme Court Chamber as we prepare to
speak. Anywhere, anytime, in any manner befitting our faith that is not scheduled
by any tax supported entity, and does not force others to join with us. And
hearing and seeing us is not forcing them to join with us.
The Constitution clearly states there may not be any law prohibiting the
free exercise of our faith. It is our own fear of that stops us from assuming an
attitude of prayer and thanking God for his bounty before we partake of a meal
in a restaurant. It is our own fear of appearing weird that stops us if we start to
whisper a short prayer asking guidance for our day as we turn on our office
computer and wait for it to boot up. It is our own fear that stops us, not the
Supreme Court. We must discard that fear, both as citizens, and as members of

134

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

the Family of Freemasonry. If a professional athlete can openly thank God for
being granted the skills to stroke a home run or score a touchdown or win a race,
why can’t we all do the same for our accomplishments in our individual lives?
Our contributions to society are no less important, and our openly expressing our
individual Faith will surely glorify God.
There is a parable in the New Testament of the King James Version of
Holy Writings that deals with three servants receiving assets from their
employer, for them to watch over while he was on a business trip. It is recorded
that during his absence, two of the servants invested the assets so that they
generated income from what they had been entrusted with. The third timidly
stored the asset in his keeping in a safe place, and returned it to the employer
upon his return, pointing out how he had stored it in a safe place and had
personally dedicated his efforts as custodian of the asset, insuring its safety.
The employer rewarded the first two servants for their efforts. The third
was penalized for making no effort to generate an increase.
It might be said that it was rather harsh treatment. But let’s be truthful,
how many of us would put our monetary savings in a bank that paid no interest?
How many of us would transfer more of our assets into a financial institution that
didn’t provide dividends?
There is another passage in the same Holy Writings that refers to a
group that is being snubbed because it is “neither cold nor hot”, but by being
lukewarm is not accomplishing any substantial good works that promote the
spreading of the Creator’s Truth.
The messages of these passages should be clear.
First, as children of One Almighty Parent, much has been given to us,
but we are expected to use a portion from it to personally help our communities.
Secondly, no matter how much our Fraternity’s predecessors and we
have done in the past, it is not enough. We cannot now stop and rest on our
laurels and point out what a great job we did for God in the past. It is required
that we each continue to contribute to society until we draw our final breath.
And lastly, no matter how much we individually praise God and
individually submit ourselves to that Beneficent Being, our claim to God’s
blessing doesn’t pass examination if we aren’t actively serving our communities
in God’s name. We need to be up and out of the pews and spreading God’s
Word and letting God’s actions manifest through us. We must invest the promise
God gave to us and expect dividends for our Creator.
In the expression of our individual Faiths, it makes little difference what
Holy Writings are consulted, they all promise much, and all require something of
us. And this is Truth.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

135

Why would our Great Creator bless us, or our Fraternity if we are not
actively spreading His message throughout the world by our good works that
glorify Him?
We must set the example to love one another and our neighbors. We
must set the example to serve each other and our communities. And we must
demonstrate to the world that this is the Supreme Architect’s Truth.
This is our Fraternity’s responsibility.
This is our Fraternity’s strength.
This is the Personal Responsibility to God from each one of us.
(Duet: “Amazing Grace” (a capella) Gail Zacher & Marianna Zacher)
Let us pray;
Father of us all, as we draw to the close of this devotional pause, grant
that each one of us may continue to exercise our Personal Responsibility to You
by practicing Brotherly Love, dispensing Relief, and by these good works, grant
that we may each be brightly lit torches of Your Truth. And may it all serve to
Glorify Thee.
Amen

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

139

REPORT OF THE GRAND ORATOR
Most Worshipful Grand Master Howard D. Kirkpatrick, Past Grand
Masters, my fellow Grand Lodge officers, distinguished visitors, members of
Grand Lodge and I’m proud to say, my brothers:
On behalf of all the elected and appointed officers, I wish to thank you,
Grand Master Kirkpatrick, for your leadership, your vision of what California
Masonry can become, your unwavering dedication to the quality of our Lodges
and our members, but most of all, for your capacity to find the very best, in each
of us and maximize our individual contributions to our beloved Fraternity. It is
Leaders like you that build the foundation for the renaissance of Masonry.
I believe the greatest experience this year was traveling our State and
meeting some of the finest men and women in the world. In conjunction with the
studies conducted at Grand Lodge this year, we have been most fortunate to
better understand the dynamics of California Masonry, the fluctuations of
membership, our demographics and their movement. This coupled with your
request of the Craft to provide their vision of what Masonry in California could
look like in 10 and 20 years, inspired us to ask that same question and
contemplate the answers as we traveled.
The responses were energetic, exciting and very upbeat. We are now
seeing Lodges with a positive net gain of membership. Not brothers joining
multiple Lodges but new young men who are fascinated with and want to learn
the philosophies, principles and mysteries of Masonry. It appears as if there has
been a paradigm shift where camaraderie is the by-product of the teachings
rather than the reason for membership.
I would like to share, allegorically, some of the things I’ve heard as we
traveled.
th

My story starts at a 150 Lodge anniversary in a small town in northern
California. The Lodge had been there since the gold rush. It was typical of that
era, small in stature but filled with antiquities and historical documents. As was
the case with so many Lodges built in a time of all wood construction, this Lodge
had burned to the ground and was rebuilt more than once.
The celebration was first-rate and well attended by the members and
their families. It included the telling of the rich history of Masonry in the
community and how Brethren from back East were helped as they entered the
town without knowing anyone, almost destitute from the trip. They were given
clothes and food and guided to the most likely places to find Gold. The story
continued about how they helped each other build their businesses and homes
and reminded all of us that brotherly love is one of our most important tenets and
was alive and well back then.

140

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Shortly after dinner I went out to a small patio area for some fresh air. I
noticed a solitary old man sitting on a bench, smoking a roll your own and just
looking thoughtfully into the woods.
I said “Do you mind if I sit down?”
He said “No, you’re more than welcomed”.
We sat quietly for a few moments taking in the beauty of the area.
Then he said “You’re a visitor up here!”
“I am” I answered.
“What do you do for the Grand Lodge” he asked
“I’m the Orator” I responded.
He chuckled and said “We used to call them the Grand Lips”
I laughed and asked him if he had heard any Grand Orations.
He said “Oh yes, many of them, I’m a Past Master for many years”.
“By the way” I said “my name is George, what’s yours?”
“My name is Benjamin, but most folks around here call me Ben.”
“You know” he said. “I’ve been watching our Fraternity change and
search for answers for many years, and have seen it grow until we were bursting
at the seams, building Lodges as fast as we could and then shrink as our
members moved away or died. But one thing remained the same.”
“What was that?” I asked
“The answers to our many questions can be found in our Ritual and the
Obligations we took at the Altar. I don’t mean the Rod or Floor work; I mean the
real meaning behind the words... If you look at most every problem or situation
we have ever had, it was created by not remembering the lessons taught in the
Ritual, or not living out our Obligation. That Obligation we so fervently swore to
uphold at the Altar. Anything less, you know, is Non-Masonic conduct.”
I agreed that our Masonic teachings were the standard of our
performance and that we needed to hold ourselves accountable. Otherwise the
whisper of good council was needed.
This led me to another thought; “Ben if we believe the Ritual and
Obligation is the mainstay then aren’t we loosing our focus by lessening the
scope of the proficiencies?”
Ben took another puff on his smoke and said “What is most important is
what the Ritual says about not recommending anyone to Masonry unless we had
good reason to believe he would bring Honor to our Fraternity. The men that
participate in the Craft must pass the test of Honor. Our standards are not being

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

141

lowered. These men should already be Masons in their hearts and searching for
new light in their lives.
We have never been a fraternity based on paling around. Our
friendships come as a result of our teamwork and focused resolve. We know that
teamwork is the fuel that allows common people to attain uncommon results. We
are here to achieve personal growth and contribute to a greater purpose. Besides,
if the Lodges are relevant to our members then the new Masons will attend and
hear the Masonic lessons at each meeting. The Lodges do teach Masonic
Education, don’t they?”
I answered, “Some do better than others. It may be they don’t know
how to present it in an effective way.”
Ben replied; “But isn’t that what leadership in Masonry is all about?”
He leaned forward and asked “George what is more important than
understanding the philosophy and principles inculcated in Masonry?”
He continued “If the relevance of our fraternity is in the teachings and
the good work we do, don’t you think someone should follow up and know that
it’s really happening? If great and thoughtful programs are developed, shouldn’t
we follow up to see they have been implemented and are successful or need to be
modified? And if a Lodge develops a great program, would it make sense to
share it?”
I responded by saying “If Leadership and these programs are so critical
to the success of Masonry and the Lodges, perhaps we need professional training
in developing teamwork and leadership with committed, ongoing, training for the
Inspectors and the Masters. We would certainly be giving more Light to our
leaders that could be used in their Lodges, their business, as well as at home with
their families. We would be making good men better.”
“What a coincidence,” he said with a smile, “we would be building our
human asset, and spreading more light among our Brethren. Another thing, this
would mean the more capability and responsibility one was willing to accept, the
more professional training they would receive. Not a bad incentive program. It’s
similar to what other successful corporations do for their leaders.”
Ben went on “It would seem to me that if our future depended on
leadership, we would be prepared to fill our vacancies with the best possible
people to do all the things we’ve been talking about.”
“Sounds difficult” I said.
Ben responded with “It could even start today by each person
identifying their potential successor and their qualifications. That information
could then be put in the Successor Book. It seems to me it would be more
thoughtful and minimize selection by random acts of kindness or default. They

142

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

would be judged on whom can best work and best agree. Capability would be
the criteria.
“George”, Ben said, “we’re running a huge corporation and continuity
is a major concern. If we don’t address it, our stockholders, the Craft, get whip
sawed, which is a great motivational deterrent. That’s why we need trained
resource facilitators at the Inspector level. They could be the continuity. The
missing link might be integrating all the leadership with similar ideals, goals,
thinking systems and visions. And that doesn’t happen in one or two meetings a
year.”
I said, “Ben, you continue to give me tuff things to think about.”
“Now you’re getting it.” he said, “You’re getting pushed out of your
comfort zone and that’s where the growth starts”.
He continued “for Masonry to be successful we need a common
thinking process, not everyone thinking exactly alike, because that stifles
creativity, but rather a way of thinking that generates collectiveness and better
understanding. The Ritual and Obligations are pretty good at that. It just needs
extra care in getting the message across.”
I was stumped. “How do you do that?” I asked.
“Back to continuity in training and follow up” he said. “You start at the
top with the Executives and their staffs and progressively work through the
organization to the Inspectors and into the Lodges. The Inspectors would be
charged to facilitate the Lodges with assistance from the great programs that are
already in place, like the Wardens Retreats, the Management Seminars and
information from the Lodge Support Committee. Maybe,” he paused, “the
Inspectors School of Instructions can become a school to improve leadership
capability, thereby becoming the developmental tool for the officers and Lodges.
Do you know that some of our Lodge Masters today have only 3 or 4
years in our Fraternity?” He Said. “That’s barely enough time to know what we
do. And our mentors, they can’t support the way they used to because of their
age or infirmities. It takes us back to the need for Grand Lodge and the
Inspectors as a point of continuity and training.”
“Professional training? That kind of training is going to cost money” I
said.
Ben laughed, “We Masons talk about Millions or pennies. I’ve seen us
pass a resolution that commits thousands of dollars in a heart beat and argue and
grumble over a $5.00 dues increase. We need to invest in our future not scramble
and patch with Bands-aids. Once we start something like that, we must be
committed to seeing it through for years not just one term of a Grand Master.
You know,” he said “the best way to prepare for the future is to create it.”

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

“Speaking of dues,” he continued, “Do you know that the average
annual dues in California in 1900 were equivalent to $115.00 in today’s dollars?
Today the average is $50.00. Our most expensive dues, now being charged in
California is equivalent to a cup of coffee a week?
Do you know, that it is not unusual for a Rotarian or Kiwanis member
to budget $1000 to $1500 for their annual membership dues and expenses?”
“But Ben, they are designed to develop business contacts which are
very relevant to their well being.”
Ben said. “George, we do the same thing, coupled with a Moral Code
and Value System as well. Most Masons would rather do business with someone
who they know is on the level and has knelt at the same Altar and swore to
protect his Brother, rather than some stranger out of the phone book.
But getting back to dues, there are Lodges out there that expect their
dues to cover all the Lodge expenses, reserves, maintenance, fraternal
Obligation, leadership training and all their costs.”
I said “That’s an interesting concept. So what do they do with their
investments?”
“Well” Ben said “That’s the best part; the interest revenue can be used
for all the things that allow Masons to do what they could not do as individuals.
It can be used for Scholarships, essay contest, public school support, programs,
community outreach like meals for the less fortunate, but most importantly, to
invest in our future, our Masonic Youth Groups, the men and women of
tomorrow, our baby Brothers and Sisters. I’ve never quite understood why we
would not support our own youth groups before we supported outside
community youth groups.”
“Interesting point” I said.
He continued, “The dollars generated by investments allows us to live
out what we say we are. Our Lodge is the first point of support for our Brethren
and their families in need.”
I commented, “Our dues would be a lot more then they are now.”
“My goodness,” he said “so what if it cost a cup of coffee a day for the
philosophies and mysteries of the ages. Don’t forget what the other fraternal
organization pay. There is a reason for that. It’s needed if you want a quality
organization that trains and develops men to see more light. The California
Mason will grow in capability and better assist their families and their Lodge. It
will come back to us, just like it says in our teachings.
We sat on the patio as the moon crested the mountains and I finally
said.

“Ben, what are your thoughts about the relationship of Grand Lodge
and the Blue Lodges?”
George, I don’t think most Masons know what the Grand Lodge does
for them in particular, and Masonry in general. There is always a suspicion when
someone is asking for money or proposing a change. However, I think the Grand
Lodge is seeing the Lodges more and more as their customers, to provide and
serve that which would be impractical for one Lodge to provide for themselves.
The Grand Lodge has a roll to play as do the Lodges. One is no more important
than the other; they both have a job to do. An example of what the Grand Lodge
does is; providing continuity of the ritual, low insurance rates due to the
economy of size, the managing the Endowment so that it continues to grow for
the next generation, renting and care of our Grand Lodge building, Leadership
training, programs, Masonic Education, New Technology, Communications, the
Masonic Homes, intercourse with other jurisdictions, data and historical storage,
the Inspector Corps, Lodge counseling and the list goes on and on. Most Masons
don’t realize that the Grand Lodge has cut over a Million equivalent dollars out
of their budget in the last five years without lowering its standards. Some real
difficult calls were made, but they did it. So you see George, it’s much more than
what’s in the CMC, it’s running a major corporation.”
I paused and said “Ben, you mentioned relevance a few times, can you
give me an example?”
Sure George” he said, “We need to look at what Masonry was about
when it was at its strongest; when Lodges served an important function in
everyone’s life. One example is our flagship, the Masonic Homes. In the early
1900’s there was no Social Security, no government benefits to speak of. If I
died my widow was on her own in an environment that was not friendly to
women working. Her alternatives were bleak at best as were the children’s. If
there was not extended family, it was not that unusual for the children to go to a
state institution of questionable capability and performance. Masonry became
that extended family and we Masons built the Masonic Homes.
Masonry was very relevant, because men whom I trusted, swore to
make sure my family would be ok. That was many years ago. Now the
government has tried to do what we Masons did years before. Masonry was once
again on the forefront of social evolution as we have been for centuries.
He continued “I’ve tried to think of what we Masons could get behind
and provide now, that would again be on the leading edge while filling a critical
void; Something that would be relevant to our members and would be as unique
as the Masonic Homes were in the 1900’s. And I think I have come up with an
idea.”

By this time I was getting excited, what could be so impacting that it

a college education for what it costs to go to our Shrine Children Hospitals or

I didn’t believe what I heard; college for Free? “How could we do

“Just like we did with the Homes,” he said, “one bite of the elephant at

“Absolutely” He said “Their children and grandchildren benefit by their

than our own members and their children.” He continued. “It might have to start

What could be more comforting than to know that your children would

I told Ben that was a big chunk to think about. He laughed and

“Benjamin” I responded.

is what I have left for you today. A lot to think about!
Thank you Most Worshipful for allowing me to meet my personal
responsibility as a Mason for our beloved Fraternity.

EMERGENT RESOLUTION
Grand Master Kirkpatrick introduced the following Emergent
Resolution to the delegates. The delegates were instructed to vote on whether or
not the Emergent Resolution would be brought before the assembly for
consideration.
Resolution No. 04-06
Modifies Homes Admissions
The proponents of this Resolution seek to increase funding sources by expanding
the persons eligible for admission to the Homeas at the skilled nursing level to
include members of the general community.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
WHEREAS, our California Masonic Homes were created to help fulfill
our obligations as Masons to relieve distress and care for the aged, indigent or
infirm members of our fraternal family and the Trustees of the Masonic Homes
have been charged with the solemn obligation of making the best and highest use
of the resources entrusted to our care;
WHEREAS, the Masonic Homes of California is presently authorized
only to assist Master Masons from California lodges, their wives, widows, and –
in exceptional circumstances – mothers of Master Masons (in addition to
assisting children of Masons and others);
WHEREAS, the burden of caring for family members other than those
presently authorized may present a significant difficulty for a Master Mason, a
burden which the Masonic Homes of California can assist with through the
expansion of its admission criteria;
WHEREAS, the primary responsibility to provide relief to Master
Masons, their wives and widows rests with the Lodges, who are in no way
restricted with regard to whom they may provide fraternal support; and
WHEREAS, the Masonic Homes of California is able to accommodate
residents who do not meet the more restrictive requirements in our present code
without impairing its ability to discharge its primary responsibility to Master
Masons, their wives, widows and orphans and can do so without using resources
restricted to charitable support of Master Masons, their wives and widows.
NOW, THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED that Section 8010 of the
California Masonic Code be amended as marked:
§8010. PURPOSE.
The Board of Trustees of the Masonic Homes of California shall
control, conduct and manage homes, hospitals and other facilities for

150

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

the care, maintenance and support of the aged, indigent or infirm
Master Masons, their wives, widows and children, and schools for the
education of such children, and grant non-resident assistance instead of
admission to the Homes. The Board may in addition establish policies
that provide for admission to the Homes at the skilled nursing level of
persons in need who are not Master Masons, their wives and widows,
provided that preferential access to the Homes shall always be given to
Master Masons, their wives and widows and that funds given for the
purpose of providing services to Masonic residents shall in no way
subsidize the costs of providing services to non-Masonic residents. In
addition, the Board of Trustees may consider for admission to the
Masonic Homes any child without a known blood relationship to a
Master Mason on the same rules, terms and conditions as a child with
such a relationship is considered; provided that preference for
admission shall always be given to children with a blood relationship to
a Master Mason of a Lodge subject to the jurisdiction of this Grand
Lodge. Any child without a known blood relationship to a Master
Mason must be sponsored by a Lodge subject to the jurisdiction of this
Grand Lodge. Under extraordinary circumstances, the Board of
Trustees may consider for admission to the Masonic Homes a mother of
a Master Mason of a Lodge subject to the jurisdiction of this Grand
Lodge and may grant her non-resident assistance instead of admission,
provided that preference for admission shall always be given to Master
Masons, their wives and widows.
The Trustees shall establish rules, terms and conditions, subject to the
approval by the Grand Master, governing admissions to the Masonic
Homes of deserving recipients of Masonic charity. They shall pay the
necessary burial expenses of residents of the Homes who die while
under the supervision of the Trustees, in an amount to be determined by
them.
Emergent Resolution not adopted. The proposed Resolution will not come
before the delegates for consideration.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

GRAND MASTER’S DECISIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS
Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s Decision No. 1
Senior Deacon Qualification
I have been asked to interpret Section 25100 of the California Masonic
Code pertaining to the qualification of the Senior Deacon for installation. That
section states, in part, that the Senior Deacon “….. is duly qualified to give the
work and Senior Deacon’s lecture of the Fellow Craft Degree” in order to be
certified for installation. A narrow interpretation of this section would lead one
to believe that all the Senior Deacon needs to know is the Senior Deacon’s
lecture, and perhaps the floorwork accompanying it, in order to be certified. I
am confident that this is not the meaning of this section. The term “work” must
include all the normal ritual work required of the Senior Deacon in the Three
Degrees of Ancient Craft Masonry according to the Standard California Ritual.
To conclude otherwise would be to install a Senior Deacon who could only
recite the Senior Deacon’s lecture of the Fellow Craft Degree – and perhaps do
the accompanying floorwork – but not able to function otherwise as the Senior
Deacon of the lodge. That would leave the lodge potentially unable to properly
confer any of the degrees, and such an interpretation is illogical.
It is therefore my decision that the language of this section of the code
clearly means that the Senior Deacon must know all the “work” pertaining to his
office as required by our Ritual, in addition to the Senior Deacon’s lecture of the
Fellow Craft Degree.
If you approve this decision, the following narrative will be added to
the appendix of the California Masonic Code in which the Grand Master’s
interpretative decisions are recorded:
Interpretation of Section 25100 of the California Masonic Code.
Senior Deacon Qualification
(2004 G.M. Decision No. 1)
Before a Brother can be installed as Senior Deacon, he must be
qualified in all the ritual work required of the Senior Deacon in each of the
Three Degrees of Masonry according to the ritual adopted by the Grand Lodge,
including the Senior Deacon’s lecture of the Fellow Craft Degree.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The purpose of Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s Decision No. 04-01 is to
settle the controversy that arises from time to time in interpreting the meaning of
Section 25100 of the California Masonic Code governing the qualifications of
the Senior Deacon. The Grand Master has decided that the language of this
section means that the Senior Deacon must know all of the work pertaining to his

152

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

office as required by our ritual, in addition to the Senior Deacon’s lecture of the
Fellow Craft Degree. Grand Master Kirkpatrick correctly states that a narrow
interpretation of this section could lead one to believe that the Staircase Lecture
and the floor work accompanying it would be the only work in which the Senior
Deacon needed to be proficient. If that was the intent of this section, then no
Lodge in California would have a Senior Deacon capable of performing all of
the ritual appertaining to his station.
Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s decision to clarify the language of section
25100 is consistent with the current Policy and General Purposes of the Grand
Lodge of California.
Report of the Committee on Ritual:
Brethren of the Grand Lodge, the Ritual Committee offers the following
report on Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s Decision No. 1 (Interpretation of
California Masonic Code Section 25100 – Senior Deacon Qualification):
This decision is consistent with the Ritual Committee’s long-standing
interpretation of the qualification of the Senior Deacon. It is our opinion that
Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s Decision No. 1 clarifies our code to agre with its
intent, and as such, needs no further response from us.
Decision AFFIRMED.
Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s Recommendation No. 2004-01
Grand Master’s Lodge
Each year a number of our lodges are faced with a decision to either
consolidate or surrender their charter. There are many reasons leading up to this
decision including but not limited to lack of officers, lack of member interest,
increased financial obligations and many more. Consolidation has been the
option chosen most often because it was the lesser of two evils but many times
consolidation was not the best choice. Our research estimates that during the
next 15 years, 160 more lodges will close.
The reason surrender of the charter was not chosen was due to the
negative effect on the lodge members. When a charter is surrendered all
members are issued a dimit which is good for one year and that member must
find another lodge within that one year or he loses all benefits of Masonry
including Masonic assistance from the Masonic Homes for both he and his wife.
Generally, the majority of these members are elderly and unwilling or unable,
due to health to actively seek out a new lodge.
In order to correct this gross inequity, I am proposing that Grand Lodge
issue a charter to a lodge called the “Grand Master’s Lodge.” The officers of
Grand Lodge would serve as officers in the Grand Master’s Lodge ex officio.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

153

When a charter is surrendered or revoked all members of that lodge would
automatically be transferred to membership in the Grand Master’s Lodge with all
rights and benefits as they had in their old lodge. The members of the Grand
Master’s Lodge would pay dues and receive dimits when requested.
The Grand Master’s Lodge will not perform degrees or hold meetings.
It simply acts as a holding area to protect the eligibility of a Mason, his wife,
widow or orphans for assistance from the Masonic Homes of California should
the need arise.
I urge a yes vote on this proposal to help our fellow Masons who
otherwise might become homeless when his lodge surrenders its charter.
If you adopt this recommendation, the following changes will be made
to the Code.
Section 230 of the California Masonic Code will be amended to read as
follows:
§230. MEMBERS AND MANNER OF ADDRESS.
Grand Lodge shall be composed of a Grand Master (whose manner of
address shall be Most Worshipful), a Deputy Grand Master, a Senior
Grand Warden, and a Junior Grand Warden (whose manner of address
shall be Right Worshipful), a Grand Treasurer, a Grand Secretary and a
Grand Lecturer (whose manner of address shall be Very Worshipful), a
Grand Chaplain (whose manner of address shall be Very Reverend), the
Assistant Grand Lecturers, a Grand Orator, an Assistant Grand
Secretary, a Grand Marshal, a Grand Standard Bearer, a Grand Sword
Bearer, a Grand Bible Bearer, a Senior Grand Deacon, a Junior Grand
Deacon, a Senior Grand Steward, a Junior Grand Steward, a Grand
Pursuivant, a Grand Organist, a Grand Tiler, and, at the discretion of
the Grand Master, an Assistant Grand Organist and an Assistant Grand
Tiler (whose manner of address shall be Worshipful), together with all
Past Grand Officers and Past Masters of this Jurisdiction, and the
Master and Wardens of the several chartered and duly constituted
Lodges (except Research Lodges or the Grand Master’s Lodge) or their
representatives duly elected.
A Past Grand Lodge officer is one of the elective Grand Lodge officers
who has been regularly elected and installed and has served his term in
Grand Lodge.
Section 20070 of the California Masonic Code will be repealed, and a
new Section 20085 will be added to read as follows:

§20085. GRAND MASTER’S LODGE.
A special classification of constituent Lodge is hereby established, to be

membership in another Lodge;

E.

F.

insofar as the provisions may be applicable to the Grand

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Grand Master Kirkpatrick is proposing that Grand Lodge issue a charter
to a Lodge called “Grand Master’s Lodge”. The purpose of this Lodge would be
to serve as a mechanism whereby members of Lodges which have their charters
suspended or revoked, could continue membership in a recognized Lodge, thus
not jeopardizing their status as “Masons in good standing”, and the benefits that
might be available to them as members.
Research shows that many of our elderly members are unable to seek
membership in a new Lodge when their Lodges close. If they do not affiliate
with another Lodge within one year, they will lose eligibility for Masonic
benefits for both themselves and their family.
While there is precedent for the formation of a special purpose Lodge,
no precedent can be found for the formation of a Lodge as proposed by this
Recommendation. However, to relieve our distressed brethren is a long
established policy of California Freemasonry. The establishment of a procedure
to assure that our members remain Masons if a Lodge loses its charter is
consistent with all we stand for as Masons.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

This Recommendation is consistent with the Policy and General
Purposes of this Grand Lodge.
Recommendation CARRIED-OVER.
Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s Recommendation No. 2004-02
Candidate’s Fees to Grand Lodge
At present a Lodge pays Grand Lodge $1.00 for every degree it confers
on its candidates, an amount that has remained unchanged since 1850. However
the cost of adding a new Entered Apprentice Mason to the rolls of Grand Lodge
is far greater than this amount. I recommend that this $1.00 per degree fee paid
by the Lodge be replaced by a new fee to be paid by the candidate along with his
application to the Lodge. The recommended amount of this fee is $30. Because
it will be paid by the candidate, and not by the Lodge directly, the Lodge will no
longer have to pay the $1.00 per degree to Grand Lodge. And while this amount
is not great, the burden on the per capita to make up the additional cost caused
by the enrolling of a new member is significant. The new fee, paid by the
candidate with his application, will defray much of the expense that Grand
Lodge will incur as he takes his degrees.
I urge you to support this more rational way of passing along the cost of
enrolling a new member to the member himself, rather than to his lodge.
If you adopt this recommendation, the following changes will be made
to the Code.
Section 13020 of the California Masonic Code will be amended to read
as follows:

Every degree it has conferred during the year - $1,

2.

Each Master Mason carried on its rolls as of June 30,
such per capita sum, not less than $.50, as Grand
Lodge requires, and

32.

Each Master Mason carried on its rolls as of June 30,
such per capita sum as Grand Lodge levies for the
support of Masonic Homes of California;

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

2.

California.

A new Section 13025 will be added to read as follows:

Each applicant for the degrees and each applicant for affiliation whose
Dimit or Certificate is from a Lodge of another Jurisdiction shall
accompany his application with a contribution of $30 for the use and
benefit of Grand Lodge. All such contributions shall be transmitted to
the Grand Secretary at the time and in the manner specified on forms
provided for that purpose. Such contributions are non-refundable.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s recommendation No. 04-02 seeks to
replace the $1.00 fee now charged to a Lodge for a candidate for degrees. This
fee has remained the same for well over 100 years. In the meantime, the
administrative costs of this Grand Lodge have risen substantially. The proposed
increase of the fee to $30 will be part of the fees for application and will be sent
directly to Grand Lodge by the applicant’s Lodge. This increase will help defray
the direct costs incurred by Grand Lodge as a candidate takes his degrees.
This Recommendation is consistent with the Policy and General
Purposes of this Grand Lodge.

additional income to the Grand Lodge, depending upon the number of candidates
in a given year.

Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s Recommendation No. 2004-03
Lodge Funds and Property
Our law provides certain guidelines for Lodge investments and
expenditures. When these guidelines were originally added to our law, they
followed the practices which people of means at the time used when dealing with
their own investments. Over the years, prudent investment practices have
changed but conforming changes have not been made in our law. I believe that
our law should be written such that it keeps current with prudent investment
practices of the times. In addition, we hear much in the media about public
corporations being transparent in their operations, so that the investing public
has a better opportunity to understand the finances of the companies in which
they have placed their trust. Our Lodges should likewise strive for transparency.
There are also some inconsistencies in our law that have occurred over time as a
result of piece meal changes. In consultation with the applicable committees of
the Grand Lodge, I present to you for your consideration a revision of our Grand
Lodge guidelines for Lodge investments and expenditures to address these
concerns.
If you adopt this recommendation, Article VIII of Part II of the
Ordinances of the Grand Lodge will be amended to read as follows:
ARTICLE VIII
FUNDS AND PROPERTY
§21010. CONTROL AND USE.
A Lodge must retain control of its own funds and property, in
accordance with this section and as provided for elsewhere in this Code.
It may deposit its funds in a sound and reputable bank or banks. A
Chartered Lodge may, after receiving consent from the Grand Lodge
Committee on Masonic Properties, cause the incorporation of a Temple
Association for the purpose of purchasing, erecting or maintaining and
holding title to real estate for a temple for Lodge uses and purposes,
and a Lodge or Lodges may hold shares of stock in such corporation.
Space in such temple may be rented for commercial and other uses
consistent with the purposes of the Lodge or the corporation and not in
violation of this Code.
A Lodge shall not incorporate. It shall not divide its funds or property
with its members, even those withdrawing to form a new Lodge or with
the new Lodge thus formed. A Lodge shall not divide its funds with an
existing Lodge by way of a contribution.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

159

A Lodge shall adopt a budget for the following twelve months at the
first Stated Meeting held after the annual installation of the officers of
the Lodge.
No money shall be drawn without the express consent of the Lodge,
even though included in a Lodge budget, except that:
A.

Dispensed by the Charity Committee;

B.

Due Grand Lodge; and

C.

Required to pay the taxes or other charges of local, State or
Federal governmental agencies.

Lodges may approve bills against the Lodge only when the bills are
supported by proper documentation, such as an invoice or receipt. No
warrant check or draft shall be drawn issued unless there are sufficient
funds to honor it.
A Lodge shall not invest any of its funds in any manner or obligate
itself in any way for the purpose of acquiring land or building a
Masonic temple except upon compliance with the provisions of this
Code.
A Lodge shall not establish or form a foundation separate from the
Lodge with power to receive, hold, administer, invest or reinvest funds
received by the Lodge.
For more information, please refer to the Appendix on Grand Masters’
Decisions.
§21020. REVOLVING FUNDS.
By appropriate action a Lodge may establish two revolving funds, one
of not more than $500, subject to provisions of this Code, for the use of
its Stewards or Junior Warden, as the Master may determine, in
providing refreshments for all regular Lodge functions; and one of not
more than $500 for the use of its Secretary for his incidental expenses,
which funds may be replenished monthly on presentation of receipted
vouchers.
For more information, please refer to the Appendix on Grand Masters’
Decisions.
§21030. INVESTMENT.
Exclusive of a Lodge investment in an incorporated Temple
Association, a Lodge may invest its funds only as provided in this

160

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

section. All Lodge investments shall be held in the name of the Lodge.
A Lodge shall not sell its property for less than the fair market value of
the property.
The Lodge’s investments allowed under this section (other than Lodge
revolving funds and accounts with depository institutions) shall be
administered either by an investment committee appointed by the
Master or by a Board of Trustees elected by the Lodge. If a Lodge uses
a Board of Trustees to administer the Lodge’s investments, the Trustees
shall be governed by a standing resolution of the Lodge, the form of
which shall be promulgated by the Grand Master. The Trustees shall
have the power to acquire, sell and convey those Lodge’s investments
to be administered by them and shall have the power to invest and
reinvest such investments; provided, however, that all investments made
and held by the Trustees shall be in accordance with this Code, the laws
of the State of California and a written investment policy statement
approved by the Lodge as to asset classes and percentage allocations of
investments among the acceptable asset classes. The Trustees shall
rebalance the Lodge’s investment portfolio at least annually to assure
compliance with the approved investment policy statement.
There shall be an odd number of Trustees, not less than three, all of
whom shall be members of the Lodge. The Trustees shall serve
staggered three-year terms. There shall be an election of Trustees
whose terms are expiring at the first Stated Meeting held after each
annual installation of the officers of the Lodge. Trustees in office may
be removed at any time by the Lodge, and any vacancy in a Trustee’s
position may be filled at any time by the Lodge. A Lodge member may
not serve as a Lodge Trustee while serving as a trustee under any trust
by the terms of which the Lodge has a present or future interest. Before
taking office and from time to time thereafter as requested, a Trustee
shall sign and agree to be bound by conflict-of-interest rules
promulgated from time to time by the Grand Master.
The Trustees shall act by a majority vote. The Trustee shall keep books
in which all financial transactions under their administration are
correctly and plainly set forth. They shall render complete written
statements of receipts and disbursements made by them, with copies of
cancelled checks or drafts, semiannually at the Stated Meeting of the
Lodge in February and August of each year. The Trustees shall serve
without compensation and without bond.

§21040. TITLE TO ASSETS.
A Lodge may hold title to all of its assets with the power to acquire,
mortgage, sell and convey property and to invest and reinvest its funds.
It may, through trustees provided for in a standing resolution, exercise
such powers, provided that they shall always be under the control of the
Lodge, and may not buy property for the Lodge or sell property of the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

163

Lodge without prior approval of the Lodge.
One who acts as trustee, for other than the Lodge, under any trust
agreement by the terms of which the Lodge has a present or future
interest, direct or indirect, is ineligible as a Lodge trustee and the
Master shall immediately remove him. The office shall be filled in the
manner provided for in the standing resolution.
A Lodge shall not sell its property for less than fair market value of the
property.
§21050. LIFE MEMBERSHIP.
The original amounts paid for life memberships shall be forever kept
and maintained as and for a permanent Life Membership Fund. An
annual amount up to four percent of the prior year end fair market value
of the assets held in the Life Membership Fund Only interest or
dividends paid in cash on bank deposits or authorized investments in
securities shall be available for general Lodge use. A Lodge may invest
its life membership funds provided for in this Code if such investments
are kept separate and apart from all other Lodge assets.
§21060. USE OF FUNDS.
Lodge funds, including fees, dues, rental, profit or otherwise, are trust
funds set apart for the payment of the necessary, discretionary and
social expenses of the Lodge and for the special calls for charity. They
may not be invested in a life membership fund. Where funds are
donated and accepted in trust for any proper Masonic purpose they may
be used only for it. They may not be expended for alcoholic beverages.
Gifts or bequests to a Lodge, which are not limited to specific purposes,
may be used by the Lodge for any purpose permitted by this Code.
A Lodge or Temple Association shall not use any of its funds and
property to subsidize, directly or indirectly, any individual or
organization using or occupying its property, except Masonic Youth
Orders. A Lodge may, by approval of a majority of those members
present and voting at a Stated Meeting, allocate funds for community
improvement, charitable activity, or sponsorship of programs that will
benefit residents of the community in which the Lodge is located. A
Lodge or Temple Association may permit use of its facility without
rental charge for community or public activities consistent with the
provisions of this Code, provided the user defrays any separate expense
resulting from such use.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Funds collected by an officer, committee, or member of a Lodge from
members of the Lodge for social purposes of the Lodge or its members
are received in trust as special funds and are not funds of a Lodge, and
can only be used for the purpose for which the funds were collected.
Receipts and disbursements thereof should not be entered in the Lodge
books, or deposited in or withdrawn from the Lodge bank account. No
officer, committee, or member of a Lodge shall use any pressure in
collecting such funds and all payments and contributions must be
voluntary. The Lodge shall approve a procedure for receiving
contributions, making disbursements, presenting accountings for such
trust funds, and disposing of surplus. Surplus may be designated for an
object or purpose which will be to the benefit or welfare of the Lodge
and may accumulate for one year after which time it shall become part
of the funds of the Lodge. All funds shall be subject to audit annually
or more frequently as the Lodge may direct.
§21070. REQUIRED EXPENDITURES.
The expenditure of funds for any program required by Grand Lodge, or
the Grand Master, shall be deemed to be part of the necessary expenses
of the Lodge.

§21090. SOCIAL AND FRATERNAL EXPENDITURES.
A Lodge may expend, not to exceed the sum of 30% of its total revenue
derived from dues, and fees and an amount equal to four percent of the
fair market value of the Lodge’s net income from investments annually,
for the purposes of refreshments and the promotion of fraternal
intercourse, including community improvement, charitable activity, or
sponsorship of programs that will benefit residents of the community in
which the Lodge is located. Bequests, contributions to the Masonic
Homes, trust funds and profit realized from the sale of capital assets
shall not be included in the calculation of the 30% allowance. In the
discretion of the Lodge, this fund may be cumulative to the extent such
monies were available and unexpended in a prior year an amount not to
exceed 30% each year.
For more information, please refer to the Appendix on Grand Masters’
Decisions.
