Finance Committee
By Larry L. Adamson, Chairman
The role of the Finance Committee is to review and approve the
operating and capital budgets and monitor actual performance against this
budget. The Committee also reviews recommendations from the Investment
Committee of Grand Lodge regarding investment strategies for Homes funds as
well as recommendations for spending guidelines that establish parameters for
operating and capital expenditures.
The Finance Committee reviews the reports of the external auditors as
well as special internal audits that are conducted from time to time, working with
management to develop any appropriate action plans. In collaboration with the
Strategic Planning Committee, the Finance Committee oversees long term
financial planning as well as the review of financial analyses relating to new
construction and/or program development. The Committee advises the Board as
to the appropriate sources of funding for all proposed projects and programs.

212

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

The Masonic Homes of California ended FY2004 with a favorable
budget variance of just over 3%, in revenues, resulting from better than projected
annual fund donations and resident related income. Expenses ended the year
approximately at budget. This performance required careful management of
expenses in light of ever increasing costs associated with workers compensation,
employee benefits and health care related services. The overall expenses for the
Homes have grown little over the past four years, despite the fact that increases
in many health care expenses have far exceeded general inflation levels.
For the last couple of years, operating and capital expenditures at the
Homes have been guided by spending parameters set at a percentage of a fiveyear rolling investment balances of the General and Endowment Fund. This
approach ensures operating expense will be somewhat buffered from significant
year-to-year market swings while protecting the integrity of the fund balances for
future generations of residents. The performance of the stock market during the
past year has helped to mitigate some of the expected decline in this spending
calculation. However, continuing inflation in the health care industry is putting
strain on our ability to operating the Homes within this guideline. The
Committee, the Board and Staff continue to evaluate the operations of the
Homes to increase efficiencies, without sacrificing our high level of care, and to
maintain the fiscal health and strength of the Homes. In addition, the
Committee, Board and Staff are investigating several opportunities to diversify
the revenue sources of the Home to better protect the operation over the long
term.
The Committee reviewed and approved policies for the administration
and use of the various designated funds established for the purposes of granting
scholarship assistance and cultural enrichment for residents and graduates of the
children’s program in Covina. Several of these funds are administered in
conjunction with the California Masonic Foundation.
The Finance Committee reviewed regular reports from the Grand
Treasurer. These reports include investment performance reports, monthly
operating statements, quarterly capital project updates and various others.
PricewaterhouseCoopers (PwC) completed the annual external audit for the
fiscal year ended June 30, 2003. PwC gave the Homes a “clean” audit by issuing
an unqualified opinion. The Committee also reviewed the results of the capital
project audit of the dining room renovation project at Union City. Through this
audit, additional policy and procedures are being developed to improve our
capital project management.
The Finance Committee worked with management on the development
of the FY2005 budget based on the direction that management attempt to
develop a “zero-based” approach to formulating operating expenses for the
coming year. The resulting budget actually came in below the 5% spending
guideline adopted by the Board.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

213

Upon the Committee’s recommendation, and with concurrence of the
Grand Treasurer, the Board of Trustee’s established a Strategic Reserve Fund.
The favorable budget variances achieved in 2004 and future years in relation to
the spending guideline will be placed in reserve for Board approved special
projects and/or to cushion years in which spending guidelines fall short of the
ever increasing costs to run the Homes.
Working with the Grand Treasurer and management, the Committee
recommended, and the Board approved, the establishment of a Capital Reserve
Fund. Contributions to this fund will be based on annual depreciation expense.
This fund will be used to pay for future capital projects as opposed to the
spending guidelines.
Special projects reviewed by the Committee included a plan for
renovation of the Lorber skilled nursing facility at Union City, and several other
projects to maintain or enhance the campuses.
The Committee and staff continue to work on a number of special
projects to improve the cost structure of the Homes without sacrificing necessary
services to our residents. These special projects include an in depth analysis of
the cost structure at both campuses. This analysis will help us to better
understand the costs of providing our high level of service and determine
effective strategies to mitigate continuing inflation of key expenses for the
Home.
The Committee is also closely monitoring the MOS (Masonic Outreach
Services) program. This program provides services to Masons and their families
in their homes and communities. The program has been so successful that we
now face expenses that are growing beyond out expectations. The Committee is
working with staff to develop strategies to manage this growth within the
constraints of our budget and financial resources.

Medical Advisory Committee
by Mark N. Gibson, Chairman
The Committee oversees the evaluation and continuous improvement of
the quality of service provided to our patients, residents and MOS clients. In
addition, the Medical Advisory Committee monitors compliance with
appropriate licensure and accreditation programs and receives reports on
resident satisfaction. This year the Committee continued to monitor a number of
quality of care indicators, comparing them both to internal longitudinal trends
and, where available, external industry standards. These include:
Quarterly tracking of statistics relating to adult residential care
indicators: medication management, skin condition, weights, hydration, falls,
and resident participation in activities. The Committee received information
demonstrating that these indicators fell within appropriate ranges for comparable
long term care facilities and reviewed plans for on-going quality improvement
initiatives designed to optimize the health of our residents.
Monitoring of the rates of resident admissions to Skilled Nursing
Facilities and Acute hospitalizations. The goal of this evaluation is to ensure
that the Homes provides an environment in which residents can “age in place”,
remaining independent as long as possible.
Utilization of services such as clinic appointments, dental
ophthalmology and referrals to specialists were reviewed for appropriateness and
timeliness. The Committee received reports from the Medical Director, Dr.
Sheila Raumer, regarding her collaboration with the primary care physicians at
Union City to monitor the utilization of services and referrals to outside
consultant services and a reduction in numbers of referrals and thus management
of costs to the Home.
Preferred Provider Relationships with community-based Skilled
Nursing Facilities. The Committee reviewed a report on the establishment of a
quality of care checklist established by the Executive Director at Covina, the
Director of Health Services. Based on these evaluations, three facilities, Mt. San
Antonio Gardens, Hillcrest, and Pilgrim Place were identified as our preferred
facilities for placement of Covina residents, except where resident preference
calls for alternative placement. The Covina management team regularly follows
residents placed in these off-site facilities reported on their care to the Medical

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

215

Advisory Committee. Residents and staff report high levels of satisfaction with
the care rendered in these skilled nursing facilities. All three facilities are part of
Continuing Care Retirement Communities (CCRC’s) and are members of the
California Association of Home and Services for the Aging (CAHSA).
Quality Indicators for the Children’s Program. The Committee
received an expanded list of quality indicators for the children’s program at
Covina and began monitoring these results on a regular basis. They include
statistics on the utilization of health services, review of community care licensing
reports, academic reports, including grade point average trends and community
and volunteer activities of the children. We also began to track and report on the
frequency of reunification with family and post-graduate progress of our children
after they leave the program as additional areas of focus in the development of
our program. The latter indicators come directly out of the work of our Child
Care Task Force, also chaired by Mark Gibson, which worked this year with
staff to refine the structure of the children’s program at Covina.

Strategic Planning Committee
by George L., Chairman
This past year has been a particularly busy and challenging one for the
Strategic Planning Committee of the Masonic Homes of California. As members
of this committee and of this Board, we continually strive to be the best possible
stewards of the resources entrusted to our care. This committee, supported by
our executive staff, has spent the past year responding to emerging needs of our
membership, maintaining our facilities, overseeing our operations while charting
a course for the future.
In November, 2003 after a two-year collaborative strategic planning
process the Board of Trustees adopted a new three year strategic plan. The
intent behind the plan was to:
Protect and further the mission of the organization;
Protect and optimize the financial resources of the organization; and,
Position the organization to respond to the changing and emerging
needs of the membership.
The process of researching our options and preparing the plan included
extensive research into industry trends and the needs of the membership as well
as surveys, focus groups and educational sessions. Particularly informative and
helpful to this process was the participation of three representatives from other
Masonic Homes in our annual strategic retreat - their input and experiences were
invaluable in shaping the proposed strategic direction.
Another significant achievement this year was the adoption of a new
vision statement for the Masonic Homes of California. This vision statement is
much more than words hanging on a plaque on the wall. This vision is a
summary of who we are and who we want to be and how we fit within the overall
fraternity. All initiatives and all activities planned for the Homes now must be
seen and judged in relation to this guiding vision.
Our Vision
The Masonic Homes of California are a unifying symbol of the
fraternal commitment to provide Relief for the betterment of our
members in need and society in general. Working in collaboration with
individual Masons and Lodges, we eliminate those conditions in life
that prevent people from achieving their full potential, enjoying
satisfying relationships with family and friends and contributing to
their communities.
We provide an innovative array of services and programs that support
people's journey through life's transitions.

218

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

We ensure seniors are provided an environment of love, care and
understanding, where they may live up to their fullest physical and
emotional potential and continue to grow and experience life in a
positive atmosphere.
Through our efforts, Children acquire a quality education, master new
skills, develop self-sufficiency and independence and learn the virtue of
generosity.
We help parents learn effective parenting and coping skills to foster
nurturing and loving relationships within their families and raise their
children in a loving and healthy environment.
We provide resources and guidance for members who support their
elderly family members through the challenges and functional changes
associated with the aging process.
We help members and lodges fulfill their fraternal obligation to support
their distressed brethren and families.
We make the best use of the resources entrusted to our care through the
generosity of our members while augmenting these resources with
additional means to support our fraternity's needs for the future.
With an appreciation and respect for our 100 year history, the Masonic
Homes of California are committed to remain relevant to today's
members. Honoring the traditions of the Masonic Fraternity and
recognizing the changing world in which we live, we are committed to
responsible change and set the standard for exceptional senior and
childcare services.
Unifying Symbol of Relief
The concept of the Masonic Homes of California as “unifying symbol
of relief” appears as the opening note in our vision statement. What does this
mean? Over 100 years ago our forefathers built the Masonic Homes of
California to act as the organized arm of relief for our fraternity. In effect, they
created a ‘safety net’ for our fraternal family.
This safety net fills the gaps in the social support network – particularly
for the elderly and children. We know that without our support, many members
of our fraternity would be living in deplorable and unsafe conditions that demean
their humanity.
Generations of Masons have given generously to the Masonic Homes of
California to prevent this kind of isolation and degradation from befalling any of
our members. The brethren have, in effect, pooled our resources to create an
institution that can compassionately and appropriately respond to the needs of

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

219

our members today and tomorrow. As such, the Masonic Homes of California
cares for seniors and children in the name of every Mason in California – past,
present and future – and symbolizes our fraternal commitment to Relief.
We are at a challenging point in the history of our fraternity in that 50%
of us are over the age of 70. Many of our members are adapting to fixed and/or
limited incomes, chronic medical conditions and the increasing need for
assistance with the activities of daily living. Many members have minimal or no
family support and would be isolated without the support of the fraternity.
Further, we know that our elderly widows are most at risk of slipping through the
cracks and spending their final years isolated, impoverished and lonely. That is,
without the support from our fraternity.
To prevent any of our members from facing this kind of isolation, the
Homes are now challenging the lodges to make a renewed commitment to the
most aged and vulnerable members of our fraternal family. This commitment
can include such things as a monthly call, a drive to a physician or a referral to
the Homes. If the lodge finds that the needs of an individual are greater than
their ability to support, these needs can be met either in the Homes or through
the outreach program.
Our research into the needs of our membership demonstrated that the
middle-aged Mason often has need of support from the Masonic Homes as well.
Many are feeling the pressures of the “sandwich” generation in that they are
being asked to care for their elderly parents and their own children. In addition,
these middle-aged Masons are also increasingly being called on to help with
their elderly fraternal brethren.
This past year, we developed an extensive communications campaign to
educate the membership about the programs and services of the Homes and to
stimulate and unite us in the shared cause of ensuring that no member of our
fraternal family goes unheeded and unheard. Our hope is that by reconnecting
the individual Mason to the Masonic Homes of California we will strengthen the
safety net for the majority of our members who are aging in the community.
A few highlights of our communication efforts this year have included:
Several mailings to our widows reminding them they are not alone
Development of all new print material on the Homes that emphasize our
theme of “Welcome Home”
Production and distribution to all lodges of a new 14 minute video on
the Homes
Launch of a new website
Regular mailings to the leadership of the fraternity
Mailing of a new poster for all lodge bulletin boards with information
about the Homes

220

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Regular mailings to all Entered/Apprenticed Masons introducing them
to the Masonic Homes
Preparation and mailing of informational packets for those who are
newly widowed
Over 100 presentations at lodges or fraternal events across the state
Challenges
Our focus in improving our communications with the membership and
outreach to our vulnerable members has proven to be successful and we now are
experiencing an unprecedented demand for our services. Both the Homes in
Union City and Covina have a waiting list of between 1-3 years, depending on
the level of service needed. And demand for our Masonic Outreach Services
(MOS) program has grown 33% in the past four months!
This is gratifying and positive in that we know we are doing more to
find those in need and have alleviated unimaginable suffering. Simply put - we
know that our services and programs are saving lives.
However, this unprecedented demand for our services is occurring at a
time when we are facing significant economic pressures. Currently, the Masonic
Homes of California relies heavily on our investments to fund operations. We
operate under spending guidelines that hedge against inflation and protect our
resources for future generations, while funding our charitable model of care.
In recent years, our investments, while outperforming the industry,
suffered reductions in value consistent with general market conditions. Further
challenging our economic future is the health care inflationary rate, which is 2.5
times that of general inflation. This makes it that much more difficult for the
earnings from investments to keep pace with increases in health care costs. We
are particularly vulnerable to these rising costs because of the high acuity levels
on our campuses and waiting lists.
To act in the best long-term interest of our organization, our options to
respond to this funding shortfall include:
Identifying new sources of revenue or charitable funding
Considering reductions and/or closures in programs
Given the demand and unmet need for services in our membership, we
are exploring every possible alternative to service reductions.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

221

Strategic Response
In the face of increasing need and an uncertain economic forecast, this
committee has spent almost three years and countless hours researching,
discussing and considering ways to respond to these challenges.
We are working closely with the Department of Philanthropy to
improve the support from the members. Our belief is that if the members knew
the value of the services and support provided through the Masonic Homes, they
would give gladly and generously. As such, our commitment is to do more to
“tell the story” of the work being carried out by the Homes and the impact on the
individuals we serve.
On the operations side, we are completing a detailed and thorough
review of all aspects of our operations and benchmarking ourselves against the
industry. We are challenging ourselves to operate as efficiently as possible,
while maintaining the highest standards of quality and resident satisfaction.
Most significantly, we have identified alternatives to service reductions
through opportunities to expand and diversify our revenue sources.
Currently, the Masonic Homes of California bills private insurance
carriers for medical services provided to our residents who have such coverage.
Consistent with the strategies of Masonic Homes across the country, we
extensively researched the impact and consequences of expanding our billing
practices to include billing Medicare and MediCal on behalf of our residents that
receive skilled nursing services on our Union City campus.
This issue is covered more extensively in the Presidents report, but I
want to offer the membership the assurance that this committee has spent three
years carefully and diligently considering all the implications of accepting this
new revenue. From multiple legal opinions, to visits and discussions with
Masonic Homes and other providers across the country, we have come to the
firm and clear conclusion that pursuing this reimbursement is the most
responsible and effective step that we can take to ensure that we can continue to
meet the today’s burgeoning need while protecting our resources for future
generations.
Other Strategic Initiatives
Beyond tapping into this one new vital source of revenue, our
committee is researching other opportunities for serving those in need. For
instance, there is an unmet need in our society for affordable aging services
targeting the middle-class. There are federal and state programs that provide
(minimal) services to the most impoverished and there are services available to
those that can afford them. But the middle income segment of the population is
not provided the same range of opportunities. From this, we are analyzing our

222

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

options for expanding our capacity and creating more market-rate services. In
considering this direction, the Board is clear that such projects would be
considered if and only if they were break-even or profitable. While the
leadership has a strong interest in extending the mission of the Homes, the
organization is not in a position to make further demands on our charitable
dollars.
Completing feasibility studies on new options that make the best and
highest use of our resources, including our land, is a key feature of our goals and
metrics for this fiscal year.
Childrens’ Services
The Masonic Homes of California currently serves 42 children in
residential programs on the Covina campus. The children may or may not have
Masonic affiliations and the philosophy behind the program is that each child
deserves the opportunity to develop to his/her highest potential. Consistent with
is philosophical and historical roots, the organization has continued to make
significant investments in this program, even in difficult economic times. Given
the critical shortage of effective residential programs for children and
adolescents in this state, the children we serve have few other options for care.
As part of the goals and metrics for the organization this year, the
leadership is clearly defining the long-term strategic vision for the program with
a focus on successful outcomes and improved cost effectiveness. This includes
restructuring the leadership of the program, evaluating the best model of care
and developing effective outcome measurements.
Conclusion
In closing, the Strategic Planning Committee and its Chairman would
like to acknowledge the leadership and support of the entire Board of Trustees of
the Masonic Homes and its President, Allen Gresham, PGM. Together we are
committed to upholding the standards of excellence demanded of us by the
guiding principles of freemasonry and to ensure our services are delivered in a
manner befitting our founding ideals. It is an honor and a privilege to be a
member of this Board.
The Committee includes:
George L. Geanoulis, Chairman
Board Trustee of the Masonic Homes of California
Allen B. Gresham, PGM
President of the Board of the Masonic Homes of California

President’s Report
By Allen B. Gresham, PGM
Last year I opened my report on the Masonic Homes of California at the
154th Annual Communication of the Grand Lodge of California by quoting a
statement made in 1873 by Grand Master Leonidis Pratt, fully 25 years before
our first Home was opened in DeCoto, California. Most Worshipful Pratt,
addressing his hopes for the Craft to the Brethren of his day, stated “I would
make Masonry as practical as the age in which we live.”
In many ways Grand Master Pratt’s prophetic proclamation has become
the bellweather refrain for the strategic direction forged by the Board of Trustees
of the Masonic Homes of California over the last year. Today, in residential and
community-based services, the Masonic Homes cares for over 500 seniors and
children in the name of every Mason in California – past, present and future. We
view ourselves as the partner and extension of the blue lodges as they strive to
meet their commitment to their brethren, wives and especially widows. The
Homes continually strive for excellence in all that we do to ensure that our
services are delivered in a manner that befits our founding ideals. In response to
the changing needs of the membership and society, the programs and services of
the Masonic Homes have evolved over the years, striving to be “as practical as
the age in which we live.” Despite these changes, our commitment to providing
relief remains unwavering.
The significant accomplishments that have occurred at the Home are
outlined in more detail in the reports of the Board Committee chairmen as well
as our management staff. However, particularly noteworthy this year was the
adoption by the Board of a new Strategic Plan in November, 2003 as well as a

224

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

new Vision Statement in August, 2004. These documents were unanimously
adopted by the Board of Trustees.
Our new Vision Statement, the first in the history of the Homes to our
knowledge, is included in the report of the Strategic Planning Committee. Our
vision calls for the Masonic Homes of California to be a unifying symbol of the
fraternal commitment to provide Relief for the betterment of our members in
need and society in general. Working in collaboration with individual Masons
and Lodges, we eliminate those conditions in life that prevent people from
achieving their full potential, enjoying satisfying relationships with family and
friends and contributing to their communities.
In our attempt to follow the vision of Most Worshipful Pratt to “stay as
practical as the age in which we live”, the Board has spent considerable time
understanding the needs of our membership today and in the future. What are
some of the challenges that the Homes and our brethren face today that are
driving the development of our services and programs?
When the Homes were founded at the turn of the last century the
average life expectancy of a man was 49; today it is 77. With this has come a
range of age-related issues unimaginable at the time of our founding, not the
least of which is Alzheimer’s and other forms of dementia that affect 50% of the
population over the age of 80.
The majority of our membership who prepared and saved responsibly
for retirement over the course of their working years now find that they lack
sufficient resources to live out their lives with dignity and self sufficiency. The
financial resources that allowed them to live until their 70’s run out as they enter
their 80’s and 90’s, just when their need for assistance is greatest.
Our elderly widows are the most vulnerable members of our fraternal
family and unfortunately the most likely to be disconnected from their husband’s
lodges and from the fraternity in general. Our research shows that our widows
are three times more likely to live in poverty than Masons in the same age
groups. Many of these widows can’t afford groceries, go without medications
and may be only weeks away from being homeless.
And what about the middle-aged Mason today? Their burden, their
distress is different than those of their fathers. While raising and educating their
children, they may also be faced with providing financial assistance and
arranging medical care for their aging parents. This is the phenomenon known
as the “sandwich generation”. We tend to say our fraternity is aging, but there
are many middle aged masons in our fraternity who will have different pressures
when they reach retirement due to the financial burdens they face today. For
these people the Masonic Homes needs to make sure we not only protect our
resources for those we serve today but also for those we will serve in 15 to 20
years.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

225

This year one of our priorities was enhancing the communication
between the Homes and the membership of the fraternity. Over the last year, the
Board, management staff and our residents have made almost 100 presentations
to lodges and other fraternal groups. We have had articles spotlighting Homes
programs and services in every issue of the California Freemason and News
from the Quarries. Despite our belief that the Homes and our Outreach program
are the “crown jewels” of California freemasonry, many brethren know
remarkably little about the Homes, our mission and the services we offer.
The result of our increased visibility is that this year we have
experienced an unprecedented 33% increase in the demand for our Masonic
Outreach Services, (or MOS as we refer to it.) Our Homes provide independent
living, assisted living and skilled nursing care to our residents. Today, our
waiting list for admission at both Homes has risen to at least one year and as
long as three years, depending on the level of care required by the applicant.
Our MOS outreach program fills the gap in social support systems for the
neediest in our fraternity and also provides a safety valve of support for those
whose needs cannot wait until there is room for their admission. This year
$650,000 was raised through our annual fund drive for the Homes, all of which
went to support the MOS program.
As President of the Board, I receive frequent letters of thanks,
especially from the widows served by the MOS program. One example, which
reflects the sentiments of all of our clients, stated: “You can’t imagine what this
program means to a widow like me who has no family. I can’t thank you and the
Masons for the help I am receiving.” Brethren, I honestly can’t imagine what
would happen to these ladies if the Homes were not there to support them. The
sad fact is that there are undoubtedly more widows out there of whom we are not
aware and who, in turn, do not know of the help that is available to them.
This brings me to some of the challenges that the Homes will face over
the next few years. Currently the Masonic Homes of California, including our
outreach program, relies heavily on the proceeds from our investments to fund
our operational needs. We operate under spending guidelines that hedge against
inflation and protect our resources for future generations, while funding our
charitable model of care and responding to the emerging needs of today’s
residents and clients. We need to fund our current obligations while preserving
our assets for those members of the fraternity in need who follow us.
In recent years, our investments have outperformed the equity market
averages, but have suffered from reductions in value consistent with the
conditions in the stock market. At the same time the rate of inflation in the
health care, the leading component of our expenses, is 2.5 times the rate of
general inflation. This makes it that much more difficult for the earnings from
investments to keep pace with increases in health care costs.

226

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

As a part of our strategic planning, we have identified three ways to
respond to the projected shortfall over the next few years between our operating
expenses and the funds available under our spending guidelines:
1.

Philanthropy – As I mentioned earlier, the annual fund proceeds now
go exclusively to support our growing outreach program. These
philanthropic dollars, as important as they are, represent only a small
portion of the Homes total operating budget. While the average size of
each gift has increased, the percentage of membership that makes
annual gifts to the Homes remains at a disappointing 18%. The takeaway message here is that we urge each and every one of the brethren to
give to the extent of your ability. However, even with our projected
increased giving, we recognize that philanthropy alone is insufficient to
address the scope of our economic challenges.

2.

Expense Reduction – For the past few years, we have embarked on a
comprehensive operational review to improve the quality and cost
effectiveness of our care. We have reduced our operating expenses to
neutralize inflation for the last four years while at the same time
improving our resident satisfaction. At this point, our operations are
efficient and while we always look for additional opportunities to
improve, we think significant additional reductions will be limited.

3.

This brings us to the most important of the three strategies, New
Revenue Sources – Currently the Masonic Homes of California bills
private insurance carriers for medical services provided to our residents
who have such coverage. Consistent with the strategies of Masonic
Homes across the country, we have extensively researched the impact
and consequences of expanding our billing practices to include
Medicare and MediCal on behalf of our residents that receive skilled
nursing services on our Union City campus.

We have studied the issue of billing for Medicare and MediCal
reimbursement for close to three years now and the Board unanimously reached
the decision that this is a fiscally prudent course for us to take -- one that secures
our resources so that we can extend our assistance to more of our fraternal family
now and in the future.
Our analysis has demonstrated that billing Medicare and MediCal for
skilled nursing services would result in another $2 - $2.5 million in revenue
annually. To put this dollar amount in perspective, this equates to the annual
cost of caring for 30 residents of our Homes or 100 more MOS clients.
Medicare and, in some cases MediCal coverage are benefits that each of our
residents have earned over their working lives. They are benefits they would be

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

227

receiving from the Medicare and MediCal programs if they were placed in any
other skilled nursing facility.
Pursuing this reimbursement will require some alteration to our current
admission policies. Legally, to be an eligible Medicare/MediCal provider we
must be willing to offer those services for which we receive reimbursement to
the general community.
This change may sound significant, but in reality it will have little
operational impact. Fortunately, under California law, fraternal and religious
organizations like the Masonic Homes may legitimately give preference to their
own fraternal constituents. This means that as long as there is demand for skilled
nursing service by any members of the fraternal family, we will not need to
admit members of the general community. Further we have an ability to hold
aside enough of our skilled nursing capacity that we can always accommodate
internal transfer to skilled nursing for current residents. As a part of our due
diligence, the Homes Board has obtained numerous legal opinions affirming
these protections under the law.
Another element of our research reveals we are one of the only Masonic
Homes in the country that is not accepting Medicare and Medicaid
reimbursement for skilled nursing care. Our colleagues in Homes in other
Masonic jurisdictions have all participated in these government funding
programs for several years and report that the requirement to be open to the
general public has not imposed restrictions on their ability to serve their
members. In most cases admission of non-fraternal patients has been infrequent.
Their consistent message is that accepting this reimbursement has allowed them
to protect and further their charitable mission and, in fact, they are generally
surprised that we do not accept the reimbursement on which they have come to
rely to strengthen their core mission.
There may be those of you who worry that this step results in letting the
government, like the proverbial nose of the camel, into the tent. With the
governmental dollar will we be faced with increased government regulation and
oversight? Well, the fact is that by virtue of our license we are already regulated
by the government. The Board has always demanded that we not just meet but
exceed the standards called for in the skilled nursing community. We’re proud
of our facilities and the care provided to our patients and we are confident we
will easily meet all regulatory requirements that accompany reimbursement.
I need to be absolutely clear about the use and protection of our
philanthropic dollars. We are fortunate to have available to us endowment and
general funds given to us over the years through the generosity of past
generations of brethren. As trustees, it is our job to ensure that those dollars are
only used for the purpose for which they are given – that is for support and
assistance of our fraternal constituents. In the event we do occasionally accept
members of the general community into our skilled nursing facility as a result of

228

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

this change, these individuals will not in any way be subsidized by our
endowment funds.
In conclusion, motivated by our commitment to Relief and our
obligation to the brotherhood, your Trustees are committed to keeping the
Masonic Homes relevant to the age in which we live. In the 1930’s, the Trustees
of the Homes decided to accept social security benefits for residents in order to
make the financial resources of the Homes go further. This was a decision they
reached only after careful consideration; however, had they not made this
decision the Homes would have been deprived of over millions in revenue in the
past 70 years. Today your Trustees feel it is essential to tap into another vital
source of revenue, one that was earned by those we serve, in order to make our
financial resources go further for present and future fraternal family.
Respectfully submitted,
Allen B. Gresham, PGM, President

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE VICE PRESIDENT
September 2004
The management team of the Masonic Homes of California is
comprised of the Executive Director, Union City, Robert Fallon, the Executive
Director, Covina, John Howl, and the Director of Program Development and
Masonic Outreach Services, Steffani Kizziar. Each of these managers report to
the Executive Vice President. In addition, the Homes contracts with Grand
Lodge Corporate Services to provide a variety of staff support services,
including Finance, Human Resources, Information Services, Membership
Services, and the Office of Philanthropy. The Director of Finance of Grand
Lodge/Homes is Mark Koenig. The Director of the Office of Philanthropy for
Masonic Services is Doug Ismail. The Director of Human Resources for the
Grand Lodge/Homes is held in a shared job structure by Wendy Riley and Linda
Schomaker, who joined the organization in November, 2003. The Corporate
Services managers who provide services to the Masonic Homes also function as
members of the Homes executive team.
The management activities of the Masonic Homes are organized into
four areas, for which annual objectives, approved by the Board of Trustees, form
the basis of the yearly evaluation of the management team. These include:
I.

QUALITY IMPROVEMENT

Initiatives focused on improving the quality of the services we provide with a
corresponding positive impact on the quality of life of our residents and clients.
Effective communication is an essential determinant of how well the
membership accesses the services and programs of the Homes. Recognizing that
there is a significant contingent of the fraternal family that is uninformed about
services and programs offered by the Homes and our outreach programs, the
Board and staff implemented a comprehensive communications plan in 20032004. We queried our residents to identify a theme for this plan and they
suggested the slogan, “Welcome Home” to denote the feelings they have toward
our wonderful facilities and programs.
The goal of the communications plan is to have the blue lodges and
membership come to view the Masonic Homes as an essential partner that can
facilitate fulfillment of their obligations to their brethren.
Over 100
presentations were made on the Homes at lodges, other fraternal gatherings and
Grand Lodge leadership programs. Each issue of the California Freemason and
News from the Quarries featured articles on the Masonic Homes of California.
Our website was redesigned in June, 2004. Posters were designed for display in
the lodge rooms that highlight how referrals and requests for information can be
submitted to Homes staff. A video and collateral material about the Homes was

230

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

sent to every Entered Apprentice to the Craft so they could be informed about
the resources we provide as they enter the Masonic family. Finally, this year a
resource guide for the Newly Widowed was written and sent to lodge
membership to help them support their brethren and widows at a time of a
difficult life transition. In short, our goal is to make sure no one in the fraternal
family who is in need of services “gets left behind”.
Our waiting lists in 2003-2004 for the Union City and Covina homes
increased to a range of one to three years depending upon the level of care
needed by the applicant. This level of interest in the Homes indicates that our
efforts to disseminate information about our services have been successful.
However, it also creates a need for us to improve our contact with and support of
those applicants on the waiting list.
We also experienced an unprecedented demand for assistance through
our Masonic Outreach Services (MOS) program. This program provides
financial and case management support to the most disadvantaged members of
our fraternal family and helps fill the gap in the social safety net for the elderly.
This year we experienced a 33% increase in enrollment this year. We can all be
gratified by this growth as it means that we are doing more to reach out to those
in need. However, this unexpected growth challenges us financially and makes
the proceeds of the Annual Fund that much more important to us.
In response to the length of our waiting lists and the increase in demand
for our MOS program, in August, 2004 we created a new Centralized Intake
function. We now have a single 800# for all calls for Masonic Assistance. Our
dedicated staff member, Norma Puplampu, coordinates this service. Her role is
to screen all applicants for immediate need and ensure they receive services as
they need them. We know that waiting for services can be difficult when needs
are immediate and now we are able to immediate respond and develop
appropriate care plans to those in need.
As our membership strives to remain in their own Homes as long as
possible, the average age of our applicants has increased to 83 and we see a
corresponding increase in their frailty. In response, we have expanded the scope
of responsibility of our MOS case managers to provide oversight for many of
those on our waiting lists. In some cases, where an applicant needs more
immediate placement than is afforded by the waiting list, we arrange placement
and financial support, if necessary, for the applicant in a high quality community
facility.
The MOS staff also established a monthly case management clinic at
Paradise Park, a Masonic community of single family homes in Santa Cruz, and
has held a community health education fair.
Several other quality improvement initiatives were introduced within
the organization in 2003-2004, including service excellence training for the staff
at our two Homes, skill development classes for our certified nurse attendants

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

231

and the expansion of our recreation and fitness programs. In addition, the dining
programs were enhanced and facilities renovated at both Homes.
Management worked with the Board this year to undertake an
assessment of the structure of our children’s program. We established a task
force chaired by Trustee Mark Gibson that has studied trends in residential care
programs for children. A new Director of the Children’s Program, Marissa
Espinoza, joined our staff in October, 2003. Building on the excellent standards
we have implemented over the last few years using the Boy’s and Girl’s Town
Program, we are attempting to strengthen the academic performance of our
children, increase the intensity of our psychological counseling, promote the goal
of family reunification where possible, and enhance the structure of our postgraduate support programs. To achieve these goals, meaningful involvement of
the child’s family in his/her therapy is increasingly important. We also are
exploring the establishment of a family resource center which could provide
outreach services to fraternally related children comparable to the MOS program
as an adjunct to the Children’s program.
We believe participation in the leadership of the broader community of
providers in the aging services industry helps keep our operations and programs
current and strengthens the industry. The management team maintains active
involvement in committees and conferences associated with both our state
professional organization, the California Association of Homes and Services for
the Aging (CAHSA) and the national organization, the American Association of
Homes and Services for the Aging (AAHSA).
This year the Board signed the Quality First Covenant that is sponsored
by AAHSA. AAHSA launched Quality First, a covenant between providers,
consumers and the government in 2002 to strive for excellence in aging services
and earn the public’s trust. Quality First is a commitment to help revolutionize
aging services to meet the needs of our current and future residents and clients.
II.

STRATEGIC
PERFORMANCE
DEVELOPMENT

AND

PROGRAM

Initiatives focused on responding to the evolving needs of the membership and
positioning our organization to remain competitive and strong for the future.
The Board completed a three year strategic plan and adopted its first
vision statement this year. Our strategic plan calls for pursuing initiatives that
gradually reduce our reliance on investment proceeds from our endowment and
general funds by identifying new sources of revenue and continuing to improve
the efficiency of our operations. Our vision statement calls for the Masonic
Homes to be a major resource to our fraternal constituents by helping them
through the challenges of life transitions, including those associated with
childhood, adolescence, parenting, middle age and the elderly.
More

232

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

information on these activities is included in this year’s report of the Strategic
Planning Committee.
Following two years of evaluation the Board of Trustees submitted
Emergent Resolution 04-06 for the consideration at the Annual Communication.
This resolution would allow the Masonic Homes to accept Medicare and
MediCal revenue for skilled nursing patients at Union City by broadening the
admission criteria for the skilled nursing facility and thereby generating $2-2.5
million in annual revenue.
Just as the Board strives to maximize the use of our endowment and
general funds they recognize that another asset of the Homes that should be put
to the best and highest use is our land. This year we began to evaluate
alternatives for developing our property that are consistent with both the mission
of the organization and which may provide a financial contribution allowing us
to extend our services to the Fraternity.
In an effort to improve the quality and scope of service to the
membership, this year we have continued to identify high quality providers in the
state who augment the services we provide directly at the Homes or for our MOS
clients. These preferred “partner” providers include three skilled nursing
facilities in southern California where Covina residents needing SNF care are
placed and numerous other assisted living facilities throughout the State where
we have placed MOS clients in need of facility care. We also have established
close ties with PACE (Programs for All-Inclusive Care of the Elderly) providers
in northern and southern California that provide comprehensive care for seniors
on a capitated basis in settings to keep them out of skilled nursing facilities as
long as possible.
This year the Board established two new special reserve funds: a
Capital Reserve Fund has been established by beginning the practice of setting
aside annual cash reserves equal to total depreciation. This fund will be used to
cover future capital requirements for routine equipment and renovation. A
Strategic Reserve Fund has been set up by setting aside any excess operating
margin over and above our spending guidelines. These funds will be available to
seed selected strategic projects in the future or to cover expenditures in excess of
spending guidelines in future years.
III.

ORGANIZATION

Initiatives focused on strengthening, educating, and improving the staff,
management and executive teams and Board of Trustees to increase job
satisfaction and quality of services provided.
Under the direction of our new Director of Human Resources the Board
and management established a new compensation philosophy for the Homes,
designed to ensure that our employees are fairly compensated and the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

233

organization can attract and retain a qualified workforce. We have completed an
evaluation of our salary ranges and benefit design and have implemented salary
range adjustments where indicated.
Two major areas of increasing expense in both the senior and child
services industries are employee benefit costs, notably health insurance and
workers’ compensation costs, as well as property and casualty liability insurance.
This year we selected new brokers for these areas that have allowed us to
structure and place our insurance coverage more creatively and thereby keep our
average cost increases below industry averages.
The management teams at Grand Lodge and the Masonic Homes held
three joint retreats this year to clarify our respective roles and to ensure that we
mutually support our priority responsibilities. These have been quite effective in
fostering closer collaboration between the Grand Lodge and Homes executive
teams. In addition, the two management groups have completed management
skills assessments in order to develop individual professional development plans.
In addition to key leadership conferences attended by both management
and the Board, the Board participated in educational sessions on the following
topics: PACE programs, the emerging directions of children’s residential
services, options for the development and operation of independent living, and
the AAHSA Quality First program.
IV.

PERFORMANCE AND COST IMPROVEMENT

Inititatives focused on identifying improved and more efficient ways of providing
service and ensuring that we remain responsible stewards of our resources.
For the fourth consecutive year the Masonic Homes of California has
experienced a favorable variance in net operations, or net revenue over expenses,
of $1,255,698 or a $955,698 favorable variance over our budgeted margin of
$300,000. As a result, spending levels for the year fell below the targeted
guidelines of 5% of the rolling average of our asset balance. This favorable
variance from the 5% target has been placed in our new Strategic Reserve Fund.
The favorable financial performance has been the result of focused efforts to
improve operational effectiveness while maintaining and improving quality and
resident satisfaction. Our costs per resident day have remained stable or
decreased in this time period as our Homes operate at full capacity and despite
that fact that health care related costs are increasing at 2.5 times the rate of
general inflation.
We are fully able to cover our financial commitments for lifetime care
to current residents and clients. By continuing to find ways to be even better
stewards of the resources entrusted to us we can ensure we will continue to make
similar commitments to future generations of Masons.

234

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

In closing, I would like to express my sincere appreciation to all of our
hardworking Trustees for their gifts of time and talent this year. This year their
time commitment was unusually demanding due to the significance of the
strategic issues facing the organization. I continue to be impressed with the
untiring devotion to the Homes and our mission. It is an honor and a privilege to
work with them on behalf of service to our residents and clients and in
furtherance of that mission.
Respectfully submitted,
Deborah E. Stebbins, Executive Vice President

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

235

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR
MASONIC HOME, UNION CITY
2003 - 2004
As I enter my fourth year here at the Masonic Home, I continue to
marvel at the services and care the Masons of California are able to provide our
residents here at the Home and through our outreach services. As society
continues to struggle with the challenges of caring for the elderly, our residents
are immune to the daily concerns that affect those seniors who reside outside of
our community.
Key achievements this past year include the ventilation repair and
remodeling of the Dining Room in the Sedam building. The new open design
was well received by the residents who have noted that it is a beautiful
complement to the dramatic improvement in overall food service and quality.
The Masonic Home staff completed a resident customer service
program to understand and implement improved methods to provide better
service to our residents. Thank You Very Much, Inc. provided a carefully
scripted training program so the staff could learn how best to deal with residents
through many situations, including providing support to residents in need and
dealing with difficult and demanding individuals. The training was well received
by the staff and in turn the residents have enjoyed the improved respect from the
staff.
This past year we implemented a campus wide computer software
program, Health Care software (HCS), to provide better tracking of the
individual health care conditions for our residents. With this new system we are
able to connect all health care components of a resident’s care needs into one
data base which, in turn, is available to all health care providers serving the
needs of this resident. This software will be able to provide a more complete
overview of each resident’s unique health care needs.
The residents of the Home recently completed the third annual election
for their seven-member Resident Council established two years ago as a unifying
voice of the resident community. The Council, though still young, is gradually
getting stronger as the community representative group. Management meets
with them every month to talk about issues of concern at the Home. The Council
has been instrumental in resolving problems within the community and providing
guidance to management on ways we could improve our services to the
residents.
Over the past few years, the management of the Masonic Home has
looked carefully at the services provided to the residents of the Home. The goal
of management has been to keep our residents as independent and as healthy as
possible. Key components of helping residents remain independent is to keep

236

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

them physically active, to continue to do their daily routines like all independent
individuals and to keep their mind active by overseeing their financials as much
as possible. We have removed previously limitless services such as groceries,
beauty salon treatments and toiletries, while at the same time increasing the
residents’ allowance with the money saved from these programs. This allows the
residents to determine what groceries, treatments and toiletries they need with
the allowances provided for them. We also opened a general store on the Union
City site to improve their ability to obtain these products.
The management team will continue to review and analyze the services
provided to the residents while always striving to be financially efficient in our
departments and services. By comparing ourselves to the long-term care
industry, we are learning and discovering better and more efficient ways to
provide programs without reducing the quality of our services. We intend to
continue to maintain high resident satisfaction ratings while at the same time
being strong fiscal stewards of our operating budget. Our scores on our resident
satisfaction surveys continue to improve each year and we will work with the
Resident Council to continue to make the Masonic Home one of the finest
retirement communities in California.
Respectfully submitted,
Robert M. Fallon, Executive Director

REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR
MASONIC HOME, COVINA
2003 – 2004
On behalf of the residents and staff of the Masonic Homes at Covina,
we are pleased to submit the following annual report summarizing the many
challenges and successes we experienced this past year. As you read our report,
we hope you will sense our enthusiasm and commitment to excellence and wise
stewardship as we strive to provide the highest quality of care for our residents.
We also would like to express our genuine appreciation for the dedicated
leadership of our Board of Trustees and the faithful support of our Masonic
Lodges.
Over the past year we served an average senior population of 72
residents. During this same period, we had 18 admissions, 5 transfers to skilled
nursing, and 15 deaths. Also during this time our waiting list expanded to 46
prospective residents, representing 36 households. The average waiting period
for admission continues to be approximately one year. If prospective residents
need transitional services prior to their admission, we are very fortunate to
access the support of our Masonic Outreach Program.
We continue to offer a senior model of care that provides a full
continuum of services and care that meets the changing physical and emotional
needs of our residents. It also provides an opportunity for our residents to “age
in place” in a safe, caring and familiar environment. Because we serve a
relatively small senior population, we are not large enough to support our own
skilled nursing facility. As our residents health care needs increase and they
require skilled nursing services, we give them a first option to move to our
Union City campus. If they prefer to stay in our surrounding community, we
facilitate a move to a high quality non-profit skilled nursing facility that meets
our standards of excellence. Our staff always remains actively involved in the
oversight and care planning of each resident, whether they reside on campus or
not.
In May of 2004 we conducted our annual resident and family
satisfaction survey through an outside professional company that specializes in
senior research services. The survey focuses on two primary areas including:
general feelings about the community, and the quality of services we provide.
The overall results were extremely positive demonstrating improvement in all
areas of service as well as exceeding industry benchmarks in all areas. Our three
highest score were in food service, environmental services and personal care.
Our most improved score was in activities and recreation services. In order to
ensure quality improvement, each department developed and implemented a
series of service action plans. In addition, with the assistance of a professional
customer service training firm, we developed our own Masonic Homes customer

246

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

service excellence program. Through our program, we teach our staff a variety
of skills that reflect the Masonic values of Brotherly Love, Relief and Truth.
This past year we also completed several significant capital projects
that we know were very much appreciated by our residents. The largest project
was the renovation and redecoration of our senior community center building.
We redecorated the main lobby, lounges and dining room, developed a fitness
center, and relocated our beauty shop and arts and crafts spaces. We also added
a needed elevator and handicapped accessible doors at our entrances. It is a
beautiful updated building for which we are thankful and it has brought a breath
of fresh air and new opportunities to our residents.
As you may be aware, we are a very unique community that serves both
seniors and children. Our children’s program has an outstanding reputation for
providing high quality residential care to children ages 4 to 17 with or without
Masonic affiliation. Over the past year we served 26 boys and 26 girls, with an
average census of forty. During this period of time we had 12 admissions, 16
discharges, and 1 graduate. Approximately 50 % of our population has a
Masonic affiliation through a family member, most commonly a grandfather.
Our average length of stay is approximately 3.5 years.
As a follow up to the construction of our beautiful new children’s
community, completed in 2002, this past year we devoted significant time and
effort to strategically assessing and updating our model and philosophy of care.
Our goal is to assure that our programs meet the diverse needs of our Masonic
families and children, and represents the standards of excellence, best practices,
and outcomes consistent with our core Masonic values. While it continues to be
a work in progress, the Board of Trustees has appointed a special Childcare Task
Force, chaired by Trustee Mark Gibson to work with staff and consultants to
complete this major undertaking.
Additionally several new children’s initiatives were introduced this past
year. Our new children’s services’ administration, led by experienced director
Marissa Espinoza, initiated our own five-week summer camp program that
coincided with summer school. Our goal was to bring our children’s community
closer together and further enrich the lives of our children. The camp featured a
variety of on-campus games, sports, swimming, and arts and crafts. It also
included a number of afternoon outings to parks, museums, amusement parks,
and volunteer opportunities. The program was a great success and we look
forward to possibly expanding it next year to include the friends of our children
and the area Masonic youth groups.
We also introduced a monthly
intergenerational theme dinner that brought our children and seniors together for
a wonderful meal and entertainment. We took this opportunity to publicly
recognize the academic success of our children and award them with academic
incentives. This was also received very well.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

247

You may not be aware of this, but nearly every weekend of the year, our
campus hosts a visit by one of our Masonic Lodges. It has become a tradition
that our lodges will prepare a barbeque on- site and share it with the children and
seniors. It typically is a great social visit and a time for our lodges to learn more
about the mission and services of our Masonic Homes. We truly appreciate the
support of our lodges and the genuine interest they show in our residents.
In closing, you will see a list of all the seniors and children we served
this past year. We pledge to you our dedication and commitment to carry out
our mission of providing high quality care to California Master Masons, their
wives, widows and mothers as well as to children with or without Masonic
affiliation, with dignity and compassion, within an atmosphere, based on
Masonic tenets, that fulfill the physical, social, spiritual and nurturing needs of
our extended community.
Respectfully submitted
John E. Howl, Executive Director
