REPORT OF THE TRUSTEES OF THE
CALIFORNIA MASONIC FOUNDATION
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
It is with great pride that I am able to report to you the activities of the
Board of Trustees of the California Masonic Foundation. Those who were
present Sunday afternoon at the public opening of this Grand Lodge heard my
report regarding the expanded work of the Foundation. Permit me, now, to
explain how this expansion of duties came about.
In mid 2003, the audit firm of Price Waterhouse Coopers was engaged
to perform the annual audit of all Grand Lodge entities. As a part of their audit
report of CMMT, Nob Hill Masonic Management, and the Foundation, they
recommended a reorganization to avoid paying unnecessary taxes by CMMT and
the Foundation. Our previous auditors had failed to tell us that the value of the
California Masonic Memorial Temple land and building must be included when
determining the required distribution each year as a private foundation. Under
IRS regulations, the CMMT was required to distribute, to a charity, at least 5%
of its non-charitable use assets.
Simply distributing the required amount from the CMMT to the
Foundation, however, was not a good solution. This was not a one-year problem
– the minimum 5% distribution is an annual problem. The only way around this
was to petition the IRS to change the status of the CMMT from a 501.c.3
“private foundation” to a 501.c.3 “public charity” and bring it under the umbrella
of the California Masonic Foundation as a “supporting organization”. That was
done by the deadline date of June 30. This action was predicated on converting
the Foundation from a 501.c.3 private foundation to a public charity. The
California Masonic Foundation met the test of a public charity because of the
amount of money it distributes each year in scholarships and for the Masonic
Student Assistance Program.
In order to comply with the IRS rules regarding a “supporting
organization”, it was necessary to have four of the five CMMT Trustees also
serve as Trustees of the California Masonic Foundation. That could only be
accomplished if both boards resigned, giving the Grand Master the prerogative
of appointing new Trustees. The appointed Trustees of both boards did resign,
and the Grand Master subsequently appointed new Trustees. The California
Masonic Foundation has eight appointed Trustees (plus one ex officio Trustee,
the Deputy Grand Master). The California Masonic Memorial Temple has four
appointed Trustees (plus one ex officio Trustee, the Junior Grand Warden).
After the reorganization the four appointed Trustees on the California Masonic
Memorial Temple Board of Trustees also serve on the California Masonic
Foundation – satisfying the requirement for a “supporting organization”.

254

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

We also became aware through this process that the CMMT could
perform the functions of Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc., thereby making
NHMM redundant. The Grand Master, accordingly, decided that Nob Hill
Masonic Management, Inc., should be terminated as a “for profit” entity
effective October 31, 2004. In order to provide for an orderly transition from
Nob Hill to CMMT, the Grand Master replaced the four appointed Nob Hill
Directors with the four appointed CMMT Trustees effective July 1. The end
result of all this is that there is now a nine-member Board of Trustees for the
California Masonic Foundation, with four Foundation Trustees also comprising
the majority of the CMMT board and the Nob Hill Board. The Deputy Grand
Master continues to serve ex officio on the Foundation board and the Junior
Grand Warden continues to serve ex officio on the CMMT Board and the Nob
Hill Board.
The net effect of all of this is that more money from the non-Masonic
utilization of this building will flow to the Foundation. The Foundation will
work closely with CMMT (through the four trustees common to both entities) to
fund the preservation of this beautiful Memorial Temple; and will have funds to
underwrite some programs which have heretofore been funded through the
Grand Lodge budget, thereby reducing the upward pressure on per capita.
This highly desirable result is already being implemented. Hear again,
this brief review of Foundation programs this year:
1.

The awarding of 72 new and renewal of 117 scholarships for a
total of $1,034,500 to deserving college students.

2.

With the cooperation of the Masonic Homes of California, the
Foundation sponsored four 3-day MSAP Workshops and two 1day booster sessions, with a total attendance of 384 public school
teachers, administrators, and staff. This brings the 10-year total
to 4,000 teachers, administrators, and staff, and interventions in
the lives of nearly 40,000 students.

3.

Funding of the California Teacher of the Year program.

4.

Assisting the Leadership Training Program of each of our four
youth organizations with checks for $1,500 each.

5.

Continued funding of the Child I.D. Program.

To this list of worthy programs, utilizing the new source of revenue I
have described, as well as voluntary donations received from the Annual Giving
Campaign, the Foundation will be adding the following programs:
1.

Furthering the education of Masons in California through our
Museum and Library, the Masonic Symposium, literature and
publications, and leadership development in the members of our
constituent Lodges. Specifically in 2005-2006 the Foundation

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

255

will underwrite a portion of the cost of the Wardens Leadership
Retreats – a cost which otherwise would be passed on to those
attending the retreats.
2.

The Trustees, at their quarterly meeting next week, will consider
the possible reinstatement of grants to public schools sponsoring
Cornerstone Ceremonies.

3.

Working closely with the Trustees of the CMMT, we will be
making grants for the preservation of this building, and other
historic sites around the state.

Every organization whether it be a part of, or beyond the scope of, our
great fraternity, builds each year on the work of their predecessors. That is
certainly true in the case of the Foundation; and I would like to acknowledge the
great work of now former Trustees Donald A. Nielson, Wayne L. Butterbaugh,
Dan H. Wasson, Jerrold A. Wohlfarth, and ex officio trustee, David R. Doan,
Deputy Grand Master. Their work has generated positive results, and will make
future success possible.
Most Worshipful Grand Master, on behalf of all Trustees of the
California Masonic Foundation, I say thank you for the opportunity to serve our
great fraternity.
Respectfully submitted,
M. William Holsinger, PGM, President
John F. Lowe, Vice President
Thomas T. Albers
Frank Loui
David D. Johnson

Myron A. Tisdel
Thomas E. Kurth
Mark E. Pressey

REPORT OF THE BOARD OF TRUSTEES
OF THE CALIFORNIA MASONIC MEMORIAL TEMPLE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
The Trustees of the California Masonic Memorial Temple are pleased
th
to present the 49 annual report to the Grand Lodge on issues concerning both
the management of the physical facility as well as the financial condition of the
Temple.
In general our temple is in good repair and all equipment is functioning
well. The excellent facility management under the direction of Terry Hobson is
evident everywhere. When you see him please thank him.

256

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Replacement of the dimmer system in the theater was 90% completed
as of the fiscal year end. This enhancement will give us greater marketability of
our theater. Plans are underway to make improvements in the dressing rooms
and service areas.
Our five-year plan includes re-modeling of the Grand Lodge offices
(currently underway at a cost of $665,000), elevator equipment upgrade
($150,000), recover the seats in the Auditorium ($370,000), replace porch
membrane (to be determined). Still to be determined as to timing, restoration of
the Mosaic Mural in the lobby and replacement of the windscreen panel
($330,000). Future changes in the building are being considered, not the least of
which is actual replacement of the elevators and re-design of certain areas to
better utilize our floor space.
Work on the P5 Level is finished. A new ramp is in place along with
electronic equipment that will allow Pine Street ingress and egress at this level.
With the positive expectation that we will increase activity in our facility, this
improvement will serve us very well.
Looking forward it is the hope of the Board that a new approach to
marketing our facility will take shape and begin to generate revenue over and
above the established level over the recent past. We have operated in the black
for tax purposes, and to meet the demands of future capital expenditures and
increase in revenue seems to be the best approach. To that end and with the help
of our selection committee successfully hired Mr. Michael Grice whose first day
on the job is July 15, 2004. He brings to us a career spanning 22 years in
entertainment facility management, fourteen years of which were at the
Spreckles Center. His analysis of the past year’s revenue showed that we are
under-using our theater for evening programs. If we continue with the daytime
activities and increase evening programs our revenue stream will begin to
provide a basis to plan and commit to the needed work now being considered.
You are invited to review the full and complete audited financials
included in the appendices of these proceedings. We are indebted to Mark
Koenig and his staff for their excellent handling of the accounting matters and
keeping the board informed regularly on our financial condition. Even your
president can understand his answers to questions about the balance sheet.
Thank you Mark.
A major re-organization of our support bodies has taken place this year.
A detailed explanation written by our Grand Secretary, John L. Cooper and
reproduced with his permission is attached. The bottom line to all this will be
that the Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc. will cease doing business as of
October 31, 2004. All of its functions, duties, and financial affairs are being
transferred to the CMMT and will be administered by that body.
As a part of the re-organization of the CMMT, CMF, and Nob Hill
Masonic Management, Inc. the Grand Master appointed new trustees to the

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

257

boards. Four of the CMMT Trustees will also serve as Trustees of the CMF and
temporarily as Directors of Nob Hill. Our thanks go out to last year’s Board
including Frederick L. Sorsabal, Junior Grand Warden, Acting President; Robert
Winston, Vice President, Ian Atkinson, Ron L. Broaddus, Ronald E. Cooper,
Thomas Kurth, Scott T. Porter, R. Stephen Doan, PGM, Ronald A. Sherod,
PGM, and Henry W. Coil, Advisor.
Our Grand Treasurer, Warren Blomseth, PGM certainly deserves
thanks for his expertise in both financial and executive guidance. The Board
also wishes to recognize with a vote of thanks to our Grand Secretary, John L.
Cooper III, who seems to get about twenty-six hours out of every day and
providing us with excellent management guidance.
On a personal note I wish to thank our Grand Master Howard D.
Kirkpatrick for the opportunity to serve on this board. His selection of board
members with their diverse background is outstanding and the brethren can
expect fine performance from them as a group.
Respectfully submitted,
David D. Johnson, President
Thomas Kurth, Vice President
John F. Lowe
Myron A. Tisdel

Melvyn B. Stein, JGW
Henry W. Coil, Consultant

REPORT OF THE BOARD OF DIRECTORS OF
NOB HILL MASONIC MANAGEMENT, INC.
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
As president of the Board of Directors of Nob Hill Masonic
Management, Inc. it is my privilege and pleasure to present on behalf of the
Board of Directors the twelfth annual report to the Grand Lodge on its
operations of the California Masonic Memorial Center. It has been the mission
for all of these years to manage the operations of this facility at a profit for and
on behalf of the California Masonic Memorial Temple (CMMT), the owner.
This report covers the fiscal year July 1, 2003 through June 30, 2004
and will include limited financial information for reasons that will become clear
as the report unfolds. Complete and Audited financial statements are included in
the Appendices of these Proceedings. The reader is encouraged to review these
reports.
year.

This year revenue increased more than $200,000 from the previous
Our interim facility manager Terry Hobson was instrumental in this

258

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

success, and the board wishes to thank him for his fine work. Operational
expenses also increased, resulting in a net negative operating income. For tax
purposes this is not bad news, as some of our costs (rent, for example) are paid
indirectly to ourselves.
Our security system continues to work well and will be deactivated
during the Annual Communication. AAMPCO continues to provide excellent
garage management under contract renewed last year. The vehicle ramp at the
Pine Street exit has been completed at budget.
Ongoing projects include continuing maintenance of the Terrazzo floor
in the Entry and Olive Porches, phase III of the HVAC system, and periodic
repair of the “Endo-Mosaic” mural in the entry lobby. It is expected that
substantial additional costs will be incurred to secure this magnificent work of
art for another 50 (almost) years. By year end our new dimmer system in the
theater lighting was 90% completed. The cost was just under $300,000. This
investment will be extremely important as we move into the next era of
promoting our facility.
Your Directors in recent years have been faced with a unique challenge
in keeping the bottom line in the black, and we all owe them a great thank you
for their success. Faced with increasing costs of maintenance and equipment
replacement it was necessary to reduce costs and cut expenses where possible. A
negative move in the investment market added to their woes by reducing
available capital and its attendant earning power. We must change that
direction, and to that end we will begin a program of marketing our facility with
a goal of increasing revenue.
The board approved a small expenditure to secure the services of an
entertainment facility consultant whose recommendations included a plan that
would aid in finding a new executive director to replace Chris Carpenter, who
resigned last year. Our team found an outstanding man in Mr. Michael Grice,
who comes to us with 22 years of experience, fourteen of those years with the
Spreckles Center. Mr. Grice is a seasoned professional with both experience and
enthusiasm for this position. His first day on the job is July 15, 2004, and we all
wish him great success. His success is our success and looking forward to
capital expenditures for new elevators, remodeling of the Grand Lodge offices,
etc.it will be vital in keeping our bottom line in the black.
Major re-organizational moves were required this year for the three bodies
serving the Grand Lodge. The California Masonic Foundation, California
Masonic Memorial Temple, and the Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc. have,
in the past, interacted to provide financial management as well as facility
management for our various properties. Out of all this it has been determined
that the Nob Hill, Inc. will cease doing business as of Oct 31, 2004. Its
responsibilities will be assumed by the CMMT. This move will mean economic
benefits to our craft and we are all very excited about our bright future. As a
part of this re-organization Grand Master Howard D. Kirkpatrick appointed new

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

259

trustees to the CMMT and CMF, four of which are serving temporarily as
directors of Nob Hill, Inc. A complete explanation of this transition is fully
covered by the attachment prepared by our Grand Secretary, John L. Cooper.
Please read it. We all wish to extend a sincere thank you to Dan R. McDaniel,
Past President, John Schneidmiller, James D. Stephens, David D. Johnson, and
Melvyn B. Stein, JGW for their excellent service on the Board last year.
Finally, and very importantly the directors of your company thank
Grand Master Howard D. Kirkpatrick for the opportunity to serve the Grand
Lodge. We also wish to acknowledge the Junior Grand Warden, Melvyn B.
Stein, the Grand Treasurer, Warren J. Blomseth, PGM and the Grand Secretary,
John L. Cooper, III for their invaluable guidance and counsel.

REPORT OF THE CALIFORNIA FREEMASON
MAGAZINE EDITOR-IN-CHIEF
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
During this Masonic year, the California Freemason celebrated 50
years of publication. Founding editor, Newcome Condee, published the Winter
1954 issue and three subsequent issues as an experiment. At the Annual
Communication of 1955, the Grand Lodge established the California Freemason
as the official publication of the Grand Lodge.
Today, the content of each quarterly issue is guided by a theme. The
theme is communicated through several articles that are related to one of seven
sections of the magazine, which include: In California, Lodge Spotlight,
Masonic Education, Cover Story, Around the World, History, and Masonic
Homes.
In the 2003/2004 Grand Lodge Year, four issues were published for a
total distribution of 335,994. Information about each issue is below.
Issue: Winter 2003
Theme: Relief
Quantity for Distribution: 84,160

260

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Issue: Spring 2004
Theme: Freemasonry and Architecture
Quantity for Distribution: 83,784
Issue: Summer 2004
Theme: The Initiatic Process
Quantity for Distribution: 83,000
Issue: Fall 2004
Theme: Freemasonry and the American Frontier
Quantity for Distribution: 84,000
Many people contribute to the successful publication of the California
Freemason. I would like to extend my appreciation to the members of our
regular writing team including Richard Berman, Terry Mendez, Dennis
Chornenky, Adam Kendall, Steffani Kizziar, and Ann Lyon Dudley. We are
also pleased to have a regular editorial from one of the members of the
Executive Committee and each month we look forward to an article of Masonic
Education from Grand Secretary John L. Cooper III. I would like to recognize
Burritt Design for the outstanding graphic design of the magazine. I also offer
praise and extend my gratitude to Kimberly Murawski who serves as the Copy
Editor of the magazine and also coordinates the publishing, printing, and mailing
components of publication.
Finally, I thank Most Worshipful Howard D. Kirkpatrick, Grand
Master, for the opportunity to lead and manage this important publication. It is,
indeed, a privilege and an honor.
Respectfully submitted,
Allan L. Casalou, Editor in Chief

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON FINANCE

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
Most Worshipful Grand Master, brethren, I am Miguel Cheng, PM, San
Francisco Lodge No. 120, Chairman of the Grand Lodge Finance Committee and
Retirement Committee. It is my honor to present the Finance Report.
For our fiscal year 2003/2004 under Grand Master Kirkpatrick, the
projected revenues and expenses, as previously approved by this Grand Lodge
for the year just concluding, were met through a carefully and well managed
“balanced budget approach”.

264

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

The total revenue was $2 million, 83% of which were per capita and
fees from the members. Nob Hill, which is this beautiful edifice, contributed
$45,000, or 2%, and has done so annually since 1993.
Fifteen percent of the revenues came from endowment fund income
which was created in 1992 from the surpluses through the years, i.e., the monies
our Past Grand Masters saved.
The endowment fund is currently at
approximately $4 million. The final year-end result brings a small surplus which
will be applied to the Endowment Fund. The income will help pay for future
expenses, thus holding down the future per capita.
The highest single expense continues to be insurance coverage for all
lodges and Masonic centers and workers’ compensation coverage, which was
$294,660 in the current year, projected to be $353,592 next year, a 20%
increase, or close to $5 per member in cost. However, without any doubt, this is
also one of the many Grand Lodge services of great importance to all Masons
and lodges, if purchased individually, the collective premium would be far
higher.
Your Grand Lodge’s finances were audited by Price Waterhouse
Cooper, completed in July 2004, the details are available to everyone in the
published copy of the Grand Lodge Proceedings.
The following legislative issues have no financial impact to the Grand
Lodge: Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s Decision No. 1; Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s
Recommendations No. 1, No. 3 and No. 4; Carry-over Resolution No. 03-02;
and Resolutions No. 04-01, 04-02, 04-03, 04-04, 04-06 and 04-11.
Adoption of Budget and Per Capita
The Finance Committee has met with the Grand Lodge Executive
officers, specifically the Senior Grand Warden, Fred Sorsabal, and reviewed the
proposed 2005-2006 budget of $2,014,211. We discussed all the line items in
detail and found it to be fiscally responsible and consistent with general
guidelines of the Grand Lodge.
The Finance Committee has met with the Grand Lodge Executive
officers, and reviewed the proposed 2005-2006 per capita of $26.00, a $1.00 or
4% increase, which is in line with general expectation of inflation guidelines.
Respectfully submitted
Miguel A. Cheng, Chairman
Gary C. Young

H. Donald Young

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

265

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON INVESTMENT
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A. M. of California:
The Investment Committee is charged with the prudent handling of
Grand Lodge investment funds, which consist primarily of the General and
Endowment funds for the Masonic Homes of California, but also include the
investment portfolios of the California Masonic Foundation, California Masonic
Memorial Temple, Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc., Grand Lodge
Retirement plan and the Grand Lodge itself. We take this charge very seriously.
In its simplest terms, we manage the investment funds with an eye both for
growth and preservation of capital. We strive to maintain a diversified portfolio
that is designed to generate the necessary funds for the continuing operation of
the Masonic functions as well as allow for growth for future needs. The Grand
Lodge Investment Committee utilizes the services of professional investment
management firms to gain access to the capital markets. Funds are primarily
invested in U.S. and international equity and fixed income markets.
The capital markets in which the Grand Lodge invests had mixed
performance over the past year. While the U.S. and international stock markets
rallied significantly, the bond markets were essentially flat. Corporate profits
and productivity were at peak levels as the U.S. consumer continued to spend
and companies capitalized on their lean workforces and low borrowing costs.
This significantly helped the equity markets. The Bush administration’s tax
relief and the Fed’s commitment to keeping interest rates low until the
employment picture improved or inflation appeared provided further justification
for higher stock prices. However, the expectation that interest rates would
remain at their 40-year low levels vanished in the Spring of this year, as better
than expected jobs reports and signs of increases in inflation sparked conjecture
that the Fed would be forced to raise rates. This had a negative impact on bonds,
as their prices move in opposite direction of interest rates.
The U.S. equity markets returned 20.5% (as measured by the Russell
3000 index) over the twelve-month period ending June 30, 2004. Performance
in the second half of 2003 was particularly strong. Stocks were up 3.4% and
rd
th
12.4%, respectively, in the 3 and 4 quarters of last year. While returns in the
first half of 2004 were also positive, performance was far more subdued. The
st
U.S. stock market returned 2.2% in the 1 quarter of this year and 1.3% in the
nd
2 quarter. The Grand Lodge’s investment exposure to foreign markets also
proved beneficial over the past year. International stock markets reported above
average returns, up 23.2% before translation into U.S. dollars. The dollar
declined significantly versus other currencies, resulting in a net international
equity return of over 30%. Bonds returned a very modest 0.3% over the year
th
nd
ended June 30 , declining almost 2.5% in the 2 quarter as interest rates rose.

266

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

The Fed appears to be adhering to what it had said would be a slow,
measured increase in interest rates. However, economists remain fearful that the
higher cost of servicing debts will have a negative impact on the indebted
consumers’ ability to spend. This combined with rising energy costs, the threat
of terrorist attacks and the mounting deficit and current account imbalance
results in a bearish outlook for many market watchers. Optimists counter that
interest rates remain very low on a relative basis and as corporate profits
continue to rise, increased business spending will make up for any decline in
consumer spending. Although the markets may remain volatile as events play
out, the Investment Committee will continue to oversee the management of the
portfolios under its charge in the same consistent and prudent manner that has
been utilized in the past.
The Investment Committee does not attempt to take advantages of
perceived short-term anomalies in the markets by chasing the most recent top
performing asset class. Rather, it arrives at its recommended allocation to stocks
and bonds and other asset classes only after very careful due diligence and
consideration. The purpose of the funds, their spending requirements, investment
time horizon and ability to assume risk are all factored into the
recommendations. The possibility and duration of potential losses are carefully
weighed against the long-term potential for appreciation of assets after
withdrawals are taken into account. The Investment Committee recommends
asset allocations that will provide the highest probability that the funds will be
able to meet their respective objectives over the long-term.
In order to fulfill our responsibilities in accordance with the Grand
Lodge Investment Policy and Philosophy, the Investment Committee meets
quarterly with our Investment Consultant, Ennis/Knupp and our individual
portfolio managers. We review their respective performance compared to
various indices representing their objective. This process provides the
Investment Committee with the necessary data to adequately evaluate each
investment manager on a timely basis, and to observe whether they continue to
achieve the objectives of their mandate.
The market value of the Masonic Homes General and Endowment
portfolios on June 30, 2004 was $633,222,171 compared to $552,326,827 a year
earlier. This, combined with the other investment portfolios on which the
Investment Committee advises, resulted in combined assets totaling slightly less
than $700,000,000.
As of June 30, 2004, the combined funds of Grand Lodge and the
related Masonic entities were up 17.6% for the twelve-month period. This
compares to 18.3% for our benchmark custom index. While consistent trailing
performance relative to the benchmark would be of concern to us, the Investment
Committee is not troubled over the results of the past year. This relative
underperformance was due primarily to the fact that many speculative,
unprofitable companies that were severely discounted from 2000 through 2002

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

267

rallied significantly last year. Equity market returns were dominated by stocks
of these types of companies. For example, in 2003 companies with the lowest
quality ratings by Standard & Poors outperformed those with the highest
rankings by over 50%.
By design, the investment mangers utilized by the Masonic Grand
Lodge and related entities focus on stocks of higher quality, profitable firms.
While this strategy was out of favor recently, longer-term results continue to be
well ahead of the benchmark. For the cumulative three-year period ending June
th
30 , the combined Grand Lodge and related entities funds were up 3.8% versus
2.6% for the benchmark. Over the past five-year period, the funds were up 4.0%
versus the 1.3% return of the benchmark.
These favorable long-term results were achievable in part because we
held a relatively large portion of the portfolio in fixed income assets and we
over-weighted “value” and smaller company stocks whose performance has
rallied significantly of late. This strong relative performance can also be
attributed to the above-benchmark returns of our investment managers. In fact,
the returns of most of our funds have consistently surpassed the performance of
the benchmark since inception, while at the same time incurring an overall lower
level of risk. Please refer to the attachments that follow for a complete
performance summary from each manager for periods covering since inception
and by calendar quarters and years.
While long-term performance for most managers has met expectations,
the Committee lost confidence in one manager and found it necessary to end this
relationship. Due to continued underperformance and significant personnel
changes, Sirach Capital Management, which managed the large and small
nd
capitalization growth portfolios, was terminated during the 2 quarter and
substituted with State Street Global Advisors. State Street will serve as an
interim passive manager until such time the Investment Committee is able to
select appropriate active managers as replacements. Separate searches are
underway for large and small capitalization growth managers. We expect to
have the new managers in place before the end of the year.
In addition to making recommendations for the Grand Lodge and its
related entities’ investment portfolios, the Committee also works closely with the
Director of Development, Doug Ismail, to ensure that donors to the various
Grand Lodge charities are provided the most competitive investment options.
The Gift Annuity program has been greatly enhanced, offering potential donors
with much higher income streams than were previously available. This
improvement has translated into a greater number of gifts. The program has
brought in $595,000 in new gifts over the past year.
As reported last year, the Committee had been evaluating potential
options for the Lodge Investment Program.
With the help of David
Schwarzenberger, our Manager of Investment Reporting and Analysis, a new

268

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

program was launched in December that offers Lodges well-needed support with
the management of their assets. The “Investment Options for California
Masonic Lodges” program is intended to assist Lodges in determining an
appropriate investment strategy and asset allocation. It will also help Lodges
establish a set of policies and procedures that are designed to ensure assets are
invested according to the needs of the Lodge and to safeguard against the misuse
of funds. Investment Committee members continue to make themselves
available to the Lodges for assistance with this program and other questions
regarding lodge investments.
I am appreciative for the dedication of Most Worshipful Warren
Blomseth in his role as Grand Treasurer and the support he provides us. Our
Manager of Investments, David Schwarzenberger, continues to perform his role
admirably. His presence has added tremendously to the efficiency of our
operations and success in our investment. And my most sincere appreciation is
also extended to the Grand Lodge Investment Committeemen for the
commitment and devotion they have for the Masonic causes. They have always
been forthright with their opinions and expertise. I am convinced we have the
finest group of investment professionals fulfilling our very important mission.
In summary, while the financial markets continue to be volatile, we are
pleased with the results we continue to achieve. Rest assured we remain ever
diligent in our attempts to achieve adequate returns to support the Homes and
other Masonic charities while at the same time preserving our asset base for
future ages.
Respectfully submitted,
Russell E. Charvonia, Chairman

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON JURISPRUDENCE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F. & A.M. of California:
The Committee on Jurisprudence serves as the legal adviser to the
Grand Master and the legal adviser to the Grand Lodge when in session. The
Committee reviews proposed resolutions and recommendations to be considered
at the next Annual Communication and prepares any necessary legislation to
carry out the intent of the proponents. At the Annual Communication, it
comments on the legality of the Grand Master’s decisions and dispensations and
on the legal effect of the proposed recommendations and resolutions. After the
Annual Communication, it prepares the changes to the California Masonic Code
to reflect the legislation which was adopted.
In the Committee’s view, those of Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s decisions
and dispensations coming to its attention were legal and within his discretion. In
the Committee’s view, the legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on August
2nd of this year is sufficient to carry out the intent of the proponents, and the
legislation contained in Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s recommendations which he
will present in his message is sufficient to carry out his intent. We believe that
each piece of this legislation has the legal effect set forth in it.
The legislation mailed by the Grand Secretary on August 2nd of this
year and the legislation contained in Grand Master Kirkpatrick’s
recommendations require no separate motion because they are automatically
before this Annual Communication. The presiding officer will announce the
vote required for the passage of each item before discussion begins. Any
legislation which does not amend the California Masonic Code or the Ritual
requires a simple majority only for adoption. Any amendment to the California
Masonic Code or the Ritual must be adopted by a five-sixths affirmative vote. If
the amendment receives a simple majority but less than a five-sixths affirmative
vote, it carries over to the next Annual Communication when only a two-thirds
affirmative vote is required for adoption.
We thank the Grand Master for the opportunity which he has given us
to serve this year.
Respectfully submitted,
R. Stephen Doan, P.G.M., Chairman
Richard S. Andrews
Lester W. Miller

Jack R. Levitt, PGM
Richard L. Riemer

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE
ON MEMBERSHIP DEVELOPMENT

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
My name is Phillip L. McAllister and I am a Past Master of AcalanesFellowship Lodge No. 480, and Chairman of the Membership Development
Committee.
It is my pleasure this afternoon to present to you the Annual Report of
our Membership Development program.
I would first like to take this opportunity to publicly acknowledge and
thank the committeemen and Grand Lodge staff members who continuously
contribute their time and efforts to making our “Pass It On” new Membership
Development program a sensational success. The committee members are Wor.
Gary L. Peterson, Past Master of Saddleback Laguna Lodge No. 672; Wor.
James E. Banta, Past Master of Fresno Lodge No. 247; Wor. Roberto G. Perez,
Past Master of Mariposa Lodge No. 24; Wor. Salvatore A. Sciortino, Past
Master of Wisdom Lodge No. 202; Wor. Felipe G. Leon, Past Master of San
Jacinto Lodge No. 338; and Wor. Edward Avertisian, Past Master of Burbank
Lodge No. 406. The Grand Lodge staff members are Wor. Allan L. Casalou,
Assistant Grand Secretary and a Past Master of Acalanes-Fellowship Lodge No.
480; Brother Angel Alvarez-Mapp, Administrative Assistant and a member of
Acalanes-Fellowship Lodge No. 480; and Wor. Erik Peterson, Worshipful
Master of Acalanes-Fellowship Lodge.
As reported to you at our Grand Lodge Communication in year 2003,
Past Grand Master, Most Worshipful M. William Holsinger, charged our
committee with the monumental task of developing a “Comprehensive
Membership Plan” that would address our most important membership issues,
which were to increase our membership levels and to attract new leaders to our
fraternity. This plan would include a uniform guide to be used by our lodge

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

275

leaders and members, making it simpler to communicate our message to
potential candidates.
Brethren, I am sure you will agree with me that the development of
quality membership is without reservation, the most important part of our work
in the Masonic era we live in.
Just to reiterate our intentions, our committee agreed that the program
would have to be supported by two external factors – the need for leadership and
a clear understanding of our purpose for the fraternity. These criteria have been
developed and are in full use within our lodges by the utilization of the “Pass It
On” program that was issued to all lodges in years 2003 and 2004.
Our committee’s first year (2003) was spent developing, packaging and
getting the “Pass It On” kits into the hands of each lodge in the state of
California. We had high hopes that you would all take advantage of the elements
in the kit, which includes a comprehensive guide on how to use the program and
several new tools to be used for membership development and retention. The
new tools include an updated brochure, a wallet brochure, a new packet to send
to applicants, and an additional packet to be sent to candidates who have been
elected to receive the degrees. Also included are candidate satisfaction surveys
and template letters to be sent to interested men and their wives. These surveys
and letters are conveniently provided on a compact disc (CD), which is also
included in the kit.
We believe that if the items in the kit are committed to and properly
used, not only will we attract a brotherhood of men who believe in a Supreme
Being, we will begin to illustrate that we are an organization that men could join
and feel comfortable with the knowledge that they can learn and that they can
lead; where they can enjoy outstanding fellowship, dignity, pride and personal
connections; and where they can participate with their families and friends in a
very positive environment.
Many of our brethren would have you believe that we are a dying
organization and these brothers are prepared to provide you with every reason as
to why their lodge can not attract and retain quality new men. However, I am
here to report that many lodges in the state of California are experiencing an
unprecedented resurgence in interest in membership and have absolutely more
degree work than they can possibly confer.
The “Pass It On” program has indeed been a very successful and viable
program for the lodges who have used it. One such lodge is Coronado Lodge
No. 441 located in the San Diego area. I quote Worshipful Frank G. Estep, Jr.,
Past Master of that lodge:
“Coronado Lodge has conferred 43 degrees this
year, with 17 more scheduled. The ‘Pass It On’ program has
been responsible for much of this activity.”

276

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Brothers of Coronado Lodge, I would like to personally salute you for
your excellence in the development of membership and also for your efforts in
securing the future of Masonry in your area.
The “Pass It On” program has identified a standard for California
Lodges in order that we have a goal to quantify our efforts. A “performing”
lodge would be one that raises five or more Master Masons. Another way to
measure a successful lodge is by total degrees conferred for any given fiscal
year.
Brothers I am very pleased to announce that during our previous Grand
Lodge fiscal year, 85 lodges conferred five or more Third Degrees. We have
139 lodges that conferred 10 or more degrees.
Last year we raised 1,047 Master Masons and we as a fraternity are on
the way up because for the third year in a row we have raised more Master
Masons than the previous year.
To aid in the implementation of the “Pass It On” program, this year our
committee members began presenting the program at the Senior and Junior
Warden Retreats with tremendous success. We also presented to and worked
with the lodge officers during the Lodge Management Workshops.
We feel that the “Pass It On” program will be the key membership
program for the Grand Lodge for many years. Therefore, it will give each ldoge
the time to integrate the process into their daily activities. Most importantly, it
will give the lodge officers clear goals, a plan and the necessary tools to organize
all of the current membership activities in a predictable format and structure.
The key issue related to the success or failure of the program is the
level of membership involvement and the quality of the implementation of each
of the program steps. Lodge effectiveness is directly related to the internal and
external organizational strategies of the lodge; the level of communications; and
ultimately exposure to the surrounding community. All the steps are geared to
preparing a lodge for new members and the retention of its existing members.
Our committee is currently in the process of completing a
comprehensive evaluation system.
As we move towards a large-scale
incorporation of the program, we must continuously evaluate its success and
understand the effectiveness of its overall viability and each of its components.
The feedback received from each lodge will be compiled and analyzed. The
analyzed data will be correlated with the membership data for each lodge,
district and division.
The evaluation system will help in two key areas. First, the committee
will be looking for a relationship between the program performances and the
increase in membership. Second, the system will be used to evaluate the success
of the program and its building blocks.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

277

We all take pride in our Masonic heritage. We need the new
membership to insure the continuity of that heritage. The “Pass It On”
Membership Development Program offers an opportunity for us to focus our
efforts on recruiting new members in addition to taking care of our existing ones.
We’re sure you’ll all agree that this is a very important issue that affects all
California Masons. Without everyone’s involvement, we cannot succeed.
On behalf of the Membership Development Committee for the Grand
Lodge of the State of California, I thank you and the Most Worshipful Howard
Kirkpatrick for giving me this opportunity to update you on the progress of the
“Pass It On” Membership Development program, a program that we feel will
truly benefit our great fraternity for many years to come.
Thank you.
Respectfully submitted,
Phillip L. McAllister, Sr., Chairman
Edward Avetisian
Felipe G. Leon
Gary L. Peterson

James E. Banta
Roberto G. Perez
Salvatore A. Sciortino
REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE ON
RESEARCH AND PLANNING

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge F. & A.M. of California:
The Committee on Research and Planning, being a special committee of
Grand Lodge to conduct research, evaluate alternatives and make
recommendations on issues impacting Freemasonry in California, met on several
occasions and considered a number of issues.
Your committee evaluated the Application for Degrees form and the
Reference to Investigative Committee form with the intent to make them more
applicable to students and younger applicants, and to eliminate unnecessary and
inapplicable questions. These forms were revised accordingly and submitted to
the Grand Master for consideration.
Your committee was requested to study and make recommendations on
the appropriate amount of fees and dues that should be charged. An extensive
amount of research was undertaken, which included:
1.

a history of those charged in this Jurisdiction;

2.

prior research and code change proposals;

3.

comparisons with fees and dues in other Grand Jurisdictions;

4.

comparisons with inflation figures;

278

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

5.

the need for a minimum as compared with a standard charge;

6.

having a floating minimum; and

7.

relationship of dues to the per capita.

The input by Most Worshipful Stanley Channon, as consultant, was
extremely helpful and his materials were of considerable assistance.
Although your committee was not asked to make a recommendation
resulting in proposed legislation, the results of its study were delivered to the
Grand Master.
The concept of a Lodge to be chartered to facilitate members of Lodges
whose charters have been revoked was studied. The purpose being to retain
those members who may have difficulty in affiliating. Many considerations were
evaluated, including dues, name of Lodge, funerals and charity obligations,
voting, officers, disposition of property etc. The chartering of such a Lodge is
being submitted as a Grand Master's recommendation for your consideration.
The issue of a division of ritual and administration that had been
considered by previous Committees on Research and Planning, was addressed by
your committee. Much of the prior information on the issue was reviewed and
considerable time was devoted to the impact on the Jurisdiction of various
proposals. The Grand Lecturer position, dividing the Inspectors into those for
ritual and those for administration, the overseeing of each function, greater use
of Lodge coaches, certifications and related matters were all studied. Your
committee has submitted to the Grand Master a comprehensive proposal for the
reorganization of ritual and administration, which includes the required code
changes to implement it.
Your committee was requested to study the use of multi-media
presentations of the lectures. CDR, videos, charts and slides of other Grand
Jurisdictions and the prior use of charts and slides in our Jurisdiction were
studied. It was concluded that there should always be a lecturer with the option
to supplement with several types of visual aids, all of which should be standard
and not include any of the secret work. Since no code changes were considered
to be necessary, the results of the study were delivered to the Grand Master.
Your committee also studied the use of costumes in the second section
of the third degree. Consideration was given to the need for costumes as well as
the benefits to be derived from their use. Uniformity was considered due to
determinations made at the Annual Communications in 1954 and 1955.
Your committee considered that there are certain pieces of clothing now
allowed that could be considered to be costumes. It also considered that there is
no present uniformity with regard to clothing.

GRAND LODGE F.& A.M. OF CALIFORNIA

279

Your committee agreed that it would make no recommendation other
than that a pilot program would be preferable to a code change specifically
authorizing costumes.
Other items that arose during discussions by the committee, that were addressed
by the members with other appropriate committees, included retention of
members, Masonic education and the Mentor program.
No amount of praise could be too much in describing the hard work and
dedication of the members of your committee. To them the chairman is forever
indebted.
Respectfully submitted,
Jack R. Levitt, PGM, Chairman
Richard Andrews
Norman Leeper

Sherman Ostrom
Mark Pressey
Melvyn Stein, JGW
REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE ON
TRIAL REVIEW

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
Since the 2003 Annual Communication there have been referred to your
Committee on Trial Review five completed (5) and four (4) applications for
restorations from expulsion.
Your committee has reviewed each of these Trial records numbered
2460, 2462, 2469 and find that in each there was compliance with the procedural
requirements with our constitutional and ordinances and the resulted is supported
in the record of each case and there are no miscarriage of justice.
Your committee has reviewed the Trial record for 2466 and your
committee finds that errors were made which resulted in a possible miscarriage
of Justice, therefore, we move that this trial be returned to the Lodge for retrial.
Your committee has reviewed the Trial record for 2470 and recommends a new
Trial Master be appointed for further investigation.
Requests for Restoration for Trial records 1994, 2366, and 2409 have
been reviewed by your committee and the penalties imposed will stand at
expulsion.
Request for Restoration for Trial record 2139 expelled in 1979 has
been reviewed and the Petition for Restoration has been approved and granted.

280

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

Trial Record No. 2466
You committee on Trial Review recommends that this case which was for failure
to repay a loan, be sent back for the following reasons:
Multiple errors were made, which resulted in a possible miscarriage of
Justice.
Trial Record No. 2470
Your committee on Trial Review recommends that a Trial Master be
appointed to investigate and report his findings.
Charges were properly stated and served on the accused to which he
responded in a timely manner with a plea of guilty on February 16,
2004. On April 13, 2004, the accuser sent a letter to the Grand
Secretary “withdrawing.” his charges. CMC Section 32070 states that a
lodge itself cannot prefer, dismiss, or otherwise control charges.
Respectfully submitted
Thomas C. Letcher, Chairman
Donald B. Constine
David A. Goble
Kirk M. Livermont

Richard A. Meyers

NAVIGATION TIPS

Scroll through the document using
scroll bars on the side of the window or
the page navigation tool at the bottom.

Click on any of the items in the bookmark
section of the document and you’ll navigate
directly to that page or section.
You can then move to the next page using the
ARROW KEYS on your keyboard.
Return to the beginning of the document at any
time by clicking on the Table of Contents
Bookmark.
If you want to enlarge or reduce the size of the
document, click on the Zoom In/Zoom Out
bookmarks.
Navigate through the document
by clicking on any of the items in
the Table of Contents or the
Index.
The “Hand Tool” will change to
an “Index Finger”…click on the
line and you’ll move to the
desired page.
To get back, click on the Table
of Contents/Index bookmarks.
